A) kubelet certificates are automatically rotated when the RotateKubeletServerCertificate feature gate is enabled and --rotate-server-certificates is set. Client certificates are auto-rotated via the RotateKubeletClientCertificate feature. Manual rotation uses kubeadm certs renew followed by restarting the kubelet
B) kubelet certificates never expire — they are valid for the lifetime of the node
C) kubelet certificates must be manually regenerated every 30 days by SSH-ing to each node
D) kubelet uses the API server's certificates — no separate kubelet TLS is needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Kubelet certificates are separate from API server certificates. Each kubelet needs a client certificate (to authenticate TO the API server) and a serving certificate (for its own HTTPS endpoint on port 10250). Kubernetes supports automatic rotation for both.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubelet Certificate Lifecycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUBELET CERTIFICATE TYPES:                                       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ① CLIENT CERTIFICATE (kubelet → API server)                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Location: /var/lib/kubelet/pki/kubelet-client-current.pem    │ │
  │  │ Rotation: AUTO (RotateKubeletClientCertificate=true)         │ │
  │  │ Process: kubelet creates CSR → API server approves → new cert│ │
  │  │ Expiry: Typically 30-90 days                                 │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ② SERVING CERTIFICATE (kubelet HTTPS endpoint :10250)            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Location: /var/lib/kubelet/pki/kubelet-server-current.pem    │ │
  │  │ Manual: --rotate-server-certificates flag                    │ │
  │  │ Or: kubeadm certs renew kubelet-server                       │ │
  │  │ Then: systemctl restart kubelet                              │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CHECKING KUBELET CERT STATUS:                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ # Check client cert expiry                                    │ │
  │  │ openssl x509 -in /var/lib/kubelet/pki/kubelet-client-        │ │
  │  │   current.pem -noout -dates                                   │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ # Check ALL kubeadm managed certs                             │ │
  │  │ kubeadm certs check-expiration                                │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ # Renew all certificates                                      │ │
  │  │ kubeadm certs renew all                                       │ │
  │  │ systemctl restart kubelet                                     │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS importance: Expired kubelet certificates cause nodes to show NotReady — a cluster-wide outage. The CKS exam tests certificate lifecycle management extensively.

Cluster Setup Q22 of 200
etcd — the cluster's database — requires TLS for both client and peer communication. What certificates does etcd need, and what flags enable them?
A) etcd uses a single shared certificate for all communication — simplicity over security
B) etcd only uses TLS when deployed on cloud providers — self-managed etcd communicates in plaintext
C) etcd TLS is optional — it's recommended but not required for CKS compliance
D) etcd requires SEPARATE certificates for: (1) Client-server communication (--cert-file, --key-file, --client-cert-auth=true), and (2) Peer-to-peer communication between etcd members (--peer-cert-file, --peer-key-file, --peer-client-cert-auth=true). The CA is specified via --trusted-ca-file and --peer-trusted-ca-file
✅ Correct Answer: D

etcd uses two separate TLS contexts: client (API server → etcd) and peer (etcd → etcd, for RAFT consensus). Each requires its own certificate pair with *-cert-auth=true to enforce mutual TLS.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd TLS — Dual Certificate Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  etcd TLS ARCHITECTURE:                                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                    etcd CA (ca.crt)                         │  │
  │  │                         │                                   │  │
  │  │         ┌───────────────┴───────────────┐                   │  │
  │  │         │                               │                   │  │
  │  │  CLIENT CERTS                    PEER CERTS                 │  │
  │  │  (API→etcd)                      (etcd↔etcd)                │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────┐       │  │
  │  │  │ server.crt       │           │ peer.crt         │       │  │
  │  │  │ server.key       │           │ peer.key         │       │  │
  │  │  │ Used by: etcd    │           │ Used by: etcd    │       │  │
  │  │  │ server to accept │           │ members to talk  │       │  │
  │  │  │ client connections│           │ to each other    │       │  │
  │  │  └──────────────────┘           └──────────────────┘       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  etcd STATIC POD FLAGS (in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml):  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # CLIENT (API server → etcd):                               │  │
  │  │ --cert-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt            │  │
  │  │ --key-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key             │  │
  │  │ --trusted-ca-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt          │  │
  │  │ --client-cert-auth=true   ← ENFORCE client certs!          │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ # PEER (etcd ↔ etcd):                                      │  │
  │  │ --peer-cert-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/peer.crt         │  │
  │  │ --peer-key-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/peer.key          │  │
  │  │ --peer-trusted-ca-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt     │  │
  │  │ --peer-client-cert-auth=true  ← ENFORCE peer mTLS!         │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ # CRITICAL: Disable plaintext fallbacks                     │  │
  │  │ --auto-tls=false           ← NEVER allow auto-generated    │  │
  │  │ --peer-auto-tls=false      ←    self-signed certs!         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  VERIFY: etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \            │
  │    --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \                     │
  │    --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \                   │
  │    --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \                    │
  │    endpoint health                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS critical: CIS benchmark checks 2.1-2.4 specifically test etcd TLS. --client-cert-auth=true and --auto-tls=false are REQUIRED for CKS compliance. Without them, anyone who can reach port 2379 can read/write ALL cluster state.

1.3 CIS Benchmarks, Platform Verification & Node SecurityQ23-Q30

Cluster Setup Q23 of 200
What are CIS Benchmarks for Kubernetes and why are they important for cluster security?
A) CIS Benchmarks are performance tuning guides — they optimize Kubernetes for maximum throughput
B) CIS (Center for Internet Security) Benchmarks are a set of 100+ independently verified security configuration checks covering API server, etcd, kubelet, and worker nodes. They provide prescriptive guidance with PASS/FAIL criteria for hardening every Kubernetes component against known attack vectors
C) CIS Benchmarks are government regulations that mandate specific Kubernetes versions
D) CIS Benchmarks are a paid enterprise product — only available for commercial Kubernetes distributions
✅ Correct Answer: B

CIS Benchmarks are the industry-standard security configuration guide for Kubernetes. They define specific, auditable checks — e.g., "1.2.1: Ensure that the --anonymous-auth argument is set to false" — with clear remediation steps. They're free, community-reviewed, and map directly to CKS exam objectives.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CIS Kubernetes Benchmark — Scope & Structure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CIS KUBERNETES BENCHMARK v1.7.0 — CHECK CATEGORIES:              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  SECTION 1: Control Plane Components                         │ │
  │  │  1.1  API Server (21 checks)  — anonymous-auth, profiling,  │ │
  │  │       AlwaysPullImages, RBAC, TLS, etcd connectivity         │ │
  │  │  1.2  Scheduler (2 checks)   — profiling, binding           │ │
  │  │  1.3  Controller Manager (3 checks) — profiling, RBAC       │ │
  │  │  1.4  Configuration Files (8 checks) — file permissions     │ │
  │  │       (/etc/kubernetes/manifests/*, admin.conf, etc.)       │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  SECTION 2: etcd (7 checks)                                  │ │
  │  │  2.1  cert-file + key-file set                               │ │
  │  │  2.2  client-cert-auth=true                                  │ │
  │  │  2.3  auto-tls=false                                         │ │
  │  │  2.4  peer-cert-file + peer-key-file set                     │ │
  │  │  2.5  peer-client-cert-auth=true                             │ │
  │  │  2.6  peer-auto-tls=false                                    │ │
  │  │  2.7  Unique CA for etcd                                     │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  SECTION 3: Control Plane Configuration (3 checks)           │ │
  │  │  SECTION 4: Worker Nodes (31 checks)                         │ │
  │  │  4.1  kubelet (14 checks) — anonymous-auth, authorization   │ │
  │  │  4.2  kubelet config (8 checks) — config file permissions   │ │
  │  │  SECTION 5: Policies (28 checks)                             │ │
  │  │  5.1  RBAC (5 checks), 5.2 Pod Security (9 checks)          │ │
  │  │  5.3 NetworkPolicy (5 checks), 5.4 Secrets (3 checks)       │ │
  │  │  5.5-5.7 Extensions, General, etc.                           │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Running CIS benchmarks against your cluster before deploying to production is a basic security hygiene practice. Even a single FAIL (e.g., anonymous auth enabled on kubelet) could allow an unauthenticated attacker to execute commands in any pod on that node.

Cluster Setup Q24 of 200
What is kube-bench and how do you use it to assess your cluster's CIS compliance?
A) kube-bench is a built-in kubectl subcommand — kubectl bench runs compliance checks natively
B) kube-bench is a GUI dashboard that displays cluster metrics — it doesn't check security
C) kube-bench is an open-source tool from Aqua Security that automates CIS Benchmark checks. It runs as a Job or binary that inspects your cluster components (API server config, etcd flags, kubelet settings) and produces a PASS/FAIL/WARN report mapped to specific CIS check IDs. Run with --targets master,node,etcd,policies
D) kube-bench only works on managed Kubernetes services (EKS, GKE, AKS) — it cannot run on self-managed clusters
✅ Correct Answer: C

kube-bench automates what would otherwise be hours of manual configuration review. It reads the actual running configuration of your components and compares them against CIS benchmarks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kube-bench — Automated CIS Compliance Scanning
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RUNNING kube-bench:                                              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # As a Kubernetes Job:                                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl apply -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/aquasec │  │
  │  │   /kube-bench/main/job.yaml                                 │  │
  │  │ kubectl logs job/kube-bench                                 │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │

  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ssh node-1                                                   │  │

  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  SAMPLE OUTPUT:                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │

  │  │        file permissions are set to 644 or more restrictive  │  │

  │  │                                                              │  │

  │  │ 1.1.12 Edit /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml   │  │
  │  │       and add: --anonymous-auth=false                       │  │

  │  │   --targets master --json > cks-compliance-report.json      │  │

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: You may be asked to run kube-bench and fix specific FAIL items. Know the common failures: anonymous-auth, profiling, AlwaysPullImages, client-cert-auth for etcd.

Cluster Setup Q25 of 200
Which etcd configuration flags are critical for CIS compliance and MUST be explicitly set?
A) --client-cert-auth=true, --peer-client-cert-auth=true, and --auto-tls=false. These enforce mutual TLS for ALL etcd connections and prevent automatic self-signed certificate generation which would bypass authentication entirely
B) Only --listen-client-urls — restricting the etcd listening address is sufficient for security
C) --data-dir and --name — etcd security is handled entirely at the network firewall level
D) etcd has no security-critical flags — it's secured by the API server's RBAC
✅ Correct Answer: A

etcd stores ALL cluster state — every Secret, every ConfigMap, every RBAC rule. Without --client-cert-auth=true, anyone who can reach port 2379 can read/write your entire cluster without authentication. These flags are CIS checks 2.2, 2.3, and 2.5.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd Security Flags — The Critical Three
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  THE THREE CRITICAL etcd SECURITY FLAGS:                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ① --client-cert-auth=true                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ CIS Check: 2.2                                               │ │
  │  │ What it does: etcd REQUIRES clients (API server) to present  │ │
  │  │ a valid certificate signed by the trusted CA                 │ │
  │  │ WITHOUT IT: Anyone on the network can:                       │ │
  │  │   etcdctl --endpoints=10.0.0.1:2379 get / --prefix           │ │
  │  │   → Dumps ALL Secrets, ConfigMaps, RBAC, EVERYTHING!         │ │
  │  │ FIX: Add to /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml              │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ② --peer-client-cert-auth=true                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ CIS Check: 2.5                                               │ │
  │  │ What it does: etcd REQUIRES peer members to present valid    │ │
  │  │ certificates for RAFT consensus communication               │ │
  │  │ WITHOUT IT: A rogue node can join the etcd cluster and       │ │
  │  │ poison the RAFT log — cluster compromise                    │ │
  │  │ FIX: Add to /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml              │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ③ --auto-tls=false                                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ CIS Check: 2.3                                               │ │
  │  │ What it does: PREVENTS etcd from auto-generating self-signed │ │
  │  │ certificates if no explicit certs are provided               │ │
  │  │ WITHOUT IT: If cert files are missing, etcd silently creates │ │
  │  │ self-signed certs — traffic is "encrypted" but ANYONE can    │ │
  │  │ generate a matching self-signed cert and connect!            │ │
  │  │ FIX: Add to /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml              │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If etcd is compromised, the attacker gets: database passwords (from Secrets), Django SECRET_KEY (from Secrets), all RBAC rules, and all ConfigMap data. The entire application's security collapses.

Cluster Setup Q26 of 200
CIS benchmarks require kubelet to disable anonymous authentication. What flags achieve this and why is it critical?
A) kubelet doesn't have authentication — pods communicate directly without any auth layer
B) --anonymous-auth=false and --authorization-mode=Webhook. Without these, an unauthenticated request to kubelet's API (port 10250) can execute commands in ANY pod, read pod logs, and access container filesystems — full node compromise without any credentials
C) Kubelet anonymous auth is a legacy feature required for backward compatibility — it cannot be disabled
D) The kubelet is protected by the API server — no separate kubelet authentication is needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

The kubelet API on port 10250 is a POWERFUL endpoint — it allows pod exec, log retrieval, and metrics. If anonymous auth is enabled (default in some versions!), anyone who can reach port 10250 can run commands in any pod on that node.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubelet Anonymous Auth — The Danger & The Fix
  THE DANGER — Anonymous Auth Enabled:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Attacker discovers node IP and port 10250 is open:               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  curl -k https://10.0.1.5:10250/runningpods/                     │
  │  → Lists ALL pods on the node (NO authentication!)                │
  │                                                                   │
  │  curl -k https://10.0.1.5:10250/run/anihpj/anihpj-db-0/postgres \│
  │    -d "cmd=env"                                                    │
  │  → Executes command in PostgreSQL container!                      │
  │  → Dumps DATABASE_PASSWORD, SECRET_KEY, AWS credentials!         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ CIS check 4.1.1: FAIL — "Ensure kubelet anonymous-auth=false"│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  THE FIX — All Options Required:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  In kubelet config (/var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml):                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ authentication:                                              │  │
  │  │   anonymous:                                                 │  │
  │  │     enabled: false    ← CIS 4.1.1: DISABLE anon access      │  │
  │  │   webhook:                                                  │  │
  │  │     enabled: true      ← CIS 4.1.2: ENABLE webhook auth     │  │
  │  │ authorization:                                               │  │
  │  │   mode: Webhook       ← CIS 4.1.2: Use API server authz     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  OR via kubelet flags:                                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ --anonymous-auth=false                                      │  │
  │  │ --authorization-mode=Webhook                                 │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  VERIFY:                                                          │
  │  curl -k https://10.0.1.5:10250/runningpods/                     │
  │  → 401 Unauthorized ✅ (Anon access BLOCKED)                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every node running anihpj-db (PostgreSQL) has kubelet on port 10250. Without these flags, an attacker who compromises ANY pod on that node can exec into the database container and dump all user data.

Cluster Setup Q27 of 200
Why does CIS require --profiling=false on the API server, and what is the AlwaysPullImages admission plugin?
A) Profiling is a deprecated feature — it's required to be disabled only for performance reasons
B) AlwaysPullImages slows down pod startup — CIS requires it disabled for efficiency
C) --profiling=false disables the pprof debugging endpoint (/debug/pprof/) which exposes memory dumps, goroutine stacks, and CPU profiles — sensitive runtime information that aids attackers in exploit development. AlwaysPullImages admission plugin forces kubelet to pull images from the registry on EVERY pod start, preventing stale cached images and ensuring image policies (signing, scanning) are enforced
D) Both are optional performance-tuning features with no security implications
✅ Correct Answer: C

--profiling exposes deep runtime internals that help attackers understand memory layout and identify vulnerabilities. AlwaysPullImages prevents an attacker from using a previously-pulled vulnerable image that has since been patched in the registry.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Server Profiling + AlwaysPullImages — Defense in Depth
  --profiling=false (CIS 1.1.21):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITH profiling enabled (default!):                               │
  │  curl https://api-server:6443/debug/pprof/heap                   │
  │  → Returns memory dump with object allocations, memory addresses  │
  │  → Attacker can analyze memory layout for exploitation            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WITH profiling disabled (CIS compliant):                         │
  │  curl https://api-server:6443/debug/pprof/heap                   │
  │  → 404 Not Found (endpoint disabled)                              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  FIX: Add --profiling=false to kube-apiserver.yaml               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  AlwaysPullImages Admission Plugin (CIS 1.1.19):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCENARIO: Image caching attack                                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Day 1: Deploy anihpj-api:v1 (contains CVE)                       │
  │  Day 2: Image pulled to node, cached locally                      │
  │  Day 3: Security team patches anihpj-api:v1 → no more CVE        │
  │  Day 4: Pod restarts — WITHOUT AlwaysPullImages:                  │
  │         kubelet uses CACHED v1 (STILL vulnerable!)                │
  │         WITH AlwaysPullImages:                                    │
  │         kubelet PULLS fresh v1 from registry (patched!)           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ENABLE:                                                          │
  │  --enable-admission-plugins=...,AlwaysPullImages,...              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ This also enforces image signature verification:              │
  │  If image is signed (Cosign) → AlwaysPullImages ensures           │
  │  you always fetch the LATEST signed version                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If the Django API image is patched for a critical CVE but AlwaysPullImages is disabled, restarted pods will use the cached vulnerable image. The admission plugin ensures you always run the intended (and scanned/signed) image.

Cluster Setup Q28 of 200
How should you verify the security configuration of CoreDNS (kube-dns) in your cluster?
A) Check that CoreDNS is not exposed externally (no NodePort/LoadBalancer), verify RBAC is scoped to kube-system only, ensure the CoreDNS ConfigMap (Corefile) doesn't contain misconfigurations like open recursion, and confirm NetworkPolicy allows DNS (UDP 53) only from intended pods
B) CoreDNS is a read-only service with no security implications — no verification is needed
C) Only check that CoreDNS pods are Running — security is handled automatically by the CNI plugin
D) CoreDNS security is managed entirely by the cloud provider — self-managed clusters don't need to check it
✅ Correct Answer: A

CoreDNS is a critical infrastructure component — if compromised, an attacker can redirect cluster DNS to malicious endpoints (DNS poisoning). Verify it's locked down like any other critical service.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CoreDNS Security Verification Checklist
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COREDNS SECURITY AUDIT:                                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ① CHECK RBAC:                                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl get clusterrole system:coredns -o yaml              │  │
  │  │ kubectl get clusterrolebinding system:coredns -o yaml       │  │
  │  │ → Should have minimal permissions (only what DNS needs)     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ② CHECK SERVICE EXPOSURE:                                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl get svc -n kube-system kube-dns                     │  │
  │  │ → TYPE should be ClusterIP (NOT NodePort or LoadBalancer!)  │  │
  │  │ → If NodePort: DNS is exposed to internet → DANGER!         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ③ CHECK COREFILE (ConfigMap):                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl get cm coredns -n kube-system -o yaml               │  │
  │  │ → Look for:                                                  │  │
  │  │   • "prometheus :9153" — OK (metrics)                       │  │
  │  │   • "health :8080" — OK (health check)                      │  │
  │  │   • "whoami" plugin — ⚠️ NOT in production (info leak)     │  │
  │  │   • Open recursion config — ⚠️ DNS amplification attack    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ④ CHECK NETWORKPOLICY:                                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl get networkpolicy -n kube-system                    │  │
  │  │ → DNS pods should have Ingress allowing UDP 53               │  │
  │  │ → Should NOT have Ingress from untrusted namespaces         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS note: A compromised CoreDNS can redirect anihpj-api's outbound connections from api.payments.com to an attacker-controlled server — silently stealing payment data. DNS security is part of the CKS attack surface assessment.

Cluster Setup Q29 of 200
Before deploying Kubernetes binaries (kubeadm, kubelet, kubectl) to production nodes, how do you verify they haven't been tampered with?
A) Check the file size — tampered binaries are always larger than originals
B) Verify the SHA256 checksum of downloaded binaries against the official Kubernetes release checksum file. For example: sha256sum kubeadm and compare with the published SHA256SUMS file from dl.k8s.io. This ensures the binary hasn't been modified in transit or replaced by a malicious mirror
C) Run the binary with --version — if it outputs the correct version number, it's authentic
D) Platform binary verification is only required for air-gapped environments — public internet downloads are always trusted
✅ Correct Answer: B

Binary verification via checksum is a fundamental supply chain security control. Without it, you could be deploying a backdoored kubelet that exfiltrates Secrets, modifies pod specs, or creates hidden containers. Always verify before deploying.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Platform Binary Verification Workflow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SECURE DOWNLOAD & VERIFICATION PROCESS:                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 1: Download binary AND checksum file (over HTTPS)           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ VERSION="v1.30.0"                                           │  │
  │  │ curl -LO "https://dl.k8s.io/release/$VERSION/bin/linux/    │  │
  │  │   amd64/kubeadm"                                            │  │
  │  │ curl -LO "https://dl.k8s.io/release/$VERSION/bin/linux/    │  │
  │  │   amd64/kubeadm.sha256"                                     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 2: Verify checksum                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ echo "$(cat kubeadm.sha256)  kubeadm" | sha256sum --check   │  │
  │  │ → kubeadm: OK  ✅ (binary matches published checksum)       │  │
  │  │ → kubeadm: FAILED  ❌ (BINARY TAMPERED! DO NOT DEPLOY!)     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 3: Repeat for ALL binaries                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ sha256sum --check kubeadm.sha256  → OK/NOT OK               │  │
  │  │ sha256sum --check kubelet.sha256  → OK/NOT OK               │  │
  │  │ sha256sum --check kubectl.sha256  → OK/NOT OK               │  │
  │  │ sha256sum --check kube-apiserver.sha256 → OK/NOT OK         │  │
  │  │ sha256sum --check etcd.sha256       → OK/NOT OK             │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ATTACK SCENARIOS THIS PREVENTS:                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ • Man-in-the-middle: Attacker replaces binary during download│ │
  │  │ • Compromised mirror: Unofficial mirror serves backdoored bin│ │
  │  │ • Supply chain attack: Upstream compromise of build pipeline │ │
  │  │ • Stale cache: CDN/proxy serves old vulnerable version       │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost infrastructure: Every node running your application stack should have its kubelet binary verified. A tampered kubelet could silently modify pod specs, inject sidecar containers, or exfiltrate Secret data to an external server.

Cluster Setup Q30 of 200
How should CIS compliance scanning be integrated into a CI/CD pipeline for continuous security validation?
A) CIS scans are a one-time activity performed during initial cluster setup only
B) CIS compliance is verified manually by a security auditor once per quarter — automation is unnecessary
C) CIS scans should run as a cronjob inside the cluster but never block deployments — compliance is informational only
D) Integrate kube-bench as a CI/CD pipeline stage that runs on every infrastructure change (cluster upgrade, node addition, config change). Automatically BLOCK deployments if CRITICAL CIS checks fail. Generate compliance reports as pipeline artifacts for audit trails. Use tools like kube-bench + Trivy + OPA/Gatekeeper for a layered compliance approach
✅ Correct Answer: D

CIS compliance is not a one-time checkbox — it's a continuous process. Every cluster upgrade, node addition, or configuration change can introduce new compliance gaps. Integrating kube-bench into CI/CD ensures continuous compliance verification.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CI/CD CIS Compliance Pipeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTINUOUS COMPLIANCE PIPELINE:                                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  TRIGGER: Infrastructure change (PR merged, cluster upgraded)     │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ STAGE 1: kube-bench scan                                      │ │
  │  │ docker run aquasec/kube-bench --targets master,node --json    │ │
  │  │ → Output: cks-compliance.json                                 │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ STAGE 2: Parse results                                        │ │
  │  │ python parse-compliance.py --input cks-compliance.json        │ │
  │  │ → Extract: FAIL count, CRITICAL fails, WARN count             │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ STAGE 3: Decision gate                                        │ │
  │  │ if CRITICAL_FAILS > 0:                                        │ │
  │  │     echo "❌ CRITICAL CIS checks FAILED — BLOCKING deploy"   │ │
  │  │     exit 1  ← Pipeline FAILS, deployment BLOCKED              │ │
  │  │ if HIGH_FAILS > THRESHOLD:                                    │ │
  │  │     echo "⚠️ WARNING: High-severity fails, review needed"    │ │
  │  │     exit 0  ← Allow but alert                                 │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ STAGE 4: Archive report                                       │ │
  │  │ Upload cks-compliance.json to artifact store (S3, GCS)        │ │
  │  │ → Audit trail: "Cluster X was CIS compliant on DATE"          │ │
  │  │ → Required for: PCI-DSS 11.2, SOC2 CC7.1, HIPAA 164.312      │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If you're processing job applications with PII (resumes, contact info), continuous CIS compliance is essential for regulatory requirements. A single kubelet misconfiguration could expose applicant data — the CI/CD gate prevents this.

🔒 Category 2: Cluster Hardening (15%)Q31-Q60

🟡 Intermediate: Locking Down Identities, Access & Policies

This category is about controlling WHO can do WHAT in your cluster. Think of it like setting up employee badges (ServiceAccounts), door locks (RBAC), security cameras (audit), and building codes (Pod Security Standards). For anihpj/jobpost, this means the API pod has only the permissions it needs to list pods — not delete them, not read Secrets, not create namespaces. Every identity is minimized, every permission is explicit.

Q31-Q60 30 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

2.1 RBAC — Role-Based Access Control Deep DiveQ31-Q42

Cluster Hardening Q31 of 200
What is the fundamental difference between Authentication and Authorization in Kubernetes RBAC?
A) Authentication and Authorization are the same thing — Kubernetes uses them interchangeably
B) Authentication controls WHAT you can do; Authorization proves WHO you are
C) Authentication (AuthN) proves WHO you are — verifying your identity via X509 certs, bearer tokens, or OIDC. Authorization (AuthZ) determines WHAT you can do — checking RBAC rules AFTER identity is confirmed. AuthN happens FIRST, then AuthZ
D) Authorization happens at the kubelet level; Authentication happens at the API server level
✅ Correct Answer: C

Authentication = "Who are you?" (identity). Authorization = "Are you allowed to do this?" (permissions). They are sequential gates in the API request pipeline — you must pass AuthN before AuthZ is even evaluated.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Request Pipeline — AuthN → AuthZ → Admission
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl get pods -n anihpj                                       │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ ① AUTHENTICATION (AuthN): "Who are you?"                     │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ Methods:                                                      │ │
  │  │ • X509 Client Certificate → CN=anihpj-api                     │ │
  │  │ • Bearer Token (ServiceAccount) → system:serviceaccount:     │ │
  │  │   anihpj:api-sa                                              │ │
  │  │ • OIDC (Google, GitHub, Azure AD) → alice@company.com        │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ FAIL: 401 Unauthorized ("I don't know who you are")          │ │
  │  │ PASS: Identity established → move to AuthZ                   │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ ② AUTHORIZATION (AuthZ): "Can you do this?"                   │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ Methods: RBAC (default), ABAC, Webhook, Node                  │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ API server checks ALL RoleBindings + ClusterRoleBindings      │ │
  │  │ → Does any binding grant "get pods" to this identity?        │ │
  │  │                                                               │ │
  │  │ FAIL: 403 Forbidden ("I know who you are, but you can't")    │ │
  │  │ PASS: Permission granted → move to Admission                 │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Memory trick: AuthN = "Show me your ID." AuthZ = "Now let me check if your ID has access to this room." You can have a valid ID (authenticated) but still be denied entry (not authorized).

Cluster Hardening Q32 of 200
When should you use a Role (namespace-scoped) vs a ClusterRole (cluster-scoped) in RBAC?
A) Always use ClusterRole — it works everywhere and is simpler to manage
B) Use Role + RoleBinding for namespace-scoped resources (pods, services, configmaps within one namespace). Use ClusterRole + ClusterRoleBinding for cluster-scoped resources (nodes, PVs, namespaces) OR when you need the same permissions across ALL namespaces. A ClusterRole can also be bound via a namespaced RoleBinding to grant cluster-role permissions in just ONE namespace
C) Role and ClusterRole are interchangeable — the only difference is the YAML filename
D) Use Role for read-only access and ClusterRole for write access regardless of scope
✅ Correct Answer: B

The key distinction: Role is for resources that exist within a namespace (pods, services, configmaps). ClusterRole is for resources that exist at the cluster level (nodes, persistentvolumes, namespaces themselves) OR for granting the same permissions across all namespaces.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Role vs ClusterRole — Scope Comparison
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Role (namespace-scoped):                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │  │
  │  │ kind: Role                                                  │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: pod-reader, namespace: anihpj}             │  │
  │  │ rules:                                                      │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                           │  │
  │  │   resources: ["pods", "services"]                           │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                           │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ Scope: ONLY namespace "anihpj"                               │  │
  │  │ CAN: get pods in anihpj                                      │  │
  │  │ CANNOT: get pods in kube-system                              │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ClusterRole (cluster-scoped):                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │  │
  │  │ kind: ClusterRole                                           │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: node-reader}                               │  │
  │  │ rules:                                                      │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                           │  │
  │  │   resources: ["nodes"]       ← Cluster-scoped resource!    │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list"]                                    │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ Scope: ENTIRE cluster (nodes have no namespace)              │  │
  │  │ CAN: kubectl get nodes                                       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  COMBO: ClusterRole + RoleBinding (namespace-scoped binding)      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ClusterRole: secret-reader (cluster-scoped rule definition) │  │
  │  │ RoleBinding in "anihpj" → Grants secret-reader in anihpj   │  │
  │  │ RoleBinding in "dev"      → Grants secret-reader in dev    │  │
  │  │ → One ClusterRole, bound to multiple namespaces!            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use a Role for api-sa permissions within the anihpj namespace (get/list pods). Use a ClusterRole only if the api-sa truly needs cluster-wide access (e.g., listing nodes for topology awareness).

Cluster Hardening Q33 of 200
What are the RBAC verbs and how do they map to HTTP methods? Which verb grants FULL access?
A) Verbs are: get, list, watch, create, update, patch, delete, deletecollection. The wildcard * grants ALL verbs (full access). These map to HTTP: get→GET, create→POST, update→PUT, patch→PATCH, delete→DELETE, list→GET(with ?watch), watch→GET(?watch=true)
B) Only three verbs exist: read, write, admin — Kubernetes maps these to CRUD operations automatically
C) Verbs are defined by the CNI plugin — they vary based on network configuration
D) The only verb that matters is * — fine-grained verbs are deprecated in modern Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: A

RBAC verbs are the atomic permission units. Using * grants all verbs — effectively admin access. For least privilege, always specify exact verbs like ["get", "list"] for read-only or ["get", "list", "create", "update"] for read-write.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RBAC Verbs — HTTP Mapping & Danger Levels
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VERB          │ HTTP      │ kubectl example        │ RISK       │
  │  ──────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────│
  │  get           │ GET       │ kubectl get pod x      │ 🟢 LOW     │
  │  list          │ GET       │ kubectl get pods       │ 🟢 LOW     │
  │  watch         │ GET       │ kubectl get pods -w    │ 🟢 LOW     │
  │  create        │ POST      │ kubectl create -f yaml │ 🟡 MEDIUM  │
  │  update        │ PUT       │ kubectl replace        │ 🟡 MEDIUM  │
  │  patch         │ PATCH     │ kubectl patch          │ 🟡 MEDIUM  │
  │  delete        │ DELETE    │ kubectl delete pod x   │ 🔴 HIGH    │
  │  deletecollection │ DELETE │ kubectl delete pods -l │ 🔴 HIGH    │
  │  * (wildcard)  │ ALL       │ ANYTHING               │ 💀 CRITICAL│
  │  ──────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────│
  │                                                                    │
  │  LEAST-PRIVILEGE PATTERN for anihpj-api-sa:                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ rules:                                                      │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                           │  │
  │  │   resources: ["pods"]                                       │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]  ← Read-only: SAFE       │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ NOT: verbs: ["*"]  ← NEVER use wildcard for app SAs!       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS Critical: The wildcard * is the most dangerous RBAC misconfiguration. A ServiceAccount with verbs: ["*"] on resources: ["*"] is equivalent to cluster-admin. Always audit for wildcard verbs in production.

Cluster Hardening Q34 of 200
What are RBAC subresources and why are they critical for security? Give examples using pods/log and pods/exec.
A) Subresources are deprecated — all resource access is now flat with no subresource concept
B) Subresources are alternative names for the same resource — pods/log is identical to pods for RBAC purposes
C) Subresources are only used for metrics and monitoring — they have no security relevance
D) Subresources allow GRANULAR control over specific operations on a resource. pods/log controls log access, pods/exec controls exec access. You can grant "get pods" WITHOUT granting "get pods/log" or "create pods/exec" — critical because exec provides SHELL access inside containers, which is far more dangerous than just viewing pod metadata
✅ Correct Answer: D

Subresources are the difference between "I can see that a pod exists" and "I can open a shell inside that pod." Without subresource control, anyone who can "get pods" could potentially "exec into pods" — a massive privilege escalation vector.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Subresource Security — The exec/log/portforward Gap
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DANGEROUS RBAC (NO subresource separation):                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ rules:                                                      │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                           │  │
  │  │   resources: ["pods"]                                       │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list"]                                    │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ With just "get pods", can I...                              │  │
  │  │ kubectl get pods?            ✅ YES (expected)              │  │
  │  │ kubectl logs pod/name?       ✅ YES (subresource implied!)  │  │
  │  │ kubectl exec -it pod -- sh?  ✅ YES (subresource implied!)  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ "get pods" implicitly grants logs + exec + portforward!  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  SECURE RBAC (subresource EXPLICITLY controlled):                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ rules:                                                      │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                           │  │
  │  │   resources: ["pods"]                                       │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list"]     ← Metadata only                │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                           │  │
  │  │   resources: ["pods/log"]    ← SEPARATE log access          │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get"]                                             │  │
  │  │ # NO pods/exec rule → exec IS BLOCKED!                       │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ kubectl get pods?            ✅ YES                          │  │
  │  │ kubectl logs pod/name?       ✅ YES (explicit pods/log)     │  │
  │  │ kubectl exec -it pod -- sh?  ❌ FORBIDDEN!                  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CRITICAL SUBRESOURCES:                                           │
  │  pods/log, pods/exec, pods/portforward, pods/attach, pods/status │
  │  nodes/proxy (allows accessing kubelet API through API server!)   │
  │  services/proxy (allows proxying to cluster internal services!)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The monitoring ServiceAccount should have pods/log access but NEVER pods/exec. If an attacker compromises the monitoring pod, they can read logs but cannot get a shell inside the database container.

Cluster Hardening Q35 of 200
What is the difference between RoleBinding and ClusterRoleBinding, and what subjects can they bind?
A) RoleBinding binds users; ClusterRoleBinding binds ServiceAccounts — they cannot bind the same subjects
B) There is no difference — they are aliases for the same resource
C) RoleBinding is NAMESPACE-scoped and grants permissions within that specific namespace. ClusterRoleBinding is CLUSTER-scoped and grants permissions across ALL namespaces. BOTH can bind the same subjects: Users, Groups, and ServiceAccounts. A ClusterRole can be bound via a RoleBinding to limit its scope to a single namespace
D) RoleBinding is for Roles only; ClusterRoleBinding is for ClusterRoles only — they cannot cross-reference
✅ Correct Answer: C

The binding (RoleBinding/ClusterRoleBinding) determines SCOPE. The role (Role/ClusterRole) defines PERMISSIONS. You can bind a ClusterRole (cluster-scoped permissions) to a RoleBinding (single namespace) — this is a powerful pattern for reusing permission sets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Binding Matrix — Scope × Permissions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              │  Role (NS-scoped)  │  ClusterRole (Cluster-wide)  │
  │  ────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────────── │
  │  RoleBinding │  NS-only perms    │  ClusterRole perms in 1 NS   │
  │  (1 NS)      │  in that NS       │  ← REUSABLE! One ClusterRole │
  │              │                   │    bound to many namespaces   │
  │  ────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────────── │
  │  ClusterRole │  INVALID          │  ClusterRole perms in ALL NS │
  │  Binding     │  (can't bind      │  ← Cluster-wide access       │
  │  (all NS)    │   NS Role to      │                               │
  │              │   cluster scope)  │                               │
  │  ────────────┴───────────────────┴───────────────────────────── │
  │                                                                   │
  │  anihpj EXAMPLE — Reusable ClusterRole pattern:                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ClusterRole: pod-reader [get, list, watch pods]            │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ RoleBinding in "anihpj": api-sa → ClusterRole pod-reader   │  │
  │  │ → api-sa can list pods in anihpj ONLY                       │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ RoleBinding in "monitoring": prometheus-sa → pod-reader    │  │
  │  │ → prometheus-sa can list pods in monitoring ONLY            │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ ONE ClusterRole definition, reused in many namespaces    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: Know the binding matrix. A common exam task: "Grant user alice the ability to read pods in namespace dev using the existing ClusterRole view." The answer is a RoleBinding, not a ClusterRoleBinding.

Cluster Hardening Q36 of 200
What is a Kubernetes ServiceAccount and how does it provide pod identity?
A) ServiceAccount is a human user account for logging into the Kubernetes dashboard
B) A ServiceAccount is a Kubernetes-native identity for PODS (not humans). Each namespace has a "default" ServiceAccount automatically created. When a pod is created without specifying a ServiceAccount, it uses the default. The SA's token is automatically mounted into the pod at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token, giving the pod an identity to authenticate to the API server
C) ServiceAccount is a cloud IAM concept — it only works on AWS, GCP, and Azure managed Kubernetes
D) ServiceAccounts are optional — pods don't need identities to function in Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: B

ServiceAccounts are machine identities — they're how pods authenticate to the Kubernetes API. When a pod needs to call the API server (e.g., a controller or operator), it uses its mounted SA token. Every pod gets a token automatically unless automountServiceAccountToken: false is set.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ServiceAccount — Pod Identity Lifecycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  POD IDENTITY FLOW:                                               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ① ServiceAccount created:                                   │  │
  │  │    kubectl create serviceaccount api-sa -n anihpj           │  │
  │  │    → SA "api-sa" exists in namespace "anihpj"               │  │
  │  │    → A Secret (token) is auto-created: api-sa-token-xxxx   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ② Pod references the SA:                                    │  │
  │  │    spec:                                                     │  │
  │  │      serviceAccountName: api-sa                              │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ③ Token mounted INTO pod:                                   │  │
  │  │    /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/           │  │
  │  │    ├── token         ← JWT token for API authentication    │  │
  │  │    ├── ca.crt        ← Cluster CA cert                      │  │
  │  │    └── namespace     ← Current namespace                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ④ Pod authenticates to API server:                          │  │
  │  │    curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $(cat /var/run/secrets/   │  │
  │  │      kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token)" \                  │  │
  │  │      https://kubernetes.default.svc/api/v1/namespaces/...   │  │
  │  │    → API server sees: system:serviceaccount:anihpj:api-sa  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Security note: The default ServiceAccount exists in every namespace and has NO permissions by default (since K8s 1.24). However, if someone previously bound RBAC to the default SA, ALL pods in that namespace inherit those permissions — a common privilege escalation path.

Cluster Hardening Q37 of 200
What does automountServiceAccountToken: false do and when should you use it?
A) It prevents the SA token from being automatically mounted into the pod at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/. Use it when your pod does NOT need to communicate with the Kubernetes API server — this reduces the attack surface. If an attacker compromises the pod, they won't find a ready-to-use API token
B) It disables the ServiceAccount entirely — the pod runs with no identity and cannot function
C) It mounts the token in a different directory — useful for legacy applications that expect tokens at a custom path
D) It only works on cloud-managed Kubernetes — on-prem clusters always mount tokens
✅ Correct Answer: A

By default, every pod gets a ServiceAccount token mounted. If your pod doesn't need API access (most application pods don't!), this token is an unnecessary attack vector. A compromised pod can use it to enumerate resources, read Secrets (if RBAC allows), or escalate privileges.

📝 Detailed Explanation
automountServiceAccountToken — Attack Surface Reduction
  WITHOUT automountServiceAccountToken: false (DEFAULT):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod: anihpj-web (compromised)                                │
  │                                                               │
  │  $ cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token   │
  │  eyJhbGciOi...  ← VALID JWT TOKEN!                           │
  │                                                               │
  │  $ curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \                  │
  │    https://kubernetes.default/api/v1/namespaces/anihpj/pods  │
  │  → Lists all pods in anihpj namespace                        │
  │  → Attacker now maps your infrastructure!                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH automountServiceAccountToken: false:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod: anihpj-web (compromised)                                │
  │                                                               │
  │  $ cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token   │
  │  cat: No such file or directory  ← Token NOT mounted!        │
  │                                                               │
  │  $ curl https://kubernetes.default/api/...                   │
  │  → Connection refused (no auth token)                        │
  │  → Attack surface REDUCED                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  APPLY TO ALL NON-API PODS:
  spec:
    automountServiceAccountToken: false
    containers:
    - name: web
      image: anihpj/web

For anihpj/jobpost: The web and API pods serve HTTP traffic — they don't need to talk to the Kubernetes API. Set automountServiceAccountToken: false on both. The database pod DEFINITELY doesn't need API access — disable it there too.

Cluster Hardening Q38 of 200
How do you test whether a user or ServiceAccount has permission to perform a specific action using kubectl auth can-i?
A) Check the RoleBinding YAML manually — there is no CLI tool for testing permissions
B) Just try the command — if it fails with 403 Forbidden, they don't have permission
C) Use kubectl describe permissions — the built-in permission inspector
D) kubectl auth can-i <verb> <resource> --as=<user> -n <ns> tests specific permissions. --list shows ALL permissions. --as-group=<group> tests group membership. This is essential for RBAC auditing and debugging "why can't my pod do X?"
✅ Correct Answer: D

kubectl auth can-i is the RBAC testing tool. It queries the API server's SubjectAccessReview API to determine if a specific identity can perform a specific action — without actually performing it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl auth can-i — RBAC Debugging
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TESTING PERMISSIONS:                                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Test specific action as a user:                                │
  │  kubectl auth can-i create pods --as=system:serviceaccount:      │
  │    anihpj:api-sa -n anihpj                                       │
  │  → yes  /  no                                                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # List ALL permissions for a ServiceAccount:                     │
  │  kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:           │
  │    anihpj:api-sa -n anihpj                                       │
  │  → Resources  Non-Resource URLs  Resource Names  Verbs            │
  │    pods       []                  []              [get list watch]│
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Test as a group:                                               │
  │  kubectl auth can-i get secrets --as=alice \                      │
  │    --as-group=developers -n anihpj                               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Test CLUSTER-level permissions (no -n flag):                   │
  │  kubectl auth can-i list nodes --as=alice                         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Check if YOUR current context can do something:                │
  │  kubectl auth can-i delete pods -n anihpj  ← Tests YOUR identity │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam tip: You'll almost certainly get an RBAC debugging task. Use kubectl auth can-i --list --as=<sa> -n <ns> to see exactly what permissions a ServiceAccount has, then fix any gaps.

Cluster Hardening Q39 of 200
Why should application pods NEVER use the cluster-admin ClusterRole, even temporarily?
A) cluster-admin is too slow — it adds latency to pod API calls
B) cluster-admin is only available on managed Kubernetes — self-managed clusters don't have it
C) cluster-admin grants UNRESTRICTED access to EVERY resource in EVERY namespace — including Secrets, RBAC rules, and node configuration. If an application pod with cluster-admin is compromised (RCE, SSRF, dependency attack), the attacker instantly has full cluster control: delete all deployments, exfiltrate all Secrets, create persistence backdoors, or deploy cryptocurrency miners cluster-wide
D) cluster-admin is fine for production — it's just a label that sounds scarier than it is
✅ Correct Answer: C

cluster-admin is the Kubernetes equivalent of Linux root. It bypasses ALL RBAC restrictions. A single compromised pod with cluster-admin means total cluster compromise — not just one namespace, not just one application, but EVERYTHING.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Least Privilege — Why cluster-admin Is a Nuclear Button
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BLAST RADIUS COMPARISON:                                         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Pod with scoped RBAC (api-sa: get/list pods in anihpj):         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Compromised pod CAN:                                        │  │
  │  │ ✅ List pods in anihpj namespace                            │  │
  │  │ ❌ Read Secrets (no RBAC)                                   │  │
  │  │ ❌ Delete deployments (no RBAC)                              │  │
  │  │ ❌ Create new pods in kube-system (no RBAC)                  │  │
  │  │ ❌ Escalate privileges (no RBAC)                             │  │
  │  │ → BLAST RADIUS: 1 namespace, read-only pod metadata         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Pod with cluster-admin:                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Compromised pod CAN:                                        │  │
  │  │ ✅ Read ALL Secrets in ALL namespaces                        │  │
  │  │ ✅ Delete ALL deployments, StatefulSets, DaemonSets          │  │
  │  │ ✅ Create new ClusterRoleBindings (persist access)           │  │
  │  │ ✅ Deploy pods in kube-system (hide in plain sight)          │  │
  │  │ ✅ Access etcd through API server proxy                     │  │
  │  │ → BLAST RADIUS: ENTIRE CLUSTER, TOTAL COMPROMISE            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The API pod should have ROLE: get/list pods only. The DB pod should have NO RBAC at all (automountServiceAccountToken: false). Never, ever, give cluster-admin to an application ServiceAccount — even during debugging.

Cluster Hardening Q40 of 200
Design the RBAC for anihpj/jobpost: api-sa needs to list pods (for service discovery), db-sa needs NO API access. What's the correct setup?
A) Bind both SAs to the view ClusterRole — it's simpler and suffices for service discovery
B) api-sa: Role with verbs [get, list, watch] on pods in anihpj namespace, bound via RoleBinding. db-sa: No RoleBinding + automountServiceAccountToken: false. This implements least privilege: API can discover pods, database has ZERO API access
C) Both SAs get cluster-admin — it's a trusted internal application, security isn't needed
D) api-sa: ClusterRole pod-reader. db-sa: Role pod-reader in anihpj namespace. Both need pods access
✅ Correct Answer: B

api-sa needs pod listing for service discovery (knowing which API pods are healthy). db-sa is PostgreSQL — it serves database queries, not Kubernetes API calls. The database pod should have its token completely disabled.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost RBAC — Least-Privilege Design
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  api-sa (Django API pod):                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │  │
  │  │ kind: Role                                                  │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: api-pod-reader, namespace: anihpj}         │  │
  │  │ rules:                                                      │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                           │  │
  │  │   resources: ["pods"]                                       │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]  ← Read-only pods        │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ---                                                          │  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │  │
  │  │ kind: RoleBinding                                           │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: api-pod-reader-binding, namespace: anihpj} │  │
  │  │ subjects:                                                    │  │
  │  │ - kind: ServiceAccount                                      │  │
  │  │   name: api-sa                                              │  │
  │  │   namespace: anihpj                                         │  │
  │  │ roleRef:                                                     │  │
  │  │   kind: Role                                                │  │
  │  │   name: api-pod-reader                                      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  db-sa (PostgreSQL pod):                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   serviceAccountName: db-sa                                 │  │
  │  │   automountServiceAccountToken: false  ← NO token mounted! │  │
  │  │   containers:                                               │  │
  │  │   - name: postgres                                         │  │
  │  │     image: postgres:15                                      │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ db-sa has: ZERO RoleBindings → ZERO API access              │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This is a textbook example of least-privilege RBAC design. Each component gets EXACTLY what it needs and NOTHING more.

Cluster Hardening Q41 of 200
How do you restrict access to the Kubernetes API server itself? What flags control authorization modes?
A) --authorization-mode=Node,RBAC on the API server enables Node authorizer (for kubelets) and RBAC (for everything else). --anonymous-auth=false blocks unauthenticated requests entirely. Combined, these ensure ONLY authenticated identities with explicit RBAC permissions can access the API
B) API server access is controlled by a firewall — no Kubernetes-level configuration is needed
C) --authorization-mode=AlwaysAllow is the secure default — it requires explicit RBAC rules
D) API server authorization is configured per namespace using ConfigMaps
✅ Correct Answer: A

The API server's --authorization-mode flag is the gatekeeper for ALL cluster access. Node,RBAC is the recommended configuration: Node authorizer handles kubelet API calls, RBAC handles everything else.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Server Authorization Modes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  API SERVER FLAGS (in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver): │
  │                                                                   │
  │  --authorization-mode=Node,RBAC                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ MODES (evaluated in ORDER):                                 │  │
  │  │ ① Node: Authorizes kubelet API requests (system:node:X)    │  │
  │  │ ② RBAC: Evaluates RoleBindings/ClusterRoleBindings         │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ OTHER MODES (use with caution):                              │  │
  │  │ • AlwaysAllow: ⚠️ DISABLES all authorization! NEVER in prod │  │
  │  │ • AlwaysDeny:  Used for testing only                        │  │
  │  │ • ABAC:        Attribute-based (legacy, avoid)              │  │
  │  │ • Webhook:     External authorization (OPA, custom)         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  --anonymous-auth=false  (CIS 1.1.12)                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ When anonymous-auth=TRUE (insecure default):                │  │
  │  │ curl https://api:6443/api/v1/pods                           │  │
  │  │ → Returns pods (as system:anonymous user)!                  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ When anonymous-auth=FALSE:                                   │  │
  │  │ curl https://api:6443/api/v1/pods                           │  │
  │  │ → 401 Unauthorized (must authenticate first)                │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS critical: CIS check 1.1.12 explicitly requires --anonymous-auth=false. This is one of the most commonly failed CIS checks and a guaranteed CKS exam topic.

Cluster Hardening Q42 of 200
What are RBAC aggregation rules and how do they use the rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-* labels?
A) Aggregation rules combine multiple namespaces into one — they're for multi-tenancy, not RBAC
B) Aggregation rules are deprecated — use ClusterRoleBinding with multiple subjects instead
C) Aggregation rules automatically delete unused ClusterRoles — they're for RBAC cleanup, not permission management
D) Aggregation rules allow a ClusterRole to dynamically INCLUDE rules from OTHER ClusterRoles that have specific labels. Example: ClusterRole "view" has aggregationRule.clusterRoleSelectors that matches roles with rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-view: "true". When you create a new CRD and a ClusterRole with that label, the "view" role AUTOMATICALLY gains access to the new CRD — no manual editing needed
✅ Correct Answer: D

RBAC aggregation is Kubernetes' built-in mechanism for dynamic permission composition. Instead of manually editing the "admin" or "view" ClusterRole every time you install a new CRD, you create a ClusterRole with the appropriate aggregation label, and it's automatically merged.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RBAC Aggregation — Dynamic Permission Composition
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HOW AGGREGATION WORKS:                                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 1: The "view" ClusterRole has an aggregationRule:            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │  │
  │  │ kind: ClusterRole                                           │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: view}                                      │  │
  │  │ aggregationRule:                                            │  │
  │  │   clusterRoleSelectors:                                     │  │
  │  │   - matchLabels:                                            │  │
  │  │       rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-view: "true"  │  │
  │  │ rules: []  ← Initially empty, filled by aggregation        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 2: A CRD provider creates a ClusterRole with the label:     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │  │
  │  │ kind: ClusterRole                                           │  │
  │  │ metadata:                                                   │  │
  │  │   name: cert-manager-view                                   │  │
  │  │   labels:                                                   │  │
  │  │     rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-view: "true"    │  │
  │  │ rules:                                                      │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: ["cert-manager.io"]                            │  │
  │  │   resources: ["certificates", "issuers"]                    │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  RESULT: The "view" ClusterRole now INCLUDES:                     │
  │  • Original rules + cert-manager.io rules (auto-merged!)         │
  │  • Anyone with "view" role can now read cert-manager resources   │
  │  • NO manual editing of the "view" ClusterRole needed!           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If you install cert-manager, its ClusterRole with aggregate-to-view: "true" automatically lets your monitoring SA (bound to "view") see certificate status — zero RBAC configuration needed.

2.2 ServiceAccount & Pod Identity SecurityQ43-Q50

Cluster Hardening Q43 of 200
What is the difference between bound tokens (TokenRequest API) and legacy tokens (Secret-based) for ServiceAccounts?
A) There is no difference — both are equally secure and interchangeable
B) Bound tokens (TokenRequest API, K8s 1.22+) are time-limited (default 1h, max 24h), audience-bound (scoped to specific API servers), and object-bound (tied to a specific pod — token dies with the pod). Legacy tokens (Secret-based) are non-expiring JWTs stored in Secrets — once stolen, valid forever. Bound tokens are MASSIVELY more secure
C) Legacy tokens are more secure because they're stored in Secrets which are encrypted at rest
D) Bound tokens only work on GKE — other distributions use legacy tokens exclusively
✅ Correct Answer: B

Bound tokens are the modern, secure replacement for legacy Secret-based SA tokens. They expire automatically, are scoped to a specific audience, and die with the pod. Legacy tokens are forever-valid JWTs stored in Secrets — a major security liability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
