Bound Tokens vs Legacy Tokens — Security Comparison
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LEGACY TOKENS (Secret-based, AVOID):                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ • Stored in: Secret (sa-name-token-xxxx)                    │  │
  │  │ • Expiry: NEVER (non-expiring JWT)                          │  │
  │  │ • Audience: None (any API server accepts it)                │  │
  │  │ • Pod binding: NONE (token works from anywhere)             │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Stolen token = PERMANENT cluster access                  │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Can be used from OUTSIDE the cluster                      │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Exposed in Secret objects (readable via RBAC)            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  BOUND TOKENS (TokenRequest API, USE THIS):                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ • Stored in: Projected volume (tmpfs, never in Secrets)     │  │
  │  │ • Expiry: 1 hour (default), renews automatically            │  │
  │  │ • Audience: Specific (e.g., "kubernetes.default.svc")       │  │
  │  │ • Pod binding: YES (token tied to specific pod UID)          │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ Stolen token expires in ≤1 hour                           │  │
  │  │ ✅ Token dies when pod dies                                  │  │
  │  │ ✅ Cannot be used from outside the cluster                   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: Bound tokens are now the default (since K8s 1.24). You should DISABLE legacy token auto-creation with the ServiceAccount admission plugin or by setting automountServiceAccountToken: false on the SA.

Cluster Hardening Q44 of 200
How are bound ServiceAccount tokens mounted into pods, and what is their default expiry?
A) Tokens are mounted as environment variables with 30-day expiry
B) Tokens are stored in etcd and fetched on-demand with no expiry
C) Tokens are mounted via projected volumes at the standard path. Default expiry is 1 hour (configurable up to 24h). The kubelet automatically rotates the token before expiry — the pod always has a valid token without restarting
D) Tokens are hardcoded into the container image at build time
✅ Correct Answer: C

Bound tokens use Kubernetes' projected volume mechanism. The kubelet handles token lifecycle: requesting a new token via the TokenRequest API, mounting it into the pod, and refreshing it before expiry. The pod never sees an expired token.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Projected Token Lifecycle — Automatic Rotation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TIMELINE: Token Lifecycle in a Running Pod                       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  T+0min:   Pod starts, kubelet requests TokenRequest API          │
  │            → Token issued (expiry: T+60min)                       │
  │            → Mounted at /var/run/secrets/.../token                │
  │                                                                   │
  │  T+50min:  kubelet detects token nearing expiry (10min buffer)    │
  │            → Requests NEW token via TokenRequest API               │
  │            → New token (expiry: T+110min) atomically replaces old  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  T+60min:  Old token expires — pod is already using NEW token     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  T+110min: kubelet rotates again... (infinite loop)               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CONFIGURING TOKEN EXPIRY:                                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   containers:                                               │  │
  │  │   - name: api                                              │  │
  │  │     volumeMounts:                                           │  │
  │  │     - name: sa-token                                       │  │
  │  │       mountPath: /var/run/secrets/tokens                    │  │
  │  │   volumes:                                                  │  │
  │  │   - name: sa-token                                          │  │
  │  │     projected:                                              │  │
  │  │       sources:                                              │  │
  │  │       - serviceAccountToken:                                │  │
  │  │           expirationSeconds: 3600  ← 1 hour (default)       │  │
  │  │           path: token                                        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The key security property: even if an attacker steals the token file from a running pod, the token expires in 1 hour. Combined with automountServiceAccountToken: false for pods that don't need API access, you dramatically reduce the attack surface.

Cluster Hardening Q45 of 200
How do you authenticate to a private container registry (like AWS ECR) from Kubernetes? What is an imagePullSecret?
A) Create a Secret of type kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson containing registry credentials (or AWS ECR auth token), then reference it in the pod spec as imagePullSecrets. This allows kubelet to authenticate to the private registry and pull the image. For AWS ECR, you can use kubectl create secret docker-registry or automate credential refresh
B) Private registries are automatically accessible — Kubernetes uses the node's IAM role to pull images
C) Store the registry password in the image name — Docker supports embedded credentials in the image tag
D) imagePullSecret is a kubectl plugin that downloads images to your local machine
✅ Correct Answer: A

imagePullSecrets is how Kubernetes authenticates to private registries. The Secret contains the registry URL, username, and password/auth-token. kubelet uses it when pulling images for pods that reference it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
imagePullSecrets — Private Registry Authentication
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CREATE THE SECRET:                                               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl create secret docker-registry ecr-creds -n anihpj \ │  │
  │  │   --docker-server=123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com\│  │
  │  │   --docker-username=AWS \                                    │  │
  │  │   --docker-password=$(aws ecr get-login-password) \          │  │
  │  │   --docker-email=admin@anihpj.com                            │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ Secret type: kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson                  │  │
  │  │ Data: .dockerconfigjson (base64-encoded JSON)                │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  USE IN POD:                                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: v1                                               │  │
  │  │ kind: Pod                                                    │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                        │  │
  │  │   imagePullSecrets:                                          │  │
  │  │   - name: ecr-creds  ← References the Secret                │  │
  │  │   containers:                                                │  │
  │  │   - name: api                                               │  │
  │  │     image: 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/       │  │
  │  │            anihpj-api:v2.1.0                                 │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ AWS ECR PASSWORD EXPIRES IN 12 HOURS!                        │
  │  Use a CronJob to refresh: every 8h, regenerate the Secret       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Store all images in AWS ECR (private). Create an ecr-creds Secret and reference it in every pod spec. Automate credential refresh since ECR tokens expire every 12 hours.

2.3 Kubernetes Upgrades & Vulnerability ManagementQ51-Q54

Cluster Hardening Q46 of 200
What is IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) and why is it superior to static AWS credentials in Secrets?
A) IRSA is the same as storing AWS keys in Secrets — it just uses a different API
B) IRSA maps a Kubernetes ServiceAccount to an AWS IAM Role using OIDC federation. The pod gets SHORT-LIVED AWS credentials via a projected token — no static access keys are ever stored. The credentials auto-rotate and are scoped to the specific SA. No Secret contains AWS keys to steal
C) IRSA is a Kubernetes-native replacement for AWS IAM — it works without AWS
D) IRSA creates IAM users for each pod — one IAM user per running container
✅ Correct Answer: B

IRSA eliminates static AWS credentials from your cluster entirely. Instead of storing AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID in a Secret (which can be leaked via RBAC, etcd dumps, or pod env vars), pods authenticate to AWS using their Kubernetes identity via OIDC federation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
IRSA — Pod Identity Without Static Credentials
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT IRSA (DANGEROUS):                                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Secret: aws-creds                                           │  │
  │  │   AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID=AKIA...      ← STATIC! Never expires!   │  │
  │  │   AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=...      ← If leaked → FULL ACCESS  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Anyone with "get secrets" RBAC can steal these           │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Visible in env vars: kubectl exec pod -- env             │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Persisted in etcd (encrypt Secrets at rest!)             │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WITH IRSA (SECURE):                                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ① ServiceAccount annotated with AWS IAM Role ARN:           │  │
  │  │    eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn: arn:aws:iam::123:role/S3-R  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ② Pod uses projected SA token → exchanges for AWS creds:   │  │
  │  │    AWS SDK auto-detects token → calls STS AssumeRoleWithWeb │  │
  │  │    Identity → gets TEMPORARY creds (1h expiry)              │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ③ NO static keys in Secrets, env vars, or etcd!            │  │
  │  │ ④ Creds auto-rotate, are scoped to pod, expire quickly     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost on AWS EKS: Use IRSA so the API pod can read from S3 (job attachments) and write to SQS (job processing queue) without a single static AWS credential in the cluster.

Cluster Hardening Q47 of 200
What are the cloud-native alternatives to IRSA for GCP and Azure — how do pods get cloud identity without static keys?
A) GCP and Azure don't support pod-level identity — you must use static keys in Secrets
B) All clouds use the same mechanism — IRSA is the universal standard
C) GCP Workload Identity maps a Kubernetes SA to a GCP IAM Service Account via GCP's IAM policy binding. Azure AD Workload Identity uses Azure AD Pod Identity or the newer workload identity federation. Both eliminate static keys — pods authenticate using their Kubernetes SA token via OIDC federation to the cloud IAM system
D) Cloud identity requires running a privileged daemonset on each node that proxies credentials
✅ Correct Answer: C

All three major clouds now support pod-level identity without static keys. The pattern is the same: Kubernetes SA token → OIDC federation → cloud IAM → temporary credentials. No cloud provider requires static keys anymore.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cloud Pod Identity — AWS · GCP · Azure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PROVIDER   │ MECHANISM              │ SA ANNOTATION              │
  │  ───────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────── │
  │  AWS        │ IRSA (IAM Roles for    │ eks.amazonaws.com/        │
  │             │ Service Accounts)      │   role-arn: arn:aws:...   │
  │  ───────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────── │
  │  GCP        │ Workload Identity      │ iam.gke.io/gcp-service-   │
  │             │                        │   account: sa@proj.iam... │
  │  ───────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────── │
  │  Azure      │ Workload Identity      │ azure.workload.identity/  │
  │             │ Federation             │   client-id: <APP-ID>     │
  │  ───────────┴────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────── │
  │                                                                   │
  │  COMMON PATTERN (all three):                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ 1. Annotate Kubernetes SA with cloud identity reference    │  │
  │  │ 2. Pod gets projected SA token                             │  │
  │  │ 3. Cloud SDK exchanges K8s token for cloud creds (STS)     │  │
  │  │ 4. Temporary, auto-rotating cloud credentials              │  │
  │  │ 5. NO static keys anywhere in the cluster                  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS relevance: The exam covers cloud security concepts including minimizing static credentials. Know the principle: K8s SA token + cloud IAM federation = zero static keys.

2.4 Pod Security Standards (PSS) & Pod Security Admission (PSA)Q55-Q60

Cluster Hardening Q48 of 200
Why is the ability to list Secrets via RBAC a ServiceAccount token enumeration attack vector?
A) Listing Secrets reveals ALL ServiceAccount tokens in the namespace — including tokens for SAs with higher privileges. An attacker who compromises a low-privilege pod can list Secrets, extract a high-privilege SA's token, and escalate privileges. This is why RBAC must restrict Secret access to only the specific SAs that truly need it
B) Listing Secrets only shows Secret names, not values — it's harmless from a security perspective
C) ServiceAccount tokens are encrypted inside Secrets and cannot be extracted
D) This attack only works on Kubernetes versions before 1.16 — modern clusters are immune
✅ Correct Answer: A

SA token enumeration is a classic privilege escalation path: compromise a low-privilege pod → list Secrets → find a high-privilege SA token → use it to escalate. This is why get/list/watch secrets should be one of the most tightly controlled RBAC permissions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SA Token Enumeration Attack — Privilege Escalation Path
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ATTACK CHAIN:                                                    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ① Attacker compromises anihpj-web pod (RBAC: get pods)           │
  │     → Current permissions: only "get pods" in anihpj namespace    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ② Attacker checks if they can list Secrets:                     │
  │     kubectl get secrets -n anihpj                                  │
  │     → If RBAC allows "list secrets":                              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ③ Attacker finds:                                                │
  │     NAME                       TYPE                               │
  │     db-sa-token-abc123        kubernetes.io/service-account-token │
  │     monitoring-sa-token-xyz   kubernetes.io/service-account-token │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ④ Attacker extracts monitoring-sa's token:                       │
  │     kubectl get secret monitoring-sa-token-xyz -o jsonpath=       │
  │       '{.data.token}' | base64 -d                                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⑤ Attacker checks monitoring-sa's permissions:                   │
  │     kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:         │
  │       anihpj:monitoring-sa -n anihpj                              │
  │     → monitoring-sa has: get/list pods, nodes, AND secrets!      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⑥ Attacker uses monitoring-sa token → ESCALATED ACCESS!          │
  │     → Now can read ALL Secrets, not just pods                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  PREVENTION:                                                       │
  │  • DENY "list secrets" to application SAs                         │
  │  • Use automountServiceAccountToken: false for non-API pods       │
  │  • Encrypt Secrets at rest (EncryptionConfiguration)              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The web and API SAs should NEVER have "list secrets" permission. Only the CI/CD deployment SA (which creates the pods) needs Secret access.

Cluster Hardening Q49 of 200
How should you disable the default ServiceAccount in a namespace and ensure pods use specific, scoped SAs?
A) Delete the default SA — Kubernetes will auto-create a secure replacement
B) Set automountServiceAccountToken: false on the default SA, create dedicated SAs per workload with minimal RBAC, and always explicitly set serviceAccountName in pod specs. Never rely on the default SA for application pods
C) The default SA is required — any attempt to disable it will break the namespace
D) Default SAs are automatically secure in Kubernetes 1.24+ — no action needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

The default ServiceAccount exists in every namespace. If you accidentally bind RBAC to it, ALL pods in that namespace get those permissions. Best practice: disable it and use dedicated SAs per workload.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Disabling Default SA — Secure SA per Workload
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SECURE SETUP:                                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # 1. Disable default SA token auto-mount                    │  │
  │  │ kubectl patch sa default -n anihpj -p '{"automountServiceAc│  │
  │  │   countToken": false}'                                       │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # 2. Create dedicated SAs with MINIMAL RBAC                 │  │
  │  │ kubectl create sa api-sa -n anihpj                           │  │
  │  │ kubectl create sa web-sa -n anihpj                           │  │
  │  │ kubectl create sa db-sa -n anihpj                            │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # 3. Always specify serviceAccountName in pod specs         │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                        │  │
  │  │   serviceAccountName: api-sa   ← EXPLICIT, never default    │  │
  │  │   automountServiceAccountToken: true  (only if needed)      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS tip: Audit with kubectl get pods -A -o json | jq '.items[] | select(.spec.serviceAccountName==null or .spec.serviceAccountName=="default")' to find pods relying on the default SA.

Cluster Hardening Q50 of 200
When you create a new ServiceAccount, what permissions does it have by default, and how should you minimize them?
A) New SAs get view access by default — they can read all resources in their namespace
B) New SAs get the same permissions as the namespace's default SA
C) New SAs have ZERO permissions by default. Permissions come ONLY from RoleBindings/ClusterRoleBindings. Minimize by: (1) creating the SA first, (2) ONLY binding the minimum required permissions (specific resources + specific verbs), (3) NOT binding cluster-admin or wildcard roles, (4) setting automountServiceAccountToken: false if the pod doesn't need API access at all
D) New SAs inherit all permissions from the creating user's kubeconfig
✅ Correct Answer: C

ServiceAccounts start with zero permissions — they're blank slates. The danger comes from what you bind to them AFTER creation. Always audit: kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:NS:SA -n NS to see exactly what they can do.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SA Permission Lifecycle — Start Empty, Add Sparingly
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SA LIFECYCLE — PERMISSIONS ARE OPT-IN:                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CREATED: api-sa                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl create sa api-sa -n anihpj                           │  │
  │  │ → Permissions: ZERO (nothing bound yet)                     │  │
  │  │ → kubectl auth can-i --list --as=...api-sa → EMPTY LIST     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │     │                                                             │
  │     ▼                                                             │
  │  BOUND: RoleBinding → Role: pod-reader (get, list pods)          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl create rolebinding api-pod -n anihpj \              │  │
  │  │   --role=pod-reader --serviceaccount=anihpj:api-sa          │  │
  │  │ → Permissions: get,list pods ONLY                           │  │
  │  │ → CANNOT: delete pods, list secrets, create anything       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  DANGER ZONE — WHAT NEVER TO BIND:                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ❌ cluster-admin ClusterRole → TOTAL cluster access         │  │
  │  │ ❌ verbs: ["*"] on resources: ["*"] → Wildcard access      │  │
  │  │ ❌ list secrets → Token enumeration attack vector           │  │
  │  │ ❌ create pods → Attacker-deployed malicious pods           │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ verbs: ["get","list","watch"] → Read-only is SAFEST      │  │
  │  │ ✅ Single resource types: ["pods"] not ["*"]                │  │
  │  │ ✅ Namespace-scoped: Role not ClusterRole                   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Create a dedicated SA for each component (api-sa, web-sa, db-sa), bind ONLY the minimum verbs and resources, and audit quarterly with kubectl auth can-i --list. Never use cluster-admin or wildcards for application SAs.

2.3 Kubernetes Upgrades & Vulnerability ManagementQ51-Q54

Cluster Hardening Q51 of 200
What is the correct step-by-step sequence for upgrading a Kubernetes cluster using kubeadm?
A) (1) Upgrade kubeadm on control-plane, (2) kubeadm upgrade plan, (3) kubeadm upgrade apply v1.xx, (4) Upgrade kubelet + kubectl on CP, (5) Drain each worker, (6) Upgrade kubeadm + kubelet on worker, (7) kubeadm upgrade node, (8) Uncordon worker. Repeat for each worker node. This ensures rolling upgrade with zero downtime
B) Simply run apt-get upgrade kubeadm on all nodes simultaneously — Kubernetes handles the rest
C) Delete the cluster and rebuild it — there's no in-place upgrade path for Kubernetes
D) Upgrade kubelet first on all nodes, then upgrade the API server — the order doesn't matter
✅ Correct Answer: A

The kubeadm upgrade sequence MUST be followed precisely. Control-plane first, workers after. kubeadm BEFORE kubelet. This is one of the most heavily-weighted CKS tasks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubeadm Upgrade Sequence — Step-by-Step
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTROL PLANE UPGRADE (on CP node):                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ① apt-mark unhold kubeadm && apt update                    │  │
  │  │ ② apt install -y kubeadm=1.30.x-* && apt-mark hold kubeadm│  │
  │  │ ③ kubeadm upgrade plan     ← CHECK compatibility          │  │
  │  │ ④ kubeadm upgrade apply v1.30.x   ← EXECUTE upgrade       │  │
  │  │ ⑤ apt install -y kubelet=1.30.x-* kubectl=1.30.x-*        │  │
  │  │ ⑥ systemctl daemon-reload && systemctl restart kubelet    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WORKER NODE UPGRADE (for EACH worker):                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ⑦ kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets               │  │
  │  │ ⑧ SSH to worker-1                                          │  │
  │  │ ⑨ apt install -y kubeadm=1.30.x-* kubelet=1.30.x-*        │  │
  │  │ ⑩ kubeadm upgrade node                                     │  │
  │  │ ⑪ systemctl restart kubelet                                │  │
  │  │ ⑫ kubectl uncordon worker-1                                 │  │
  │  │ ⑬ REPEAT ⑦-⑫ for worker-2, worker-3...                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ CRITICAL RULES:                                              │
  │  • kubeadm MUST be upgraded BEFORE kubelet                       │
  │  • Workers MUST be drained before upgrade (zero downtime)        │
  │  • NEVER upgrade all workers simultaneously (rolling upgrade)    │
  │  • kubelet version MUST NOT exceed API server version             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam tip: The upgrade sequence is a guaranteed task. Memorize: unhold → install kubeadm → plan → apply → install kubelet → restart. For workers: drain → install → upgrade node → restart → uncordon.

Cluster Hardening Q52 of 200
What is the Kubernetes version skew policy and why does it matter for upgrades?
A) All components must be the exact same version — any difference causes cluster failure
B) Version skew only applies to etcd — Kubernetes components can differ by any number of versions
C) The skew policy allows kubelet to be 3 versions ahead of the API server — for rolling upgrades
D) API server can be ±1 minor version from kubelet. This allows rolling upgrades: upgrade API server to v1.30, then upgrade workers one-by-one. A kubelet at v1.29 still works with API server at v1.30. This policy enables zero-downtime upgrades
✅ Correct Answer: D

The version skew policy is what makes rolling upgrades possible. kube-apiserver at v1.30 can serve kubelets at v1.29, v1.30, or v1.31 — giving you a 2-version window during upgrades.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Version Skew Policy — What's Compatible
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VERSION SKEW COMPATIBILITY MATRIX:                               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  API SERVER at v1.30 ──► CAN TALK TO:                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubelet v1.29  ✅ (1 minor behind — allowed)                │  │
  │  │ kubelet v1.30  ✅ (same version — ideal)                    │  │
  │  │ kubelet v1.31  ✅ (1 minor ahead — allowed)                 │  │
  │  │ kubelet v1.28  ❌ (2 versions behind — NOT supported)       │  │
  │  │ kubelet v1.32  ❌ (2 versions ahead — NOT supported)        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  kubelet at v1.29 ──► CAN TALK TO:                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ API server v1.28 ✅  API server v1.29 ✅  API server v1.30 ✅│  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WHY THIS MATTERS FOR UPGRADES:                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Start:   CP=v1.29  Workers=v1.29  (all same)                │  │
  │  │ Step 1:  CP=v1.30  Workers=v1.29  ✅ (1 version behind OK) │  │
  │  │ Step 2:  CP=v1.30  W1=v1.30       ✅                        │  │
  │  │ Step 3:  CP=v1.30  All=v1.30      ✅ DONE!                  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ NEVER: CP=v1.30  Workers=v1.28  ❌ 2 versions = BROKEN!  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: If asked "can you upgrade from v1.28 to v1.30 directly?", the answer is YES for the control plane (kubeadm supports skipping one minor version), but NO for kubelets on nodes — you must upgrade node-by-node respecting the ±1 skew.

Cluster Hardening Q53 of 200
A critical CVE (CVE-2024-xxxx) is announced affecting Kubernetes v1.29. Your cluster runs v1.29.5. What's the remediation path?
A) Wait for the next major release — CVEs are automatically patched in-place
B) Check the CVE advisory for the fixed version (typically v1.29.6 or v1.30.x). Plan and execute a kubeadm upgrade to the patched version during a maintenance window. If the fix is in v1.29.6, upgrade within the same minor. If fixed only in v1.30.x, perform a minor version upgrade following the skew policy. Always test in staging first
C) Manually patch the affected binary — CVEs are fixed by replacing individual files
D) CVEs in Kubernetes don't affect running clusters — they only impact new installations
✅ Correct Answer: B

CVE remediation IS cluster upgrades. Kubernetes doesn't have a "patch Tuesday" — security fixes come in point releases. Your upgrade pipeline IS your CVE response pipeline. This is why CKS emphasizes upgrade procedures.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CVE Remediation via Cluster Upgrades
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CVE RESPONSE WORKFLOW:                                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ① MONITOR: Track CVEs for your Kubernetes version                │
  │     • kubernetes.io/blog, GitHub Security Advisories              │
  │     • kube-bench (CIS), Trivy (CVE scans)                         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ② ASSESS: Check CVE severity (CVSS score)                        │
  │     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │     │ CRITICAL (9.0-10.0): Patch within 24-48 hours        │     │
  │     │ HIGH (7.0-8.9):      Patch within 1 week             │     │
  │     │ MEDIUM (4.0-6.9):    Patch within 30 days            │     │
  │     │ LOW (0.1-3.9):       Patch in next planned cycle     │     │
  │     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ③ PLAN: Find the fixed version                                   │
  │     • CVE-2024-xxxx fixed in: v1.29.6, v1.30.2, v1.31.0          │
  │     • Choose the LATEST patch in your minor version               │
  │     • Test upgrade in STAGING cluster first!                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ④ EXECUTE: kubeadm upgrade to fixed version                      │
  │     • Follow the standard upgrade sequence (Q51)                  │
  │     • Verify with: kubectl version --short                       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ NEVER: Manually replace individual binaries                  │
  │  ✅ ALWAYS: Upgrade via kubeadm (tested, reproducible)           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If a CVE affects kubelet and allows container escape, every pod running on affected nodes is at risk. Your PostgreSQL data, Django SECRET_KEY, and user PII are all exposed until you upgrade. This is why CKS makes upgrades a core competency.

Cluster Hardening Q54 of 200
Before upgrading a worker node, you must drain it. What does kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets do and why is it necessary?
A) Drain deletes the node permanently — it's the first step in decommissioning
B) Drain just reboots the node — pods restart automatically after the reboot
C) Drain EVICTS all non-DaemonSet pods from the node, respecting PodDisruptionBudgets and graceful termination (default 30s). --ignore-daemonsets skips DaemonSet pods (which would immediately reschedule anyway). After drain, the node has zero application pods — safe to upgrade/reboot. Uncordon allows pods to schedule again
D) Drain copies all pod data to another node — it's a migration tool, not an upgrade prerequisite
✅ Correct Answer: C

Drain is the graceful eviction mechanism. It respects PDBs (won't violate min-available), honors terminationGracePeriodSeconds, and ensures zero-downtime node maintenance.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Node Drain — Safe Pod Eviction Workflow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data│
  │                                                                   │
  │  WHAT DRAIN DOES (step by step):                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ① CORDS the node (no NEW pods schedule here)                │  │
  │  │ ② For EACH pod on the node:                                 │  │
  │  │    • Sends SIGTERM (graceful shutdown)                      │  │
  │  │    • Waits terminationGracePeriodSeconds (default 30s)      │  │
  │  │    • If pod doesn't stop → SIGKILL                          │  │
  │  │    • Respects PodDisruptionBudget (PDB)                     │  │
  │  │ ③ DaemonSets SKIPPED (--ignore-daemonsets)                 │  │
  │  │ ④ EmptyDir data DELETED (--delete-emptydir-data)           │  │
  │  │ ⑤ Node is now EMPTY — safe to upgrade/reboot               │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  DRAIN BLOCKERS (what can prevent drain):                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ❌ PDB with minAvailable=1 but only 1 replica running       │  │
  │  │ ❌ Pod with no controller (bare pod — won't restart)        │  │
  │  │ ❌ Pod using emptyDir with critical data                    │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ FIX: --force --disable-eviction (override PDB — caution!)   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: You WILL get a node maintenance task. The full sequence: cordon → drain → work → uncordon. Always verify with kubectl get nodes after each step.

2.4 Pod Security Standards (PSS) & Pod Security Admission (PSA)Q55-Q60

Cluster Hardening Q55 of 200
What are the three Pod Security Standards (PSS) policy levels and what does each permit?
A) Basic, Standard, Enterprise — three pricing tiers for Kubernetes security
B) Privileged: Unrestricted (allows everything — hostPath, hostNetwork, privileged containers). Baseline: Minimally restrictive (blocks known privilege escalations — no hostPath, no hostNetwork, no privileged). Restricted: Heavily restricted (must run as non-root, drop ALL capabilities, read-only root FS, no privilege escalation). These are INCREASINGLY restrictive
C) Public, Private, Hybrid — three network isolation levels for pods
D) There's only one standard — PodSecurityPolicy — which was deprecated in v1.25
✅ Correct Answer: B

PSS replaced the deprecated PodSecurityPolicy (PSP). The three levels form a progressive security ladder. For anihpj: web and API pods should target Restricted, monitoring agents may need Baseline.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSS Levels — Progressive Restriction Ladder
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PRIVILEGED             BASELINE              RESTRICTED          │
  │  (unrestricted)         (minimal block)       (heavily locked)    │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐ │
  │  │ ✅ hostPath      │   │ ❌ hostPath      │   │ ❌ hostPath      │ │
  │  │ ✅ hostNetwork   │   │ ❌ hostNetwork   │   │ ❌ hostNetwork   │ │
  │  │ ✅ hostPID/IPC   │   │ ❌ hostPID/IPC   │   │ ❌ hostPID/IPC   │ │
  │  │ ✅ privileged    │   │ ❌ privileged    │   │ ❌ privileged    │ │
  │  │ ✅ runAsRoot     │   │ ✅ runAsRoot     │   │ ❌ runAsRoot     │ │
  │  │ ✅ all caps      │   │ ✅ all caps      │   │ ❌ ALL caps drop │ │
  │  │ ✅ writable root │   │ ✅ writable root │   │ ❌ root RO       │ │
  │  │                  │   │                  │   │ ❌ priv esc      │ │
  │  │ Use: NEVER       │   │ Use: monitoring  │   │ Use: apps       │ │
  │  └─────────────────┘   └─────────────────┘   └─────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Cluster Hardening Q56 of 200
What does the Baseline PSS profile specifically block?
A) Baseline blocks known privilege escalations: no hostPath volumes, no hostNetwork/hostPID/hostIPC, no privileged containers, no hostPort ranges, no CAP_SYS_ADMIN/NET_ADMIN without explicit drops. It allows running as root and writable root filesystems — it's a MINIMAL bar, not complete hardening
B) Baseline blocks all external network access — pods can only communicate within the cluster
C) Baseline is identical to Restricted — they block the same things
D) Baseline only blocks pods from using more than 1 CPU core
✅ Correct Answer: A

Baseline is the "do no obvious harm" level. It prevents container escape via host namespace sharing but doesn't enforce non-root or read-only root. Think of it as the minimum security bar for any production namespace.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Baseline Profile — What It Blocks vs Allows
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BASELINE PROFILE — BLOCKED (known privilege escalations):        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ❌ hostPath volumes         → Container escapes to node FS  │  │
  │  │ ❌ hostNetwork: true        → Pod uses node's network stack │  │
  │  │ ❌ hostPID: true            → Pod sees ALL node processes   │  │
  │  │ ❌ hostIPC: true            → Pod accesses node's IPC       │  │
  │  │ ❌ privileged: true         → All caps, all devices, root   │  │
  │  │ ❌ CAP_SYS_ADMIN            → Near-root kernel capabilities │  │
  │  │ ❌ hostPort ranges          → Bypasses NetworkPolicy         │  │
  │  │ ❌ SELinux type overrides   → Escapes SELinux confinement   │  │
  │  │ ❌ /proc mount type changes → Procfs abuse                   │  │
  │  │ ❌ AppArmor unconfined      → No AppArmor enforcement       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  BASELINE PROFILE — STILL ALLOWED (not blocked at this level):    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ runAsUser: 0 (root)      → Allowed! (blocked in Restricted)│
  │  │ ✅ writable root filesystem → Allowed! (blocked in Restricted)│
  │  │ ✅ all Linux capabilities   → Allowed! (dropped in Restricted)│
  │  │ ✅ privilege escalation     → Allowed! (blocked in Restricted)│
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  anihpj EXAMPLE: A monitoring agent that needs hostPath to read   │
  │  node metrics would FAIL Restricted but PASS Baseline.            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Use Baseline as your STARTING point for production. Then progressively harden to Restricted. Every pod that can run under Restricted SHOULD run under Restricted.

Cluster Hardening Q57 of 200
What does the Restricted PSS profile enforce that Baseline does not?
A) Restricted adds network isolation — it's essentially a NetworkPolicy
B) Restricted only adds CPU and memory limits — it's a resource management tool
C) Restricted enforces: (1) runAsNonRoot: true (MUST run as non-root UID), (2) Drop ALL capabilities (any added capability = violation), (3) allowPrivilegeEscalation: false (no child gaining parent's privs), (4) Seccomp profile must be RuntimeDefault or Localhost (not Unconfined), (5) Volume types restricted to ConfigMap, Secret, EmptyDir, PVC, and projected volumes only
D) Restricted only applies to the kube-system namespace — user namespaces are exempt
✅ Correct Answer: C

Restricted is the "defense-in-depth" level. It's what you should aim for with ALL application pods. For anihpj/jobpost, the web, API, and Redis pods can all run under Restricted with proper configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Restricted Profile — Defense-in-Depth Hardening
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RESTRICTED = BASELINE + THESE ADDITIONAL CONTROLS:               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ① runAsNonRoot: true     ← MUST use UID > 0 (e.g., 1000)   │  │
  │  │ ② Drop ALL capabilities  ← NO cap add! (drop: [ALL])       │  │
  │  │ ③ allowPrivilegeEscalation: false ← No child gains privs   │  │
  │  │ ④ seccompProfile: RuntimeDefault or Localhost               │  │
  │  │    (NOT Unconfined!) ← Restricts syscall surface            │  │
  │  │ ⑤ Volume types limited to: ConfigMap, Secret, EmptyDir,     │  │
  │  │    PVC, DownwardAPI, Projected — NO hostPath!               │  │
  │  │ ⑥ readOnlyRootFilesystem: true (implied by default)         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  anihpj-api pod — RESTRICTED-COMPLIANT securityContext:           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ securityContext:                                             │  │
  │  │   runAsNonRoot: true                                        │  │
  │  │   runAsUser: 1000                                           │  │
  │  │   allowPrivilegeEscalation: false                            │  │
  │  │   capabilities:                                             │  │
  │  │     drop: [ALL]                                              │  │
  │  │   readOnlyRootFilesystem: true                               │  │
  │  │   seccompProfile:                                           │  │
  │  │     type: RuntimeDefault                                     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ COMMON COMPATIBILITY ISSUES:                                  │
  │  • Python apps: Need writable /tmp (use emptyDir volume)          │
  │  • Java apps: Need /tmp for JVM (emptyDir volume)                 │
  │  • Nginx: Needs writable /var/cache/nginx (emptyDir volume)      │
  │  ⚠️ PostgreSQL: CANNOT run read-only root (needs writable /var)  │
  │    → PostgreSQL must use Baseline, not Restricted                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django API and Nginx web pods run perfectly under Restricted. PostgreSQL, however, needs writable directories for its data — use Baseline with strong additional hardening (runAsNonRoot, drop ALL caps, seccomp) as a compromise.

Cluster Hardening Q58 of 200
How does Pod Security Admission (PSA) enforce PSS at the namespace level, and what are the three modes?
A) PSA is a kubectl plugin — it scans pods manually and generates reports
B) PSA replaces RBAC — it's the primary authorization mechanism in modern Kubernetes
C) PSA only works on cloud providers — self-managed clusters use PodSecurityPolicy
D) PSA is a BUILT-IN admission controller (no webhook needed!) that enforces PSS via namespace labels. Three modes: enforce (REJECT violating pods), audit (allow but LOG violations), warn (allow but WARN the user). Labels: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted
✅ Correct Answer: D

PSA is Kubernetes' BUILT-IN replacement for the deprecated PodSecurityPolicy. No external webhooks, no OPA/Gatekeeper needed — just label your namespaces.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSA Namespace Labels — Three Modes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl label namespace anihpj \                                 │
  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted \                │
  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit=restricted \                  │
  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn=restricted                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  MODE      │ BEHAVIOR                                              │
  │  ──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  enforce   │ REJECT the pod — it will NOT be created              │
  │  audit     │ ALLOW the pod, LOG the violation to audit log        │
  │  warn      │ ALLOW the pod, WARN the user (kubectl output)        │
  │  ──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │                                                                   │
  │  TYPICAL ROLLOUT:                                                 │
  │  dev:      warn=restricted     (warn devs, don't block)           │
  │  staging:  audit=restricted    (log violations, don't block)      │
  │  prod:     enforce=restricted  (BLOCK violating pods)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Cluster Hardening Q59 of 200
What are the exact PSA namespace labels and how do you apply them to enforce the Restricted profile?
A) kubectl label ns anihpj pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit=restricted pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn=restricted. Version label pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version=v1.30 can pin to a specific K8s version's PSS definition for consistency across upgrades
B) kubectl annotate ns anihpj security=restricted — PSA uses annotations, not labels
C) PSA is configured in a ConfigMap per namespace — labels are not used
D) kubectl create psa restricted --namespace anihpj — PSA has its own dedicated resource type
✅ Correct Answer: A

PSA is 100% label-driven — no YAML manifests, no webhooks, no CRDs. Just kubectl label. The version label ensures consistent PSS interpretation across cluster upgrades.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSA Labels — All Available Options
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PSA NAMESPACE LABELS (all are OPTIONAL — use what you need):     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ENFORCEMENT LEVELS:                                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: <privileged|baseline   │  │
  │  │   |restricted>          ← REJECTS violating pods            │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: <privileged|baseline     │  │
  │  │   |restricted>          ← ALLOWS pod, LOGS to audit log     │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: <privileged|baseline      │  │
  │  │   |restricted>          ← ALLOWS pod, WARNS user            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  VERSION PINNING (optional but recommended):                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: v1.30           │  │
  │  │ pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit-version: v1.30             │  │
  │  │ pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn-version: v1.30              │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ WHY PIN: PSS definitions evolve. v1.30's "restricted"      │  │
  │  │ may differ from v1.31's. Pinning ensures consistent         │  │
  │  │ behavior across cluster upgrades.                           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  QUICK APPLY (anihpj production namespace):                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl label ns anihpj \                                    │  │
  │  │   pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted \            │  │
  │  │   pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version=v1.30           │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # Check: kubectl get ns anihpj --show-labels                │  │
  │  │ # Remove: kubectl label ns anihpj \                          │  │
  │  │ #   pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-                      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: You'll likely be asked to label a namespace for PSA enforcement. Know the exact label format: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted. The trailing dash removes a label: kubectl label ns X pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-.

Cluster Hardening Q60 of 200
How should you implement PSA for anihpj/jobpost across dev, staging, and production namespaces?
A) Apply enforce=restricted to all namespaces immediately — security first, fix breakages later
B) PSA doesn't apply to application namespaces — it's only for kube-system and infrastructure
C) Progressive rollout: dev = warn:restricted (developers see warnings), staging = audit:restricted (violations logged), prod = enforce:restricted (violations BLOCKED). This allows teams to fix violations in lower environments before production enforcement. Use the version label to lock PSS behavior
D) Use enforce=baseline for all — Restricted is too strict for any real application
✅ Correct Answer: C

This is the production-tested rollout pattern. You don't jump straight to enforce — you warn, then audit, then enforce. This prevents "PSA broke my deployment!" incidents while still achieving full security compliance.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj PSA Rollout — Dev → Staging → Prod
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NAMESPACE   │ LABELS                                  │ EFFECT   │
  │  ────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┼───────── │
  │  anihpj-dev  │ warn=restricted, warn-version=v1.30      │ ⚠️ WARN  │
  │  anihpj-stg  │ audit=restricted, audit-version=v1.30    │ 📝 LOG   │
  │  anihpj-prod │ enforce=restricted, enforce-version=v1.30│ 🚫 BLOCK │
  │  ────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┴───────── │
  │                                                                   │
  │  PROGRESSION:                                                     │
  │  Week 1-2: dev=warn (developers fix violations)                   │
  │  Week 3:   staging=audit (verify before prod)                     │
  │  Week 4:   prod=enforce (FULL security enforcement)               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💻 Category 3: System Hardening (10%)Q61-Q80

🟡 Intermediate: Hardening the Node — OS, Kernel, and Runtime

This category is about hardening the NODE itself — the operating system, kernel, and container runtime. Think of it like reinforcing the foundation and walls of your fortress. Even the strongest Kubernetes RBAC is useless if an attacker can compromise the underlying node. For anihpj/jobpost, this means ensuring every node running your application stack has minimal attack surface: no unnecessary services, hardened kernel parameters, and strict container runtime security.

Q61-Q80 20 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

3.1 Minimize Host OS Footprint & Attack SurfaceQ61-Q65

System Hardening Q61 of 200
Why is minimizing the host OS footprint critical for Kubernetes node security, and what specific actions should you take?
A) Host OS hardening is optional — Kubernetes security policies cover all host-level risks
B) Every installed package is a potential attack vector. Remove unused packages (compilers, debugging tools, network clients), disable unused services (bluetooth, cups, avahi), and use a minimal OS distribution (Container-Optimized OS, Flatcar, Bottlerocket). A smaller footprint means fewer CVEs, fewer attack paths, and less maintenance burden
C) Install ALL available security packages — more security tools mean better protection
D) Only the API server node needs hardening — worker nodes are isolated by Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Minimalism is security. Every unnecessary binary on a node is a tool an attacker can use after gaining initial access. A compiler (gcc) enables compiling exploit code. wget/curl enables downloading payloads. Python enables running scripts. Remove them all.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Host OS Attack Surface — Before vs After Minimization
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BEFORE (Default Ubuntu Server — Attack Surface LARGE):           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Packages: 1,200+   Services: 45 running   CVEs: ~200        │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Attacker gains shell on node via pod escape:              │  │
  │  │    • gcc → compile privilege escalation exploit              │  │
  │  │    • wget → download rootkit from C2 server                  │  │
  │  │    • python3 → run sophisticated attack scripts             │  │
  │  │    • ssh → pivot to other nodes                             │  │
  │  │    • nc → establish reverse shell                           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  AFTER (Minimal Container-Optimized OS):                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Packages: ~150    Services: 8 running    CVEs: ~15           │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ Attacker gains shell:                                     │  │
  │  │    • NO compilers → can't compile exploits                  │  │
  │  │    • NO wget/curl → can't download payloads                 │  │
  │  │    • NO python → can't run scripts                          │  │
  │  │    • Service stripped → minimal attack surface             │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  COMMANDS TO AUDIT:                                               │
  │  apt list --installed | wc -l        (count packages)             │
  │  systemctl list-units --type=service  (count services)            │
  │  apt autoremove --purge               (remove unused deps)        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every node running PostgreSQL containers should run a minimal OS. If an attacker escapes the DB container, they should find an empty toolbox — no compilers, no downloaders, no scripting languages.

System Hardening Q62 of 200
Which kernel modules should be blacklisted on Kubernetes nodes, and why?
A) All kernel modules should be loaded — they're available but not actively used
B) Only the kubelet module matters — other kernel modules are irrelevant to container workloads
C) Blacklist unused and potentially dangerous protocols: DCCP (Datagram Congestion Control), SCTP (Stream Control Transmission), RDS (Reliable Datagram Sockets), and TIPC (Transparent Inter-Process Communication). These are rarely needed in K8s environments and each is a potential kernel exploit vector
D) Kernel module blacklisting breaks CNI plugins — all modules must be available
✅ Correct Answer: C

CIS benchmark 4.1.6 explicitly requires blacklisting these four modules. They enable obscure network protocols that are almost never needed in Kubernetes environments but provide additional kernel attack surface.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kernel Module Blacklisting — CIS 4.1.6
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BLACKLIST CONFIGURATION (/etc/modprobe.d/cis.conf):              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ install dccp /bin/true    ← Datagram Congestion Control     │  │
  │  │ install sctp /bin/true    ← Stream Control Transmission     │  │
  │  │ install rds  /bin/true    ← Reliable Datagram Sockets      │  │
  │  │ install tipc /bin/true    ← Transparent Inter-Process Comm  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  VERIFY:                                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ lsmod | grep -E "dccp|sctp|rds|tipc"  ← Should be EMPTY    │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # If any are loaded, remove immediately:                     │  │
  │  │ sudo rmmod dccp sctp rds tipc                               │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WHY THESE FOUR? They are:                                        │
  │  • Rarely needed by K8s workloads (no container uses DCCP)       │
  │  • Historically vulnerable (CVE history in each)                 │
  │  • Each adds kernel code that runs with FULL KERNEL PRIVILEGES   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam tip: CIS check 4.1.6 is frequently tested. Know the four module names and the config file path: /etc/modprobe.d/cis.conf.

System Hardening Q63 of 200
Which Linux services should be disabled on Kubernetes nodes to reduce attack surface?
A) Disable non-essential services: bluetooth (wireless attack vector), cups (print service — never needed on servers), avahi-daemon (mDNS/Bonjour — network discovery), sendmail/postfix (mail transport — unless specifically needed). Verify with systemctl list-units --state=running
B) Only stop services — Kubernetes will restart them if they're needed by containers
C) Services are containerized by default — no host-level services exist on modern Linux
D) Disabling services breaks kubelet — all systemd services are required for Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Default Linux distributions ship with dozens of network-facing services. On a Kubernetes node, only kubelet, containerd/CRI-O, and SSH (key-only) should be running. Everything else is attack surface.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Audit — Before vs After Hardening
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMMANDS TO DISABLE UNNECESSARY SERVICES:                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ sudo systemctl stop bluetooth && sudo systemctl disable     │  │
  │  │ sudo systemctl stop cups && sudo systemctl disable          │  │
  │  │ sudo systemctl stop avahi-daemon && sudo systemctl disable  │  │
  │  │ sudo systemctl stop sendmail && sudo systemctl disable      │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # VERIFY: Only critical services remain                     │  │
  │  │ systemctl list-units --type=service --state=running         │  │
  │  │ → kubelet, containerd, sshd (key-only), systemd-journald    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  REQUIRED SERVICES (DO NOT DISABLE):                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ kubelet       — Node agent (heart of K8s)                 │  │
  │  │ ✅ containerd    — Container runtime                         │  │
  │  │ ✅ sshd          — Remote access (key-only auth)             │  │
  │  │ ✅ systemd-journald — Logging                               │  │
  │  │ ✅ chronyd/ntpd  — Time sync (critical for certs)           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
System Hardening Q64 of 200
What is the "cattle, not pets" philosophy for Kubernetes nodes, and how does immutable infrastructure improve security?
A) It means nodes should have unique names — each node is individually configured and maintained
B) It's just a marketing slogan with no practical security implications
C) It means you should physically label servers with cattle ear tags for inventory management
D) Nodes should be TREATED AS DISPOSABLE. When a node has a security issue, don't SSH in and manually patch — TERMINATE it and REPLACE with a fresh, hardened image. This eliminates configuration drift, undetected compromises, and "snowflake" servers. Immutable infrastructure means the running node is NEVER modified after creation
✅ Correct Answer: D

Immutable infrastructure is a paradigm shift: instead of patching servers in-place (creating drift and hiding compromises), you replace them entirely. The new node comes from a known-good, hardened image.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pets vs Cattle — Security Implications
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PETS (Mutable — DANGEROUS):                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Node "anih-db-01": Running for 2 years                       │  │
  │  │ • 47 manual patches applied                                  │  │
  │  │ • 3 different admins SSH'd in and "fixed things"             │  │
  │  │ • Unknown: Was it compromised 6 months ago?                  │  │
  │  │ • Configuration drift from baseline                          │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ If compromised, you LOSE the forensic trail               │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Can NEVER be certain of its security state                │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CATTLE (Immutable — SECURE):                                     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Node "worker-7f3a2": Running for 2 weeks                     │  │
  │  │ • Built from hardened golden image (Packer/AMI)              │  │
  │  │ • NEVER SSH'd into after creation                            │  │
  │  │ • Any issue → TERMINATE → Auto-replaced by autoscaler       │  │
  │  │ • Security patch → New AMI → Rolling replace (zero downtime) │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ KNOWN state at all times                                  │  │
  │  │ ✅ Compromise contained to single node lifespan              │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Packer to build a hardened AMI (AWS) or image (GCP). When a CVE is patched, build a new image and do a rolling node replacement — don't SSH in and apt-get upgrade.

System Hardening Q65 of 200
How does container base image selection affect system hardening? Compare distroless, scratch, and alpine.
A) All base images are equally secure — the application code determines security
B) Scratch (0 packages, 0 CVEs — but no shell, no libc). Distroless (~2 packages, ~2 CVEs — has libc but no shell/package manager). Alpine (~15 packages, ~0 CVEs but musl libc compatibility issues). Ubuntu (~200+ packages, ~200+ CVEs — full OS). Smaller base = fewer CVEs = smaller attack surface. This is SYSTEM HARDENING applied to containers
C) Ubuntu is always best because it has the most security patches available
D) Base image selection is only about image size — it has no security implications
✅ Correct Answer: B

Every package in your base image is a potential CVE. Distroless images reduce the attack surface by 99% compared to full OS images. No shell means no interactive access if compromised.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Base Image Comparison — CVEs vs Functionality
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IMAGE       │ SIZE   │ PACKAGES │ CVEs  │ SHELL? │ USE CASE     │
  │  ────────────┼────────┼──────────┼───────┼────────┼───────────── │
  │  Scratch     │ 0 MB   │ 0        │ 0     │ ❌     │ Go/Rust bins │
  │  Distroless  │ ~20 MB │ ~2       │ ~2    │ ❌     │ Python/Node  │
  │  Alpine      │ ~5 MB  │ ~15      │ ~0*   │ ✅     │ General use  │
  │  Ubuntu      │ ~77 MB │ ~200     │ ~200+ │ ✅     │ Legacy apps  │
  │  ────────────┴────────┴──────────┴───────┴────────┴───────────── │
  │                                                                   │
  │  anihpj/jobpost RECOMMENDATION:                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ web (nginx):  FROM nginx:alpine (small, secure)             │  │
  │  │ api (Django): FROM python:3.12-slim AS builder → distroless│  │
  │  │ db (PG):      FROM postgres:15-alpine (official, minimal)   │  │
  │  │ redis:        FROM redis:7-alpine (official, minimal)       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  * Alpine had ~0 CRITICAL CVEs at scan time but musl libc can    │
  │    cause compatibility issues with some Python C extensions      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS relevance: Base image selection directly impacts your CVE count. Trivy scan results for ubuntu:latest vs gcr.io/distroless/python3 will show 200+ vs 2 CVEs. This is a core supply chain + system hardening concept.

3.2 Least-Privilege Identity & AccessQ66-Q70

System Hardening Q66 of 200
How should SSH access to Kubernetes nodes be secured?
A) Use password authentication with complex passwords — it's more convenient for administrators
B) SSH security is handled by the cloud provider — no configuration is needed on self-managed nodes
C) Disable password authentication completely (SSH keys only). Use a bastion host as the single entry point — no direct SSH to worker nodes. Configure PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no, MaxAuthTries 3. Audit all SSH sessions with auditd rules on /usr/sbin/sshd
D) SSH should be disabled entirely — Kubernetes nodes don't need remote access
✅ Correct Answer: C

SSH is the primary administrative access path to nodes. If compromised, an attacker gets shell access to the host OS — bypassing all Kubernetes security controls. Hardening SSH is a foundational security measure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SSH Hardening — Bastion Host Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SECURE SSH ARCHITECTURE:                                         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Admin ──SSH(key)──► Bastion Host ──SSH(key)──► Worker Nodes     │
  │  (laptop)           (single entry)               (no direct access)│
  │                                                                   │
  │  /etc/ssh/sshd_config (on ALL nodes):                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ PermitRootLogin no          ← NEVER allow root SSH         │  │
  │  │ PasswordAuthentication no   ← KEYS ONLY, never passwords   │  │
  │  │ PubkeyAuthentication yes    ← Require SSH key              │  │
  │  │ MaxAuthTries 3              ← Limit brute force attempts   │  │
  │  │ ClientAliveInterval 300     ← Timeout idle connections     │  │
  │  │ AllowUsers admin@10.0.0.0/8 ← Restrict source IPs          │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  AUDIT SSH ACCESS:                                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # /etc/audit/rules.d/ssh.rules                              │  │
  │  │ -w /usr/sbin/sshd -p x -k sshd_execution                   │  │
  │  │ -w /var/log/auth.log -p wa -k ssh_auth                     │  │
  │  │ → Logs EVERY SSH login attempt                             │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
System Hardening Q67 of 200
What flags secure the kubelet API (port 10250) as part of system hardening?
A) --anonymous-auth=false (no unauthenticated access) and --authorization-mode=Webhook (delegate authz to API server). Combined with --read-only-port=0 (disable insecure read-only port 10255). These ensure only authenticated, authorized requests reach the kubelet
B) The kubelet is secured by the API server — no separate kubelet configuration is needed
C) Kubelet security is configured via a ConfigMap in the kube-system namespace
D) --anonymous-auth=true is the secure default — it requires explicit RBAC
✅ Correct Answer: A

The kubelet API is a powerful endpoint — it allows exec, logs, and metrics. Without these flags, anyone who can reach port 10250 can execute commands in any pod. This is CIS check 4.1.1.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubelet API Security — Critical Ports
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUBELET PORTS — SECURE BOTH:                                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  PORT 10250 (HTTPS — MUST SECURE):                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ --anonymous-auth=false           ← CIS 4.1.1               │  │
  │  │ --authorization-mode=Webhook     ← CIS 4.1.2               │  │
  │  │ --authentication-token-webhook=true                          │  │
  │  │ → Only authenticated API server requests allowed            │  │

  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  PORT 10255 (HTTP READ-ONLY — DISABLE):                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ --read-only-port=0               ← CIS 4.1.3               │  │
  │  │ → Completely disables insecure read-only port              │  │
  │  │ → curl http://node:10255/stats → Connection refused        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │

System Hardening Q68 of 200
How do you enable and verify TLS on the kubelet, ensuring certificate rotation?
A) Kubelet TLS is auto-configured by kubeadm — no manual configuration is possible
B) Configure --tls-cert-file and --tls-private-key-file in kubelet config. Enable automatic rotation: --rotate-server-certificates=true and feature gate RotateKubeletServerCertificate=true. Verify with openssl s_client -connect node:10250 and kubeadm certs check-expiration
C) Kubelet TLS uses the same certificates as the API server — no separate configuration
D) TLS is only needed for external-facing services — internal kubelet traffic can use HTTP
✅ Correct Answer: B

Every kubelet MUST serve HTTPS with a valid certificate. Without TLS, kubelet traffic (including pod logs and exec commands) travels in plaintext — readable by anyone on the network.

  │  │ # Check certificate expiry                                  │  │

  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │

  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml                              │  │
