System Hardening Q69 of 200
How should network access to etcd be restricted as part of system hardening?
A) etcd is automatically isolated — it only listens on localhost by default
B) etcd security is handled entirely by its TLS configuration — firewall rules are redundant
C) Restrict etcd access at MULTIPLE layers: (1) etcd binds only to localhost (--listen-client-urls=https://127.0.0.1:2379), (2) Host firewall (iptables/nftables) blocks port 2379/2380 from all except API server IPs, (3) NetworkPolicy allows only API server pods to reach etcd ports. Defense-in-depth: even if TLS is misconfigured, the firewall blocks the connection
D) Expose etcd on a public load balancer for high availability — TLS handles security
✅ Correct Answer: C

etcd is the crown jewels — it contains ALL cluster state. Defense-in-depth means layering controls: localhost binding + host firewall + Kubernetes NetworkPolicy. If any layer fails, the others still protect.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd Access Control — Defense in Depth
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LAYER 1: etcd BINDING (only API server can connect):            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ --listen-client-urls=https://127.0.0.1:2379                 │  │
  │  │ → etcd listens ONLY on localhost — NOT on network interface│  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  LAYER 2: HOST FIREWALL (iptables/nftables):                     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # Only allow API server IPs to etcd ports                   │  │
  │  │ iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 2379 -s 10.0.1.10 -j ACCEPT│  │
  │  │ iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 2379 -j DROP               │  │
  │  │ iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 2380 -s 10.0.1.10 -j ACCEPT│  │
  │  │ iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 2380 -j DROP               │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  LAYER 3: NetworkPolicy (if etcd runs as pods):                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Only kube-system API server pods can reach etcd:2379        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
System Hardening Q70 of 200
What ports MUST be open on a Kubernetes worker node, and what should be explicitly blocked?
A) Open all ports for maximum compatibility — Kubernetes handles security at the application layer
B) ALLOW only required ports: kubelet (10250), NodePort range (30000-32767), and SSH (22 - key only). BLOCK everything else. Explicitly block etcd ports (2379-2380) on workers (only CP nodes need them). Use a default-deny firewall policy with explicit allows
C) The only port that matters is 6443 (API server) — all other ports are internal
D) Node firewall is the cloud provider's responsibility — no configuration is needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

A default-deny firewall on every node is a fundamental system hardening measure. Only the ports your cluster actually uses should be open — everything else is attack surface.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Node Firewall — Required Ports
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTROL PLANE NODES:                                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ALLOW: 6443 (API), 2379-2380 (etcd), 10250 (kubelet),      │  │
  │  │        10257 (kube-controller), 10259 (kube-scheduler), 22 │  │
  │  │ BLOCK: EVERYTHING ELSE (default-deny)                       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WORKER NODES:                                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ALLOW: 10250 (kubelet), 30000-32767 (NodePorts), 22 (SSH)  │  │
  │  │ BLOCK: 2379-2380 (etcd — workers should NEVER access!),    │  │
  │  │        6443 (API — pods use Service IP, not direct)        │  │
  │  │        EVERYTHING ELSE                                      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  QUICK FIREWALL AUDIT:                                            │
  │  sudo iptables -L -n -v              (current rules)              │
  │  sudo ss -tlnp                        (listening ports)           │
  │  nmap -p- worker-1                    (port scan from external)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3.3 Kernel Hardening: AppArmor, seccomp & SELinuxQ71-Q76

System Hardening Q71 of 200
What are Linux Security Modules (LSMs) — seccomp, AppArmor, and SELinux — and how do they differ?
A) They are all the same thing — just different names for the same kernel feature
B) LSMs are user-space tools and don't affect container security
C) Only SELinux matters — AppArmor and seccomp are legacy and should not be used
D) seccomp restricts SYSCALLS (what a process can ask the kernel to do — a syscall firewall). AppArmor restricts FILE PATHS and capabilities per-program. SELinux provides mandatory access control with LABEL-based policies. They operate at different layers and can be used TOGETHER for defense-in-depth
✅ Correct Answer: D

These three LSMs form a layered defense system. seccomp filters syscalls (what you can DO), AppArmor restricts files (what you can ACCESS), and SELinux enforces mandatory policies (what you ARE allowed to do based on labels).

📝 Detailed Explanation
LSM Stack — Three Layers of Kernel Protection
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTAINER PROCESS → SYSCALL → KERNEL:                            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ① seccomp (syscall firewall — BLOCKS syscalls):                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ open("/etc/shadow")  →  Is open() in allowed list?         │  │
  │  │ clone()              →  Is clone() allowed?  ❌ BLOCKED!   │  │
  │  │ mount()              →  Is mount() allowed?  ❌ BLOCKED!   │  │
  │  │ Scope: WHICH syscalls are permitted                        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ② AppArmor (path-based MAC — RESTRICTS file access):            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Profile: docker-default                                      │  │
  │  │ /tmp/**       rw,   ← Can read/write /tmp                   │  │
  │  │ /etc/ssl/**   r,    ← Can read SSL certs                    │  │
  │  │ /proc/sys/**  denied ← CANNOT touch kernel params!          │  │
  │  │ Scope: WHICH files/paths are accessible                     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ③ SELinux (label-based MAC — ENFORCES policy):                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Container label: system_u:system_r:container_t:s0:c1,c2    │  │
  │  │ File label:      system_u:object_r:container_file_t:s0     │  │
  │  │ → container_t can READ container_file_t (policy allows)    │  │
  │  │ → container_t CANNOT access etc_t (policy denies)          │  │
  │  │ Scope: WHICH labels can interact                            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: Know the differences. seccomp restricts syscalls, AppArmor restricts files, SELinux enforces label-based policies. They work TOGETHER and should all be enabled on production nodes.

System Hardening Q72 of 200
What is seccomp (Secure Computing Mode) and how does it protect containers?
A) seccomp is a network firewall that blocks unauthorized pod-to-pod communication
B) seccomp is a Linux kernel feature that acts as a SYSCALL FILTER. It defines which system calls a process (container) is allowed to make. If a container tries to call a blocked syscall (e.g., mount, reboot, kexec_load), the kernel terminates the process with SIGSYS. This is the first and strongest line of kernel defense
C) seccomp is a Kubernetes admission controller that validates pod security contexts
D) seccomp only works on Ubuntu — it's not available on other Linux distributions
✅ Correct Answer: B

seccomp is the FIRST filter a syscall encounters. If seccomp blocks it, the syscall never reaches AppArmor, SELinux, or the kernel — it's killed at the gate. This is the most efficient and most fundamental container security control.

📝 Detailed Explanation
seccomp — The Syscall Gatekeeper
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT seccomp (container can make ~300+ syscalls):             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ open(), read(), write(), close()  ← Normal operations       │  │
  │  │ mount()  ← Mount filesystems (container escape!)            │  │
  │  │ reboot() ← Reboot the host!                                 │  │
  │  │ kexec_load() ← Load new kernel!                             │  │
  │  │ clone()  ← Create new processes (privilege escalation!)     │  │
  │  │ → ALL ~300 syscalls AVAILABLE to attacker                   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WITH seccomp RuntimeDefault (~40 risky syscalls BLOCKED):        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ open(), read(), write(), close()  ← ✅ Allowed              │  │
  │  │ mount()  → SIGSYS ❌ (kills process!)                       │  │
  │  │ reboot() → SIGSYS ❌                                        │  │
  │  │ kexec_load() → SIGSYS ❌                                    │  │
  │  │ clone()  → SIGSYS ❌ (unprivileged clone blocked)           │  │
  │  │ → Attack surface REDUCED by ~40 dangerous syscalls          │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Apply seccomp RuntimeDefault to EVERY container. The API pod doesn't need mount or reboot — block them. This prevents the most common container escape techniques.

System Hardening Q73 of 200
What is the difference between RuntimeDefault and custom seccomp profiles?
A) There is no difference — RuntimeDefault IS a custom profile
B) RuntimeDefault blocks ALL syscalls; custom profiles allow selective syscalls
C) RuntimeDefault is a pre-built profile provided by the container runtime (Docker/containerd) that blocks ~40 known-dangerous syscalls. Custom profiles are hand-written JSON files defining exactly which syscalls to allow/block using Berkeley Packet Filter (BPF) rules — you can be MORE restrictive (e.g., block ptrace) or MORE permissive (e.g., allow clone for specific use cases)
D) Custom profiles are deprecated — only RuntimeDefault should be used in modern Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: C

RuntimeDefault is your starting point — it blocks the most obvious attack syscalls with zero configuration. Custom profiles give you fine-grained control when you need it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
seccomp Profile Levels
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  INCREASING SECURITY →                                            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Unconfined        RuntimeDefault       Custom Profile           │
  │  (ALL syscalls)    (~40 blocked)        (YOU define what's OK)   │
  │  ┌────────────┐   ┌────────────────┐   ┌────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ALL 300+   │   │ ✅ open,read,  │   │ {                   │   │
  │  │ syscalls   │   │    write,close │   │   "defaultAction":  │   │
  │  │ available  │   │    ... (260)   │   │     "SCMP_ACT_ERRNO",│   │
  │  │            │   │ ❌ mount       │   │   "syscalls": [     │   │
  │  │ ⚠️ NEVER   │   │ ❌ reboot      │   │     {"name":"open", │   │
  │  │ in prod    │   │ ❌ kexec_load  │   │      "action":"ALLOW"}│   │
  │  │            │   │ ❌ ptrace      │   │     ...              │   │
  │  │            │   │ ❌ clone (unpriv)│  │   ]                 │   │
  │  └────────────┘   └────────────────┘   │ }                   │   │
  │                                         └────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  KUBERNETES POD CONFIG:                                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ securityContext:                                             │  │
  │  │   seccompProfile:                                           │  │
  │  │     type: RuntimeDefault  ← BEST for most workloads        │  │
  │  │     # OR type: Localhost, localhostProfile: my-profile.json│  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
System Hardening Q74 of 200
What is AppArmor and how do you use it to restrict containers in Kubernetes?
A) AppArmor is a Linux Security Module providing PATH-BASED mandatory access control. Create profiles in /etc/apparmor.d/ that define which files and capabilities a program can access. In K8s, reference via annotation: container.apparmor.security.beta.kubernetes.io/<container>: localhost/<profile>. Modes: enforce (block violations), complain (log only)
B) AppArmor is a Kubernetes-built security tool — it doesn't require host-level configuration
C) AppArmor and SELinux are mutually exclusive — you can only use one per node
D) AppArmor only controls network access — it doesn't restrict file access
✅ Correct Answer: A

AppArmor is Ubuntu/Debian's primary LSM. It uses path-based profiles to restrict what files, capabilities, and network operations a process can access.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AppArmor in Kubernetes — Profile Management
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CREATE PROFILE ON NODE (/etc/apparmor.d/deny-write):            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ #include <tunables/global>                                  │  │
  │  │ profile deny-write flags=(attach_disconnected) {            │  │
  │  │   #include <abstractions/base>                              │  │
  │  │   /bin/**       r,           ← Read binaries OK            │  │
  │  │   /usr/bin/**   r,                                         │  │
  │  │   /etc/**       r,           ← Read configs OK             │  │
  │  │   /tmp/**       rw,          ← Write to /tmp OK            │  │
  │  │   deny /etc/passwd w,        ← BLOCK writing to passwd!    │  │
  │  │   deny /proc/sys/** w,       ← BLOCK kernel param changes  │  │
  │  │ }                                                           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  LOAD & APPLY:                                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ sudo apparmor_parser -r /etc/apparmor.d/deny-write          │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # K8s pod annotation:                                       │  │
  │  │ metadata:                                                    │  │
  │  │   annotations:                                               │  │
  │  │     container.apparmor.security.beta.kubernetes.io/         │  │
  │  │       api: localhost/deny-write                              │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
System Hardening Q75 of 200
What is SELinux and how does it use contexts (user, role, type, level) for mandatory access control?
A) SELinux is a firewall solution — it only controls network port access
B) SELinux uses LABEL-BASED mandatory access control. Every process and file gets a context label: system_u:object_r:container_file_t:s0. The policy defines which types can interact. Unlike AppArmor (path-based) and seccomp (syscall-based), SELinux enforces based on CONTEXT LABELS — even if a file moves or a process changes name, the LABEL controls access
C) SELinux is a deprecated technology — it's been replaced by AppArmor in all modern distributions
D) SELinux only works on Red Hat systems — it cannot run on Ubuntu or Debian
✅ Correct Answer: B

SELinux is the most sophisticated of the three LSMs. Its label-based approach means access control follows the DATA, not the path. Even if a file is moved or renamed, its SELinux context follows it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SELinux Context Structure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SELINUX CONTEXT: user:role:type:level                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ system_u:system_r:container_t:s0:c123,c456                  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ user:  system_u  (SELinux user — fixed for system processes)│  │
  │  │ role:  system_r  (SELinux role)                              │  │
  │  │ type:  container_t (DOMAIN — this is what policies check!)   │  │
  │  │ level: s0:c123,c456 (MLS/MCS sensitivity + categories)       │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ POLICY RULE EXAMPLE:                                         │  │
  │  │ allow container_t container_file_t:file read;                │  │
  │  │ → "Processes in container_t can READ files with              │  │
  │  │    container_file_t type"                                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WHY LABELS > PATHS:                                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ AppArmor: /usr/bin/myapp r,  ← Tied to PATH                 │  │
  │  │   If file moves to /opt/myapp → PROFILE BROKEN              │  │
  │  │ SELinux:  type bin_t can execute type usr_t                  │  │
  │  │   File can be ANYWHERE → LABEL controls access              │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

RHEL/CentOS/Fedora use SELinux as the default LSM. Ubuntu/Debian use AppArmor. For CKS, know both but focus on whichever your target distribution uses.

System Hardening Q76 of 200
Which kernel parameters should be hardened via /etc/sysctl.d/ on Kubernetes nodes?
A) Kernel parameters are managed by the container runtime — manual configuration breaks Kubernetes
B) Only net.ipv4.ip_forward=1 matters — that's the only kernel parameter Kubernetes needs
C) Harden key parameters: net.ipv4.ip_forward=1 (required for pod networking), kernel.randomize_va_space=2 (ASLR — address space layout randomization), net.ipv4.tcp_syncookies=1 (SYN flood protection), net.ipv4.conf.all.rp_filter=1 (anti-spoofing), kernel.kptr_restrict=2 (hide kernel pointers). Place in /etc/sysctl.d/99-kubernetes-cis.conf
D) Set all to maximum security values — performance is secondary to security
✅ Correct Answer: C

CIS benchmarks specify several kernel parameters that must be hardened. These protect against common network attacks (SYN floods, IP spoofing) and make memory exploitation harder (ASLR, pointer hiding).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kernel Hardening — Critical sysctl Parameters
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  /etc/sysctl.d/99-kubernetes-cis.conf:                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # NETWORK HARDENING                                         │  │
  │  │ net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1          ← Required for pod net    │  │
  │  │ net.ipv4.tcp_syncookies = 1      ← SYN flood protection    │  │
  │  │ net.ipv4.conf.all.rp_filter = 1  ← Anti-spoofing           │  │
  │  │ net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects = 0  ← Block ICMP redir│  │
  │  │ net.ipv4.conf.all.send_redirects = 0   ← Don't send redirs│  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # MEMORY HARDENING                                           │  │
  │  │ kernel.randomize_va_space = 2     ← Full ASLR              │  │
  │  │ kernel.kptr_restrict = 2          ← Hide kernel pointers   │  │
  │  │ kernel.dmesg_restrict = 1         ← Restrict dmesg access  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # APPLY                                                      │  │
  │  │ sudo sysctl --system              ← Apply all sysctl.d/*    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3.4 Runtime & Container SecurityQ77-Q80

System Hardening Q77 of 200
Compare container runtimes — containerd, CRI-O, gVisor, and Kata Containers. How do they differ in security?
A) All runtimes are functionally identical — they all run containers the same way
B) containerd/CRI-O: Standard OCI runtimes, containers share the HOST KERNEL (good performance, weaker isolation). gVisor: User-space kernel intercepts syscalls — containers see a FAKE kernel, never touch the real one (better isolation). Kata Containers: Each pod gets its own lightweight VM with its OWN kernel — strongest isolation, highest overhead. For CKS: use standard runtimes for trusted workloads, gVisor/Kata for untrusted/multi-tenant
C) gVisor and Kata are deprecated — they've been replaced by containerd's built-in sandboxing
D) Runtime selection is a developer preference — it has no security implications
✅ Correct Answer: B

Runtime selection is a fundamental security decision. Each level trades performance for isolation. For anihpj/jobpost: use containerd for API/web pods (trusted), gVisor for user-uploaded file processing (untrusted).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Runtime Isolation Spectrum
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ISOLATION     │ RUNTIME    │ KERNEL       │ PERF   │ USE CASE   │
  │  ──────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┼────────┼─────────── │
  │  LOW           │ containerd │ SHARED host  │ 100%   │ Trusted    │
  │                │ CRI-O      │ kernel       │        │ workloads  │
  │  ──────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┼────────┼─────────── │
  │  MEDIUM        │ gVisor     │ FAKE kernel  │ ~85%   │ Untrusted  │
  │                │ (runsc)    │ (Sentry)     │        │ tenants    │
  │  ──────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┼────────┼─────────── │
  │  HIGH          │ Kata       │ OWN kernel   │ ~70%   │ Highly     │
  │                │ Containers │ per pod (VM) │        │ sensitive  │
  │  ──────────────┴────────────┴──────────────┴────────┴─────────── │
  │                                                                   │
  │  KUBERNETES RuntimeClass:                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: node.k8s.io/v1                                  │  │
  │  │ kind: RuntimeClass                                          │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: gvisor}                                    │  │
  │  │ handler: runsc                                              │  │
  │  │ → Pod spec: runtimeClassName: gvisor                        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
System Hardening Q78 of 200
How does RuntimeClass allow you to select different container runtimes per pod?
A) RuntimeClass is deprecated — all pods must use the same runtime
B) RuntimeClass is a kubectl plugin that converts pod specs between runtimes
C) RuntimeClass selects the CNI plugin — it doesn't affect the container runtime
D) RuntimeClass is a cluster-scoped resource that maps a NAME (e.g., "gvisor") to a runtime HANDLER (e.g., "runsc"). Pods specify runtimeClassName: gvisor in their spec to use that handler. This enables a SINGLE cluster to run both trusted pods (runc) and untrusted pods (gVisor/Kata) simultaneously
✅ Correct Answer: D

RuntimeClass enables mixed-trust workloads on the same cluster. Your API pods run with containerd (fast, trusted), while user-uploaded file processing runs with gVisor (isolated, untrusted). This is a core CKS multi-tenancy pattern.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RuntimeClass — Mixed-Trust Pod Scheduling
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CLUSTER WITH MULTIPLE RUNTIMES:                                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # 1. Install multiple runtimes on each node:                │  │
  │  │ sudo apt install containerd          ← default (runc)      │  │
  │  │ sudo apt install runsc               ← gVisor              │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # 2. Configure containerd to recognize both:                │  │
  │  │ # /etc/containerd/config.toml                               │  │
  │  │ [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes]  │  │
  │  │   [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes │  │
  │  │     .runc]            ← Standard runtime                    │  │
  │  │   [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes │  │
  │  │     .runsc]           ← gVisor runtime                      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # 3. Create RuntimeClass resources:                         │  │
  │  │ apiVersion: node.k8s.io/v1                                  │  │
  │  │ kind: RuntimeClass                                          │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: gvisor}                                    │  │
  │  │ handler: runsc                                              │  │
  │  │ ---                                                         │  │
  │  │ apiVersion: node.k8s.io/v1                                  │  │
  │  │ kind: RuntimeClass                                          │  │
  │  │ metadata: {name: kata}                                      │  │
  │  │ handler: kata                                               │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # 4. Pod spec selects runtime:                              │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   runtimeClassName: gvisor   ← Uses gVisor for isolation   │  │
  │  │   containers:                                               │  │
  │  │   - name: file-processor                                   │  │
  │  │     image: anihpj/file-processor                            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  anihpj DEPLOYMENT STRATEGY:                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ api (Django):      runtimeClassName: (default = runc)      │  │
  │  │ web (Nginx):       runtimeClassName: (default = runc)      │  │
  │  │ db (PostgreSQL):   runtimeClassName: (default = runc)      │  │
  │  │ file-upload:       runtimeClassName: gvisor  ← UNTRUSTED!  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS relevance: RuntimeClass is the mechanism for implementing pod-level sandboxing. For user-uploaded content (resumes, images), always use gVisor or Kata. The CKS exam tests your ability to configure RuntimeClass and select appropriate runtimes for different trust levels.

System Hardening Q79 of 200
Why are privileged containers extremely dangerous, and what do they grant access to?
A) Privileged containers are just a label — they don't actually grant additional access
B) Privileged containers are required for all production workloads for optimal performance
C) A privileged container runs with privileged: true in its securityContext, which grants: ALL Linux capabilities, access to ALL host devices (/dev/*), disables seccomp/AppArmor/SELinux restrictions, and effectively runs as root on the HOST. A compromised privileged container = full host compromise. NEVER use in production unless absolutely required (e.g., CNI plugins, CSI drivers)
D) Privileged containers are safer than regular containers because they have more security features enabled
✅ Correct Answer: C

Privileged containers bypass EVERY security control. They can access host devices, load kernel modules, and escape to the host OS trivially. They should only exist in kube-system for infrastructure components.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Privileged Container — What It Grants
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  REGULAR CONTAINER (Restricted PSS):                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ runAsNonRoot: true (UID 1000)                             │  │
  │  │ ✅ capabilities: drop [ALL]                                  │  │
  │  │ ✅ readOnlyRootFilesystem: true                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ seccomp: RuntimeDefault                                   │  │
  │  │ ❌ /dev host devices: BLOCKED                                │  │
  │  │ ❌ Host PID namespace: BLOCKED                               │  │
  │  │ → Container escape: VERY DIFFICULT                          │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  PRIVILEGED CONTAINER (NEVER in production):                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ❌ Runs as root (UID 0)                                      │  │
  │  │ ❌ ALL capabilities (CAP_SYS_ADMIN, CAP_NET_ADMIN, ...)     │  │
  │  │ ❌ seccomp: DISABLED                                         │  │
  │  │ ❌ AppArmor: DISABLED                                        │  │
  │  │ ✅ ALL /dev host devices: ACCESSIBLE                         │  │
  │  │ ✅ Host PID namespace: VISIBLE                               │  │
  │  │ → Container escape: TRIVIAL — chroot to host FS             │  │
  │  │ → mount /dev/sda1 /mnt → FULL host filesystem access!       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS critical: PSA Restricted explicitly blocks privileged containers. Audit with: kubectl get pods -A -o json | jq '.items[] | select(.spec.containers[].securityContext.privileged==true) | .metadata.name'

System Hardening Q80 of 200
How do you run CIS benchmark scans specifically for worker nodes using kube-bench?
A) Run kube-bench --targets node which executes 30+ CIS checks on the worker node: kubelet configuration, file permissions, kernel parameters, and service status. Focus areas: anonymous-auth disabled, authorization-mode Webhook, kernel modules blacklisted, sysctl parameters hardened. Output shows PASS/FAIL/WARN for each CIS check ID
B) CIS scans only work on control plane nodes — worker nodes don't have scannable components
C) Use kubectl describe node — it includes built-in CIS compliance reporting
D) Worker node CIS compliance is verified by running kube-bench from a pod in the kube-system namespace
✅ Correct Answer: A

kube-bench with --targets node focuses specifically on worker node security. This is distinct from --targets master (control plane) and --targets etcd (etcd-specific).

📝 Detailed Explanation
CIS Worker Node Scan — Key Checks
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │                                                                   │

🛡️ Category 4: Minimize Microservice Vulnerabilities (20%)Q81-Q120

🟡 Intermediate: Securing Your Actual Applications

This category is about securing the applications themselves — the pods, containers, security contexts, and admission controls. Think of it like securing each room inside the fortress with its own locks, cameras, and access controls. For anihpj/jobpost, this means every pod runs with locked-down securityContext, Secrets are encrypted at rest, and admission controllers enforce security policies before any pod is created.

Q81-Q120 40 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

4.1 Security Context — Pod & Container Level SecurityQ81-Q88

Microservice Vulns Q81 of 200
What is the difference between PodSecurityContext and Container SecurityContext — where does each apply?
A) They are the same thing — you can use either interchangeably
B) PodSecurityContext applies at the POD level and affects ALL containers in the pod (e.g., runAsUser: 1000, fsGroup: 2000). Container SecurityContext applies per individual container and OVERRIDES pod-level settings (e.g., readOnlyRootFilesystem: true, allowPrivilegeEscalation: false). Pod-level is the DEFAULT, container-level OVERRIDES it
C) PodSecurityContext only works on Linux; Container SecurityContext is cross-platform
D) PodSecurityContext is deprecated — only Container SecurityContext should be used
✅ Correct Answer: B

Pod-level settings are the base; container-level settings override. This is similar to CSS inheritance — the most specific setting wins.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SecurityContext Hierarchy — Pod → Container
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: v1                                                   │
  │  kind: Pod                                                        │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    securityContext:           ← POD LEVEL (defaults for all)      │
  │      runAsUser: 1000                                              │
  │      runAsGroup: 3000                                             │
  │      fsGroup: 2000           ← Shared volume ownership           │
  │    containers:                                                    │
  │    - name: api                                                    │
  │      image: anihpj/api                                           │
  │      securityContext:        ← CONTAINER LEVEL (overrides)       │
  │        allowPrivilegeEscalation: false                            │
  │        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true                               │
  │        capabilities:                                              │
  │          drop: ["ALL"]                                            │
  │    - name: sidecar                                                │
  │      image: anihpj/sidecar                                       │
  │      securityContext:        ← Different container, own context  │
  │        readOnlyRootFilesystem: false   ← Override for sidecar    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  RESULT: api runs as UID 1000 with readOnlyRoot.                 │
  │          sidecar also runs as UID 1000 (inherits) but writable.  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Set runAsNonRoot: true and fsGroup at the pod level for all containers. Then customize per-container settings like readOnlyRootFilesystem where needed.

Microservice Vulns Q82 of 200
What does runAsNonRoot: true enforce, and why is it a critical security setting?
A) It PREVENTS the container from starting if its image is configured to run as UID 0 (root). The kubelet validates this BEFORE the container starts — if the image's USER is root, the pod is REJECTED. This is the single most impactful security setting because it eliminates the most common privilege escalation path
B) It just prints a warning but still allows the container to run as root
C) It automatically changes the container's UID to 1000 — it's an automatic remapping tool
D) It only works with container-optimized base images — standard images ignore this setting
✅ Correct Answer: A

runAsNonRoot: true is the single most important security setting. It's an ENFORCEMENT, not a suggestion. If your Dockerfile says USER root, the pod will NOT start. This catches misconfigurations at deploy time.

📝 Detailed Explanation
runAsNonRoot — The Gatekeeper
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT runAsNonRoot:                                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12                                            │  │
  │  │ # No USER directive → defaults to root (UID 0)              │  │
  │  │ → Container runs as root → ALL capabilities available       │  │
  │  │ → If compromised → ATTACKER IS ROOT inside container        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WITH runAsNonRoot: true (pod spec):                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ securityContext:                                             │  │
  │  │   runAsNonRoot: true                                        │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # Dockerfile MUST include:                                   │  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12                                             │  │
  │  │ RUN useradd -u 1000 app && mkdir /app && chown app /app     │  │
  │  │ USER 1000    ← Required for runAsNonRoot to work!          │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ → Container runs as UID 1000 → NO root access               │  │
  │  │ → Even if compromised → attacker is UID 1000 (limited)      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS critical: PSA Restricted REQUIRES runAsNonRoot. All CKS exam pod specs should include this. Combined with drop: [ALL] capabilities, it eliminates 95% of container escape techniques.

4.2 Secrets ManagementQ89-Q94

Microservice Vulns Q83 of 200
What are runAsUser and runAsGroup in a SecurityContext, and why should you explicitly set them?
A) They are optional cosmetic labels — they don't affect actual container permissions
B) They only work with stateful applications — stateless apps should use the default root UID
C) runAsUser forces the container to run as a SPECIFIC UID (e.g., 1000), OVERRIDING whatever the Dockerfile USER directive says. runAsGroup sets the primary GID. This prevents container image authors from sneaking in root access and ensures consistent UID/GID across deployments for file permission management
D) Both are deprecated — use runAsNonRoot instead which handles UID mapping automatically
✅ Correct Answer: C

Explicit UID/GID gives you CONTROL. Without it, you're trusting the container image's USER directive — which defaults to root in most base images. Setting runAsUser: 1000 ensures consistency regardless of the image.

📝 Detailed Explanation
UID/GID Control — Defense Against Root
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj RECOMMENDED UID/GID SCHEME:                               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ securityContext:                                             │  │
  │  │   runAsNonRoot: true                                        │  │
  │  │   runAsUser: 1000       ← Django app user (non-root)        │  │
  │  │   runAsGroup: 3000      ← App group for shared access       │  │
  │  │   fsGroup: 3000         ← Volume files owned by this group  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ WHY 1000? Any UID > 0 works. 1000 is conventional.          │  │
  │  │ WHY 3000? Separate group for shared volume access —         │  │
  │  │         multiple pods can share volumes via group membership │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  DOCKERFILE MUST MATCH:                                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12-slim                                        │  │
  │  │ RUN groupadd -g 3000 appgroup && useradd -u 1000 -g 3000 app│  │
  │  │ USER 1000:3000                                               │  │
  │  │ # Now pod's runAsUser: 1000 matches image's USER 1000       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q84 of 200
What is fsGroup and why is it important for shared volumes between containers?
A) fsGroup is a filesystem encryption setting — it encrypts data at rest on volumes
B) fsGroup sets the GROUP OWNERSHIP of files on mounted volumes. When a volume is mounted, Kubernetes recursively changes ALL files/directories to be owned by this GID. Multiple containers in the same pod (or across pods with the same fsGroup) can read/write shared data. Without fsGroup, files created by one container may be inaccessible to another
C) fsGroup is only needed for NFS volumes — local volumes handle permissions automatically
D) fsGroup is a network policy setting that groups pods for firewall rules
✅ Correct Answer: B

fsGroup solves the "file permission hell" problem. Container A writes a file as UID 1000; container B (also UID 1000 but different container) can't read it because the file is owned by 1000:0. fsGroup ensures the file is 1000:3000 — both containers in group 3000 can access it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
fsGroup — Shared Volume Permissions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PROBLEM (without fsGroup):                                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Pod: anihpj-api                                             │  │
  │  │ Container A (UID 1000): writes /data/file.txt → 1000:0     │  │
  │  │ Container B (UID 1000): reads /data/file.txt → PERM DENIED! │  │
  │  │   → File owned by 1000:0, B is in group 0 but file is 0:0  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  SOLUTION (with fsGroup: 3000):                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ securityContext:                                             │  │
  │  │   fsGroup: 3000                                              │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ Container A (UID 1000): writes /data/file.txt → 1000:3000  │  │
  │  │ Container B (UID 1000): reads /data/file.txt → ALLOWED ✅   │  │
  │  │   → Both containers in group 3000, file is group-readable   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q85 of 200
What are Linux capabilities and why should you drop ALL and add only needed ones?
A) Capabilities are just another name for RBAC permissions — they control API access, not container privileges
B) All capabilities should be kept for maximum compatibility — dropping them breaks applications
C) Only drop NET_ADMIN — other capabilities are harmless for web applications
D) Capabilities are granular kernel permissions (not all-or-nothing root). Default Docker grants ~14 capabilities. drop: [ALL] removes EVERYTHING, then add: [NET_BIND_SERVICE] adds back only what's needed (bind to ports <1024). This implements least-privilege at the kernel level
✅ Correct Answer: D

Capabilities break the monolithic "root" into 40+ individual permissions. drop: [ALL] removes every single one, then you add back only what your app actually needs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Capabilities — Drop ALL, Add Only What's Needed
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEFAULT (Docker grants ~14 caps — WAY too many):                 │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ CHOWN, DAC_OVERRIDE, FSETID, FOWNER, SETGID, SETUID,       │  │
  │  │ SETPCAP, NET_BIND_SERVICE, NET_RAW, SYS_CHROOT, KILL,      │  │
  │  │ AUDIT_WRITE, MKNOD, SETFCAP                                 │  │
  │  │ → 14 attack vectors available to a compromised container    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  SECURE (drop ALL, add minimum):                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ securityContext:                                             │  │
  │  │   capabilities:                                             │  │
  │  │     drop: [ALL]       ← Remove ALL 40+ capabilities         │  │
  │  │     add: [NET_BIND_SERVICE]  ← Only needed for port 80/443 │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ anihpj-web (Nginx): add: [NET_BIND_SERVICE] — bind port 80  │  │
  │  │ anihpj-api (Django): add: [NET_BIND_SERVICE] — bind 8000   │  │
  │  │ anihpj-db (PG): drop: [ALL] — PostgreSQL needs NO caps!     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4.3 Isolation Techniques — Multi-Tenancy & SandboxingQ95-Q102

Microservice Vulns Q86 of 200
What does allowPrivilegeEscalation: false prevent?
A) It prevents ANY child process from gaining MORE privileges than its parent. Specifically, it sets the no_new_privs flag — even if a process has SUID binaries or additional capabilities, child processes CANNOT use them. A shell spawned by a compromised app cannot execute sudo or SUID-root binaries to escalate
B) It prevents the pod from being scheduled on nodes with higher privileges
C) It's a Kubernetes RBAC setting that limits which users can exec into the pod
D) It only applies to containers running as root — non-root containers ignore this setting
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is a critical defense-in-depth setting. Even if your container runs as non-root, a SUID binary could allow escalation. allowPrivilegeEscalation: false blocks that path entirely.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Privilege Escalation Prevention
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ATTACK SCENARIO (without allowPrivilegeEscalation):              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ $ id → uid=1000(app) gid=3000(app)                          │  │
  │  │ $ ls -la /usr/bin/sudo → -rwsr-xr-x root root /usr/bin/sudo│  │
  │  │ $ sudo whoami → root  ← ESCALATED TO ROOT via SUID binary!  │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ SUID binaries (sudo, su, passwd) run as FILE OWNER       │  │
  │  │   regardless of who executes them — classic escalation path │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  DEFENSE (with allowPrivilegeEscalation: false):                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ securityContext:                                             │  │
  │  │   allowPrivilegeEscalation: false                            │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ $ sudo whoami                                                │  │
  │  │ sudo: effective uid is not 0, is sudo installed setuid root? │  │
  │  │ → BLOCKED! no_new_privs flag prevents SUID execution        │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ALSO BLOCKS:                                                 │  │
  │  │ • Child processes gaining capabilities via setcap binaries   │  │
  │  │ • Seccomp filter changes (if set before this flag)           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q87 of 200
What does readOnlyRootFilesystem: true do and why should most application containers use it?
A) It makes the entire container filesystem read-only including mounted volumes
B) It only affects the container image layers — runtime writes are still permitted
C) It makes the container's ROOT filesystem read-only — applications CANNOT write to /, /etc, /bin, etc. Only explicitly mounted volumes are writable. This prevents attackers from modifying binaries, installing backdoors, or dropping malware. Use emptyDir volumes for directories that need writes (e.g., /tmp)
D) It's a filesystem encryption feature — data is encrypted but still writable
✅ Correct Answer: C

Immutable container filesystems prevent runtime tampering. If an attacker gets shell access, they can't modify binaries, drop malware, or change configurations — the filesystem is locked.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Read-Only Root — Writable Only Where Needed
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-api POD with readOnlyRootFilesystem:                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ securityContext:                                             │  │
  │  │   readOnlyRootFilesystem: true                               │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ volumes:                                                     │  │
  │  │ - name: tmp                                                  │  │
  │  │   emptyDir: {}              ← Writable /tmp (in-memory)     │  │
  │  │ - name: cache                                                │  │
  │  │   emptyDir: {}              ← Writable cache                │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ volumeMounts:                                                │  │
  │  │ - name: tmp                                                  │  │
  │  │   mountPath: /tmp                                           │  │
  │  │ - name: cache                                                │  │
  │  │   mountPath: /var/cache                                     │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ RESULT:                                                      │  │
  │  │ /         → READ-ONLY (can't modify binaries)               │  │
  │  │ /etc      → READ-ONLY (can't modify configs)                │  │
  │  │ /bin      → READ-ONLY (can't install malware)               │  │
  │  │ /tmp      → WRITABLE (emptyDir volume)                      │  │
  │  │ /var/cache → WRITABLE (emptyDir volume)                     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
