4.2 Secrets ManagementQ89-Q94

Microservice Vulns Q89 of 200
Kubernetes Secrets are base64-encoded, NOT encrypted by default. What does this mean for security?
A) Base64 is a form of encryption — Secrets are secure by default in Kubernetes
B) Base64 is ENCODING, not encryption. Anyone with etcd access can decode Secrets trivially: echo <base64> | base64 -d. Secrets are stored as PLAINTEXT in etcd unless EncryptionConfiguration is enabled. Additionally, anyone with RBAC "get secrets" can read them. You MUST enable encryption at rest and restrict RBAC
C) Secrets are encrypted in transit but plaintext at rest — only network attackers are blocked
D) Base64 encoding is sufficient for production — no additional encryption is needed per Kubernetes docs
✅ Correct Answer: B

This is the most commonly misunderstood security concept in Kubernetes. Base64 is reversible trivially — it's encoding, not encryption. Treat Secrets as plaintext unless encryption at rest is explicitly configured.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Base64 vs Encryption — The Hard Truth
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT EncryptionConfiguration:                                 │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl create secret generic db-pass --from-literal=pw=x   │  │
  │  │ kubectl get secret db-pass -o jsonpath='{.data.pw}'         │  │
  │  │ → eA==                                                       │  │
  │  │ echo eA== | base64 -d → x   ← DECODED IN 1 SECOND!          │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ # Direct etcd access:                                        │  │
  │  │ etcdctl get /registry/secrets/default/db-pass               │  │
  │  │ → {"data":{"pw":"eA=="}}  ← PLAINTEXT base64 in etcd!       │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Anyone with etcd access = ALL SECRETS VISIBLE            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WITH EncryptionConfiguration (ENCRYPTED at rest):                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ etcdctl get /registry/secrets/default/db-pass               │  │
  │  │ → k8s:enc:aescbc:v1:key1:<encrypted-blob>  ← ENCRYPTED!    │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ Even with etcd access, Secrets are UNREADABLE            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q90 of 200
How do you enable encryption at rest for Secrets using EncryptionConfiguration?
A) Encryption is enabled by default in Kubernetes 1.24+ — no configuration needed
B) Set an annotation on each Secret — encryption is per-Secret, not cluster-wide
C) Create an EncryptionConfiguration YAML specifying resources (secrets), providers (aescbc, secretbox, aesgcm, or kms), and keys. Pass it to the API server via --encryption-provider-config. The FIRST provider is used for WRITES, subsequent providers for READS. Restart API server. Then run kubectl get secrets --all-namespaces -o json | kubectl replace -f - to re-encrypt all existing Secrets
D) Use kubectl encrypt secret — the built-in encryption command works on individual Secrets
✅ Correct Answer: C

EncryptionConfiguration is the CKS-critical feature for Secret protection. Without it, etcd access = total Secret compromise. The API server encrypts on write, decrypts on read, transparently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
EncryptionConfiguration — Setup & Re-encrypt
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  /etc/kubernetes/encryption-config.yaml:                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: apiserver.config.k8s.io/v1                      │  │
  │  │ kind: EncryptionConfiguration                               │  │
  │  │ resources:                                                  │  │
  │  │ - resources: [secrets]                                      │  │
  │  │   providers:                                                │  │
  │  │   - aescbc:           ← WRITE provider (encrypts new)       │  │
  │  │       keys:                                                 │  │
  │  │       - name: key1                                          │  │
  │  │         secret: <base64-encoded-32-byte-key>               │  │
  │  │   - identity: {}      ← READ provider (for decryption only) │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  API SERVER FLAG:                                                 │
  │  --encryption-provider-config=/etc/kubernetes/encryption-config   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  RE-ENCRYPT EXISTING SECRETS (required after enabling):           │
  │  kubectl get secrets -A -o json | kubectl replace -f -            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q91 of 200
How do you rotate Secrets without causing application downtime?
A) (1) Create a NEW Secret with the updated value (e.g., db-pass-v2), (2) Update the Deployment to reference the new Secret name, (3) Perform a rolling restart (kubectl rollout restart deploy). The old pods use the old Secret until they're replaced. Alternatively, mount Secrets as volumes — Kubernetes auto-updates mounted Secrets (within ~90s), so just update the Secret data and pods will see the new value
B) Delete the old Secret and create a new one with the same name — pods automatically reconnect
C) Secrets cannot be rotated — you must delete and recreate the entire application
D) Use kubectl edit secret — the API server handles rolling the update to all pods
✅ Correct Answer: A

Secret rotation without downtime is critical for production. The two strategies are: versioned Secrets with rolling restart, or volume-mounted Secrets with auto-update (if your app supports hot-reload).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Zero-Downtime Secret Rotation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STRATEGY 1: Versioned Secrets + Rolling Restart                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl create secret generic db-pass-v2 --from-literal=pw │  │
  │  │ kubectl patch deploy anihpj-api -p '{"spec":{"template":{" │  │
  │  │   spec":{"volumes":[{"name":"db-pass","secret":            │  │
  │  │   {"secretName":"db-pass-v2"}}]}}}}'                        │  │
  │  │ → Rolling restart: new pods use v2, old pods drain           │  │
  │  │ → Delete db-pass-v1 after all pods migrated                  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STRATEGY 2: Volume Mount + Auto-Update (simpler)                 │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl create secret generic db-pass --from-literal=pw=old │  │
  │  │ # Pods mount db-pass as volume                               │  │
  │  │ # Later:                                                      │  │
  │  │ kubectl create secret generic db-pass --from-literal=pw=new \│  │
  │  │   --dry-run=client -o yaml | kubectl apply -f -             │  │
  │  │ → Kubernetes updates mounted Secret within ~90 seconds       │  │
  │  │ → App MUST support hot-reload (watch file changes)           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q92 of 200
Why use external secret stores (HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager) instead of Kubernetes Secrets?
A) External stores are faster — Kubernetes Secrets have high latency on read
B) External stores provide: (1) Dynamic secrets (auto-generated, short-lived), (2) Automatic rotation with no pod restart, (3) Audit logging of every access, (4) Fine-grained access policies, (5) Multi-cluster secret sharing. Operators like External Secrets Operator sync external secrets INTO Kubernetes, maintaining compatibility while using the external store as the source of truth
C) Kubernetes Secrets are deprecated — external stores are the only supported option in modern clusters
D) External stores are only needed for financial applications — regular apps can use standard Secrets
✅ Correct Answer: B

External Secret Operators (ESO) bridge the gap between external vaults and Kubernetes. You define an ExternalSecret resource, and ESO syncs it into a native Kubernetes Secret — pods use standard Secret mounts, but the source of truth is the vault.

📝 Detailed Explanation
External Secrets Operator — Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐                    │
  │  │ AWS Secrets Mgr  │     │ HashiCorp Vault  │   (Source of Truth)│
  │  │ /prod/db/password│     │ secret/db-pass   │                    │
  │  └────────┬─────────┘     └────────┬─────────┘                    │
  │           │                        │                              │
  │           └────────┬───────────────┘                              │
  │                    │                                              │
  │                    ▼                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐                         │
  │  │ External Secrets Operator (ESO)     │  (Controller)            │
  │  │ Reads ExternalSecret CRDs           │                          │
  │  │ Syncs values into K8s Secrets       │                          │
  │  └────────────────┬────────────────────┘                         │
  │                   │                                               │
  │                   ▼                                               │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐                         │
  │  │ Kubernetes Secret: db-pass           │  (K8s-native)           │
  │  │ Pod mounts as volume/env             │                          │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────┘                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q93 of 200
Why should you NEVER put Secrets in environment variables, and what's the secure alternative?
A) Environment variables are encrypted by Kubernetes — they're the safest option for Secrets
B) Environment variables are fine for production — they're only visible to the container runtime
C) The risk is minimal — only root can read /proc/self/environ inside the container
D) /proc/self/environ is WORLD-READABLE inside every container. Any process — even a compromised child — can read ALL environment variables. env vars also appear in crash logs, debug output, and kubectl describe pod. Use volume mounts instead — files have permissions that can be restricted, and Kubernetes only exposes mounted files to the specific container
✅ Correct Answer: D

Environment variables are a security anti-pattern for secrets. Any process in the container can read them. Volume mounts provide file-level permissions and are not exposed to crash dumps or debug output.

📝 Detailed Explanation
env vs Volume — Secret Exposure Risk
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DANGEROUS: envFrom (environment variables)                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   containers:                                               │  │
  │  │   - name: api                                              │  │
  │  │     envFrom:                                                │  │
  │  │     - secretRef: {name: db-pass}  ← ALL keys become env    │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ $ cat /proc/1/environ | tr '\0' '\n'  → password=s3cret!   │  │
  │  │ $ kubectl exec pod -- env  → password=s3cret!              │  │
  │  │ $ kubectl logs pod (crash dump) → password=s3cret!         │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ EXPOSED in 3+ locations!                                 │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  SECURE: Volume mount (file-based)                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ spec:                                                       │  │
  │  │   containers:                                               │  │
  │  │   - name: api                                              │  │
  │  │     volumeMounts:                                           │  │
  │  │     - name: db-pass                                        │  │
  │  │       mountPath: /etc/secrets/db                           │  │
  │  │       readOnly: true                                        │  │
  │  │   volumes:                                                  │  │
  │  │   - name: db-pass                                          │  │
  │  │     secret: {secretName: db-pass, defaultMode: 0400}       │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ $ cat /etc/secrets/db/password  → s3cret!  (file only)     │  │
  │  │ $ kubectl exec pod -- env  → NO SECRETS EXPOSED            │  │
  │  │ ✅ Only file-based access, permissions 0400 (owner read)   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q94 of 200
What are the trade-offs between mounting Secrets as volumes vs using envFrom?
A) envFrom is always better — it's faster and simpler than volume mounts
B) Volume mounts require a CSI driver — they only work on cloud providers
C) envFrom: simpler, auto-injected, but exposed to all processes, crash dumps, and debug output. Volume mounts: file-based with permissions, auto-updates (kubelet refreshes ~90s), not in crash logs, but requires app to read files. Security best practice: ALWAYS use volume mounts for sensitive values; envFrom only for non-sensitive config
D) Both are identical from a security perspective — it's just developer preference
✅ Correct Answer: C

Volume mounts win on security: auto-updating, permission-controlled, not in crash logs, not in /proc. The only advantage of envFrom is simplicity — and that's not worth the security trade-off for production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Volume vs envFrom — Security Comparison
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VOLUME MOUNT (SECURE)            ENVFROM (LESS SECURE)           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐  ┌────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ Auto-updates (kubelet    │  │ ❌ Static at pod start     │  │
  │  │    refreshes ~90s)          │  │    (needs restart to update)│  │
  │  │ ✅ File permissions (0400)  │  │ ❌ World-readable in pod   │  │
  │  │ ✅ Not in crash dumps       │  │ ❌ Appears in crash logs   │  │
  │  │ ✅ Not in kubectl describe  │  │ ❌ In kubectl describe      │  │
  │  │ ✅ App controls read timing │  │ ❌ All env loaded at start  │  │
  │  │ ✅ Atomic updates           │  │ ❌ Symlink swap            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘  └────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Mount database credentials as files in /etc/secrets/db/ with mode 0400. The Django app reads them at startup and whenever the file changes (using a file watcher). Never use envFrom for production secrets.

4.3 Isolation Techniques — Multi-Tenancy & SandboxingQ95-Q102

Microservice Vulns Q95 of 200
What are the levels of container isolation, from weakest to strongest?
A) All containers have equal isolation — Docker provides the same security regardless of configuration
B) Shared Kernel (runc/containerd — containers share host kernel, weakest) → User Namespace (UID remapping — container root ≠ host root) → User-Space Kernel (gVisor — fake kernel intercepts syscalls) → Hardware VM (Kata/Firecracker — each pod gets its OWN kernel, strongest). Each level trades performance for isolation
C) VM-based isolation is weakest because of the hypervisor overhead — shared kernel is strongest
D) Container isolation is binary — either isolated or not, there are no levels
✅ Correct Answer: B

CKS tests your understanding of the isolation spectrum. For anihpj: API/web = shared kernel (fast), file-processing = gVisor (isolated), multi-tenant DB = Kata (strongest).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Isolation Spectrum
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ISOLATION   RUNTIME       KERNEL          PERF   USE CASE        │
  │  ─────────── ───────────── ─────────────── ────── ────────────── │
  │  WEAKEST     runc/         SHARED with     100%   Trusted apps   │
  │              containerd    host kernel             (anihpj-api)   │
  │  ─────────── ───────────── ─────────────── ────── ────────────── │
  │  BETTER      User NS       Remapped UIDs    ~98%   Basic hardening│
  │              (K8s 1.25+)   (0→65534)                                │
  │  ─────────── ───────────── ─────────────── ────── ────────────── │
  │  STRONG      gVisor        FAKE kernel      ~85%   Untrusted code│
  │              (runsc)       (Sentry in user)         (file upload) │
  │  ─────────── ───────────── ─────────────── ────── ────────────── │
  │  STRONGEST   Kata          OWN kernel       ~70%   Multi-tenant  │
  │              (QEMU/Fire)   per pod (VM)             (financial)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS relevance: The exam expects you to match workloads to appropriate isolation levels. "A financial services pod processing PII" → Kata. "A public-facing web API" → runc with securityContext hardening.

Microservice Vulns Q96 of 200
What is gVisor's architecture — the Sentry, Gofer, and Platform components?
A) Sentry: User-space kernel that intercepts ALL syscalls (the app thinks it's talking to Linux, but it's talking to Sentry). Gofer: File proxy that handles filesystem operations (no direct host FS access). Platform: Abstraction layer (ptrace or KVM) for switching between guest and host. Together they provide ~85% of native performance with strong isolation
B) gVisor is just a container runtime — it has the same architecture as containerd
C) Sentry is a network proxy and Gofer is a logging daemon — they don't affect isolation
D) gVisor uses a full hypervisor like VirtualBox — it's essentially a VM per container
✅ Correct Answer: A

gVisor's genius is the Sentry — a user-space kernel that implements ~200 Linux syscalls. The app thinks it's talking to Linux; it's actually talking to a Go program. This means kernel exploits in the HOST kernel cannot directly compromise gVisor containers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
gVisor Architecture — Sentry Intercepts Syscalls
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  APPLICATION (unmodified binary)                                  │
  │       │ syscall (open, read, write...)                           │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ SENTRY (user-space kernel written in Go)                  │   │
  │  │ • Implements ~200 Linux syscalls                         │   │
  │  │ • Filters dangerous operations                            │   │
  │  │ • NO direct host kernel access                            │   │
  │  │ • If app makes blocked syscall → SIGSYS                   │   │
  │  └──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                     │ limited, filtered syscalls                  │
  │                     ▼                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ GOFER (file proxy)     │  PLATFORM (ptrace/KVM)           │   │
  │  │ Handles 9P filesystem  │  Switches guest↔host contexts    │   │
  │  │ operations safely      │                                   │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                     │                                             │
  │                     ▼                                             │
  │              HOST LINUX KERNEL (protected)                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

gVisor runs as a normal user process on the host — no kernel modules needed. If gVisor itself has a vulnerability, the attacker is trapped in userspace, not the kernel.

Microservice Vulns Q97 of 200
How do Kata Containers provide isolation, and how do they differ from gVisor?
A) Kata and gVisor are identical — both use user-space kernel interception
B) Kata uses Docker-in-Docker — it runs a nested container runtime for isolation
C) Kata uses SELinux labels — it's a policy-based isolation system, not a separate runtime
D) Kata runs each pod in its OWN lightweight VM using a hypervisor (QEMU or Firecracker). Each pod gets its OWN Linux kernel — completely separate from the host. This provides hardware-level isolation (stronger than gVisor's user-space approach). Trade-off: ~30% performance overhead, slower startup (VM boot time)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kata is the "nuclear option" for isolation. Each pod is a VM. Even if the guest kernel is exploited, the attacker is trapped inside the VM — they cannot reach the host kernel. Use for: multi-tenant databases, financial transactions, highly sensitive workloads.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kata Containers — VM-Level Isolation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  gVisor (user-space kernel)     Kata (hardware VM)                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ App → Sentry → Host  │      │ App → Guest Kernel → Hyper   │  │
  │  │   (fake kernel)      │      │   visor → Host Kernel        │  │
  │  │                       │      │                               │  │
  │  │ Pro: Fast (~85%)      │      │ Pro: STRONGEST isolation     │  │
  │  │ Con: Sentry = attack  │      │ Con: Slower (~70%), more RAM │  │
  │  │       surface (Go)    │      │                               │  │
  │  └──────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  KATA ISOLATION: Each pod gets:                                   │
  │  • OWN Linux kernel (mini kernel ~20MB)                           │
  │  • OWN hardware virtualized via QEMU or Firecracker               │
  │  • COMPLETE kernel isolation from host                            │
  │  • Even if guest kernel is exploited → CANNOT escape to host      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS tip: Kata is the answer when the question mentions "multi-tenant," "PII data," "financial transactions," or "strongest isolation required." Know the performance trade-off: ~70% of native speed.

Microservice Vulns Q98 of 200
What are user namespaces and how do they map container UID 0 to host UID 65534?
A) User namespaces are a Kubernetes feature that renames users — cosmetic only
B) User namespaces only work with Docker — Kubernetes doesn't support them
C) User namespaces map a range of UIDs INSIDE the container to a DIFFERENT range on the HOST. Container's UID 0 (root) is mapped to host UID 65534 (nobody). Inside the container, the process "thinks" it's root, but on the HOST, it's an unprivileged user. Kubernetes 1.25+ supports this natively via hostUsers: false in Pod spec
D) User namespaces are enabled by default — all containers already run with remapped UIDs
✅ Correct Answer: C

User namespaces are a game-changer. Even if a container "breakout" occurs, the attacker escapes as UID 65534 (nobody) — not root. Combined with seccomp and drop ALL capabilities, this eliminates entire classes of container escape exploits. K8s 1.25+ with hostUsers: false.

📝 Detailed Explanation
User Namespace UID Mapping
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT User Namespaces:                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Container: UID 0 (root) → HOST: UID 0 (root!)              │  │
  │  │ Container: UID 1000        → HOST: UID 1000                │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Container root = HOST root → Docker escape = ROOT!      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WITH User Namespaces (K8s 1.25+):                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Container: UID 0 (root) → HOST: UID 65534 (nobody!)        │  │
  │  │ Container: UID 1000       → HOST: UID 101000               │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ Container "thinks" it's root → on host it's NOBODY      │  │
  │  │ ✅ Even if breakout → attacker is UID 65534 (powerless)    │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ENABLE in Pod spec:                                          │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                        │  │
  │  │   hostUsers: false   ← Maps container UIDs to host range   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Enable user namespaces on ALL pods. The Django API can run as UID 1000 in the container but maps to some high UID on the host. Defense-in-depth with zero application changes.

Microservice Vulns Q99 of 200
How should you assign RuntimeClasses in production based on workload sensitivity?
A) Use the same runtime for everything — consistency is more important than isolation
B) Default (runc): Standard web/API workloads (anihpj-web, anihpj-api). gVisor: Untrusted workloads (user-uploaded file processing). Kata: Highly sensitive workloads (multi-tenant databases, PII processing). This tiered approach balances performance, cost, and security
C) Always use Kata for everything — maximum security regardless of cost
D) RuntimeClass is a development-only feature — production clusters should use a single runtime
✅ Correct Answer: B

Tiered runtime assignment is the production pattern. Not every pod needs VM-level isolation — it would be prohibitively expensive. Match the isolation level to the data sensitivity and trust level of each workload.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj RuntimeClass Assignment Strategy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WORKLOAD          │ RUNTIME     │ REASON                        │
  │  ──────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────── │
  │  anihpj-web        │ runc        │ Static content, trusted        │
  │  anihpj-api        │ runc        │ Business logic, trusted        │
  │  anihpj-db         │ runc        │ DB engine, trusted             │
  │  anihpj-redis      │ runc        │ Cache, trusted                 │
  │  anihpj-file-upload│ gVisor      │ User uploads = UNTRUSTED!      │
  │  anihpj-payment    │ Kata        │ PCI-DSS compliance required    │
  │  ──────────────────┴─────────────┴────────────────────────────── │
  │                                                                   │
  │  PRINCIPLE: Default = runc (fast). Escalate isolation             │
  │  for untrusted inputs, PII processing, and compliance.           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS exam note: The exam may ask you to create a RuntimeClass and assign it to a specific pod. Know the syntax: apiVersion: node.k8s.io/v1, kind: RuntimeClass, handler: <runtime>.

Microservice Vulns Q100 of 200
What are multi-tenancy patterns in Kubernetes — namespace isolation, ResourceQuota, and NetworkPolicy?
A) Multi-tenancy just means using separate clusters — there are no patterns within a single cluster
B) Namespace isolation is automatic — creating a namespace fully isolates tenants from each other
C) ResourceQuota is the only multi-tenancy tool — it limits CPU/memory per namespace
D) Multi-tenancy uses layered controls: (1) Namespace: Logical boundary with RBAC, (2) ResourceQuota: Limits CPU/memory/storage per tenant, (3) NetworkPolicy: Isolates tenant networks (tenant-A pods cannot reach tenant-B pods), (4) PSA: Enforces security standards per namespace, (5) RuntimeClass: Stronger isolation for untrusted tenants. These COMBINE for defense-in-depth
✅ Correct Answer: D

Soft multi-tenancy (single cluster) uses these 5 layers. For anihpj: each environment (dev/staging/prod) is a namespace with its own quotas, network isolation, and PSA level. This prevents a compromised dev pod from accessing production data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Tenancy — 5-Layer Defense Model
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LAYER 1: Namespace (logical boundary)                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj-dev │ anihpj-staging │ anihpj-prod  ← ISOLATED NS   │  │
  │  │ Tenant A   │ Tenant B       │ Tenant C                      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  LAYER 2: RBAC (who can access what)                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ RoleBinding: dev-team → Role: dev-admin (NS: anihpj-dev)   │  │
  │  │ → Dev team CANNOT access staging or production!            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  LAYER 3: ResourceQuota (prevent resource hogging)               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj-dev: cpu=10, mem=20Gi   anihpj-prod: cpu=50,mem=100│  │
  │  │ → Noisy neighbor cannot starve other tenants                │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  LAYER 4: NetworkPolicy (traffic isolation)                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ingress: from namespaceSelector {name: anihpj-prod} → DENY │  │
  │  │ → dev pods CANNOT reach production pods                     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  LAYER 5: PSA + RuntimeClass (pod-level security)                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj-dev:  warn=restricted    (warn, don't block)         │  │
  │  │ anihpj-prod: enforce=restricted (BLOCK insecure pods)      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CKS critical: Multi-tenancy is tested heavily. Know ALL 5 layers and how they work together. No single layer is sufficient — defense-in-depth requires combining them.

Microservice Vulns Q101 of 200
Why are hostPID, hostIPC, and hostNetwork dangerous and when should they be used?
A) hostPID: true lets the pod see ALL host processes (including other pods' processes!). hostIPC: true shares host IPC namespace (shared memory attacks). hostNetwork: true uses the host's network stack (bypasses NetworkPolicy, sees all node traffic). These should ONLY be used for infrastructure components (CNI plugins, kube-proxy) — NEVER for application pods
B) These settings improve performance and should be enabled for all production pods
C) Kubernetes automatically blocks these — they require cluster-admin to enable
D) These are legacy settings preserved for backward compatibility — they have no effect in modern K8s
✅ Correct Answer: A

These three settings completely break container isolation. PSA Baseline explicitly blocks all three. PSA Restricted also blocks them. Only kube-system infrastructure pods should ever use them.

📝 Detailed Explanation
hostPID/hostIPC/hostNetwork — Container Escape Vectors
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DANGEROUS SETTINGS (PSA RESTRICTED BLOCKS ALL):                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ hostPID: true    → Pod sees ALL node processes via /proc    │  │
  │  │                    $ ps aux → sees kubelet, sshd, other pods│  │
  │  │                    $ kill -9 <kubelet-pid> → KILL KUBELET! │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ hostIPC: true    → Shared memory with host (shm attacks)    │  │
  │  │                    $ ipcs -m → see ALL shared memory segs   │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ hostNetwork: true→ Uses HOST network, bypasses CNI          │  │
  │  │                    $ tcpdump -i eth0 → sniff ALL traffic    │  │
  │  │                    → Sees API server traffic, DB queries    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  ONLY ALLOWED FOR: CNI plugins (Calico, Cilium), kube-proxy      │
  │  NEVER FOR: Application pods (web, api, db)                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: NEVER set any of these on application pods. If a pod spec requests them, it's either a misconfiguration or an attack — the PSA enforce=restricted will BLOCK it.

Microservice Vulns Q102 of 200
What does the NodeRestriction admission controller do?
A) It limits the number of pods per node — it's a resource management tool
B) It restricts which users can SSH into nodes — it's an SSH policy controller
C) It limits kubelet's API access to ONLY modify its OWN node's objects. A kubelet on worker-1 cannot modify pods or nodes labeled for worker-2. This prevents a compromised kubelet from modifying other nodes' pods, stealing their Secrets, or impersonating other nodes — critical for multi-tenant cluster security
D) It's deprecated — kubelets are trusted components and don't need restriction
✅ Correct Answer: C

NodeRestriction prevents lateral movement from a compromised node. Even if an attacker compromises kubelet on worker-1, they cannot use its credentials to access pods on worker-2 or modify other nodes' configurations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NodeRestriction — Containing Node Compromise
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT NodeRestriction:                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Worker-1 kubelet compromised → Attacker can:                │  │
  │  │   ❌ kubectl get pods -A (read all pods)                    │  │
  │  │   ❌ kubectl label node worker-2 ... (modify other nodes)   │  │
  │  │   ❌ Access Secrets on other nodes via kubelet API          │  │
  │  │   → LATERAL MOVEMENT across entire cluster                  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WITH NodeRestriction (--enable-admission-plugins=...,NodeRest):  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Worker-1 kubelet CAN ONLY:                                  │  │
  │  │   ✅ Modify pods labeled nodeName=worker-1 (its OWN pods)   │  │
  │  │   ✅ Update its own Node object (status, labels)             │  │
  │  │   ❌ Cannot touch any other node's pods or objects          │  │
  │  │   → COMPROMISE CONTAINED to a single node                   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q103 of 200
Why does pod-to-pod encryption matter, and what attack does it prevent?
A) Pod-to-pod traffic is automatically encrypted by Kubernetes — no additional configuration needed
B) By default, pod-to-pod traffic is PLAINTEXT. A network sniffer on any node (or compromised pod with hostNetwork) can capture database queries, API tokens, and user data in transit. Pod-to-pod encryption (mTLS via Istio/Linkerd or WireGuard via Cilium) prevents network sniffing, man-in-the-middle attacks, and ensures traffic authenticity
C) Pod-to-pod encryption is only needed for external traffic — internal cluster traffic is isolated by the CNI
D) Encryption adds unacceptable latency — it should never be used in production
✅ Correct Answer: B

By default, Kubernetes pod networks are UNENCRYPTED. Any pod on any node can potentially sniff traffic. mTLS ensures every connection is encrypted AND authenticated — both parties verify each other's identity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod-to-Pod Encryption — Three Approaches
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  APPROACH        │ HOW IT WORKS             │ OVERHEAD │ EASE     │
  │  ────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────── │
  │  Cilium WireGuard│ Kernel-level, no sidecar │ ~10%     │ Easiest  │
  │                  │ cilium config enable-    │          │          │
  │                  │ wireguard=true            │          │          │
  │  ────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────── │
  │  Istio mTLS      │ Sidecar proxy per pod    │ ~15%     │ Medium   │
  │                  │ App unaware, auto certs   │          │          │
  │  ────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────── │
  │  Linkerd mTLS    │ Rust micro-proxy         │ ~5%      │ Easy     │
  │                  │ Lightweight, fast         │          │          │
  │  ────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────┴───────── │
  │  FOR ANIHPJ: Cilium WireGuard = simplest (zero app changes)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q104 of 200
How does Cilium provide transparent pod-to-pod encryption using WireGuard or IPsec?
A) Cilium requires application code changes to enable encryption — it's not transparent
B) Cilium uses TLS certificates — each pod must have a certificate to communicate
C) Cilium provides TRANSPARENT encryption at the kernel level — no application changes, no sidecars. Enable with cilium config set enable-wireguard true or enable-ipsec true. All pod-to-pod traffic is automatically encrypted at the network layer. WireGuard offers better performance (~10% overhead vs ~30% for IPsec)
D) Cilium encryption only works on AWS — it requires cloud-specific networking features
✅ Correct Answer: C

Cilium's transparent encryption is the simplest path to pod-to-pod encryption. No sidecars, no code changes, no certificate management. One flag enables WireGuard for all inter-node pod traffic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cilium Transparent Encryption — Zero-Touch mTLS
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ENABLE (one command):                                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ cilium config set enable-wireguard true                     │  │
  │  │ # OR for IPsec:                                             │  │
  │  │ cilium config set enable-ipsec true                         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WHAT HAPPENS:                                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Node-1                          Node-2                      │  │
  │  │ ┌──────────┐                   ┌──────────┐                │  │
  │  │ │ anihpj-  │─── WireGuard ────►│ anihpj-  │                │  │
  │  │ │ api      │    encrypted       │ db       │                │  │
  │  │ │ :8000    │    tunnel          │ :5432    │                │  │
  │  │ └──────────┘                   └──────────┘                │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │ ✅ App sends plaintext HTTP → Cilium encrypts at kernel    │  │
  │  │ ✅ Pods see each other via regular ClusterIP                │  │
  │  │ ✅ WireGuard keys auto-generated, rotated                   │  │
  │  │ ✅ ~10% overhead (vs ~30% for IPsec)                        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q105 of 200
How does Istio service mesh provide automatic mTLS between services?
A) Istio requires developers to manually add TLS code to each microservice
B) Istio uses a central proxy that all traffic routes through — single point of failure
C) Istio modifies the application container to inject TLS — it's an application-level solution
D) Istio injects a SIDECAR proxy (Envoy) into each pod. The sidecar intercepts ALL inbound/outbound traffic and automatically wraps it in mTLS. The application code is COMPLETELY UNAWARE — it sends plaintext HTTP to localhost, and Envoy encrypts it. Istio's control plane (istiod) manages certificate distribution and rotation
✅ Correct Answer: D

Istio's sidecar pattern means zero application changes. Your Django app sends HTTP to localhost:8000 and Envoy handles mTLS, retries, timeouts, and circuit breaking transparently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Istio Sidecar — Automatic mTLS
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-api Pod                          anihpj-db Pod            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐               ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ App (Django)         │               │ App (PostgreSQL)     │ │
  │  │ curl localhost:5432  │               │ listen :5432         │ │
  │  │        │              │               │        ▲              │ │
  │  │        ▼              │               │        │              │ │
  │  │ ┌──────────────────┐ │  mTLS (auto)  │ ┌──────────────────┐ │ │
  │  │ │ Envoy Sidecar    │─┼───────────────┼─│ Envoy Sidecar    │ │ │
  │  │ │ (istio-proxy)    │ │  encrypted!   │ │ (istio-proxy)    │ │ │
  │  │ └──────────────────┘ │               │ └──────────────────┘ │ │
  │  └──────────────────────┘               └──────────────────────┘ │
  │  istiod → manages certs, rotates automatically                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Microservice Vulns Q106 of 200
How does Linkerd differ from Istio for mTLS — why is it considered "lightweight"?
A) Linkerd uses a RUST-based micro-proxy (not Envoy) that's purpose-built for K8s. No CRDs for configuration, no complex control plane — just TLS identity + mTLS + metrics. ~10MB per proxy vs Envoy's ~100MB. Simpler to operate, fewer moving parts, less attack surface
B) Linkerd doesn't do mTLS — it only provides metrics and dashboards
C) Linkerd is a CNI plugin, not a service mesh — it operates at Layer 3, not Layer 7
D) Linkerd and Istio are functionally identical — just different vendors selling the same product
✅ Correct Answer: A

Linkerd is the "just works" service mesh. Install, annotate namespaces, and every pod gets mTLS. No YAML configuration, no complex CRDs. The Rust proxy is memory-safe and has zero CVEs (as of writing).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Istio vs Linkerd — Architecture Comparison
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ISTIO                            LINKERD                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Proxy: Envoy (C++)       │    │ Proxy: linkerd2-proxy     │   │
  │  │ Size:  ~100MB per pod    │    │        (Rust)             │   │
  │  │ CRDs:  50+               │    │ Size:  ~10MB per pod      │   │
  │  │ Config: Complex          │    │ CRDs:  5 (minimal)        │   │
  │  │ Install: helm install +  │    │ Config: Auto-detect       │   │
  │  │         extensive config │    │ Install: linkerd install  │   │
  │  │                          │    │          && linkerd inject │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────┘   │
  │  FOR ANIHPJ: Linkerd = simpler, faster, less attack surface     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4.4 Pod-to-Pod Encryption — mTLS & Service MeshQ103-Q108

Microservice Vulns Q103 of 200
Why does pod-to-pod encryption matter, and what attack does it prevent?
A) Pod-to-pod traffic is automatically encrypted by Kubernetes — no additional configuration needed
B) By default, pod-to-pod traffic is PLAINTEXT. A network sniffer on any node (or compromised pod with hostNetwork) can capture database queries, API tokens, and user data in transit. Pod-to-pod encryption (mTLS via Istio/Linkerd or WireGuard via Cilium) prevents network sniffing, man-in-the-middle attacks, and ensures traffic authenticity
C) Pod-to-pod encryption is only needed for external traffic — internal cluster traffic is isolated by the CNI
D) Encryption adds unacceptable latency — it should never be used in production
✅ Correct Answer: B

By default, Kubernetes pod networks are UNENCRYPTED. Any pod on any node can potentially sniff traffic. mTLS ensures every connection is encrypted AND authenticated — both parties verify each other's identity.

Microservice Vulns Q104 of 200
How does Cilium provide transparent pod-to-pod encryption using WireGuard or IPsec?
A) Cilium requires application code changes to enable encryption — it's not transparent
B) Cilium uses TLS certificates — each pod must have a certificate to communicate
C) Cilium provides TRANSPARENT encryption at the kernel level — no application changes, no sidecars. Enable with cilium config set enable-wireguard true or enable-ipsec true. ✅ Correct Answer: C

Microservice Vulns Q105 of 200
How does Istio service mesh provide automatic mTLS between services?
A) Istio requires developers to manually add TLS code to each microservice
B) Istio uses a central proxy that all traffic routes through — single point of failure
C) Istio modifies the application container to inject TLS — it's an application-level solution

Microservice Vulns Q106 of 200
How does Linkerd differ from Istio for mTLS — why is it considered "lightweight"?
A) Linkerd uses a RUST-based micro-proxy (not Envoy) that's purpose-built for K8s. No CRDs for configuration, no complex control plane — just TLS identity + mTLS + metrics. ~10MB per proxy vs Envoy's ~100MB. Simpler to operate, fewer moving parts, less attack surface

Microservice Vulns Q107 of 200
What is the difference between Istio's mTLS modes — PERMISSIVE vs STRICT?
A) PERMISSIVE blocks all plaintext; STRICT allows both plaintext and TLS
B) PERMISSIVE: Accepts BOTH plaintext AND mTLS connections — used during migration when not all services have sidecars yet. STRICT: REJECTS plaintext — ONLY mTLS connections allowed. Migration path: Start PERMISSIVE → deploy sidecars everywhere → switch to STRICT. STRICT ensures no traffic can be intercepted
C) PERMISSIVE and STRICT are identical — the names are historical
D) STRICT blocks all external traffic; PERMISSIVE allows it — these are firewall modes, not mTLS
✅ Correct Answer: B

The migration path is critical. You can't go directly to STRICT — it would break all non-mesh services. PERMISSIVE → STRICT is the zero-downtime path to full encryption.

Microservice Vulns Q108 of 200
How do you verify that mTLS is actually working between pods?
A) Check pod logs — applications log whether they received encrypted connections
B) Run tcpdump on the host — network packets show encryption headers
C) Istio: istioctl authn tls-check <pod> shows mTLS status for each connection. Linkerd: linkerd edges -n <ns> shows encrypted/unencrypted connections. Cilium: cilium encrypt status shows WireGuard/IPsec status. Always verify — don't assume encryption is working because you enabled it
D) mTLS verification requires specialized network hardware — software tools cannot detect it
✅ Correct Answer: C

Trust but verify. Enabling mTLS doesn't guarantee it's working — misconfiguration, missing sidecars, or CNI incompatibility can silently leave traffic unencrypted. Always run the verification commands.

4.5 Admission Controllers, OPA & KyvernoQ109-Q120

Microservice Vulns Q109 of 200
What are Kubernetes Admission Controllers and where do they sit in the API request flow?
A) Admission controllers are external firewalls that sit in front of the API server
B) Admission controllers are PLUGINS that intercept API requests AFTER authentication and authorization but BEFORE the object is persisted to etcd. They can MUTATE (modify) or VALIDATE (accept/reject) requests. They are the last gatekeeper before an object is created — configured via --enable-admission-plugins on the API server
C) Admission controllers are a deprecated feature — they've been replaced by OPA/Gatekeeper
D) Admission controllers run inside kubelet and filter pod creation at the node level
✅ Correct Answer: B

Admission controllers are the LAST line of defense before etcd. AuthN proves identity, AuthZ checks permissions, but Admission can enforce organizational policies — like "all pods must have resource limits" or "no latest tag images."

Microservice Vulns Q110 of 200
What is the difference between Mutating and Validating Admission Controllers?
A) Mutating runs FIRST and can MODIFY the request (add fields, inject sidecars, set defaults). Validating runs SECOND and can only ACCEPT or REJECT (cannot modify). Both can be external webhooks. MutatingUseful for: injecting default securityContext, adding sidecars. ValidatingUseful for: blocking :latest tag, enforcing label requirements
B) Mutating controllers run last and override Validating decisions
C) There is no difference — both do the same thing
D) Mutating controllers are deprecated — only Validating controllers should be used
✅ Correct Answer: A

The order matters: Mutating FIRST, Validating SECOND. This means a Mutating webhook can fix a pod (add securityContext) BEFORE the Validating webhook checks it (verifies securityContext is present).

Microservice Vulns Q111 of 200
Which built-in admission controllers are most important for security, and what does each do?
A) All admission controllers are security-focused — there are no distinctions
B) Only NamespaceLifecycle matters — other controllers are for performance
C) Built-in controllers are legacy — modern clusters use only external webhooks
D) NamespaceLifecycle (prevents creation in terminating namespaces), LimitRanger (enforces default resource limits), AlwaysPullImages (forces fresh pulls — no cached images), NodeRestriction (limits kubelet cross-node access), PodSecurity (enforces PSA). These are ENABLED by default and provide baseline security without external tools
✅ Correct Answer: D

These built-in controllers are your first line of defense. They're compiled into the API server — no external dependencies, no webhook latency, always available. Always verify they're enabled with kube-apiserver -h | grep enable-admission-plugins.

Microservice Vulns Q112 of 200
How does a ValidatingAdmissionWebhook work, and what's a common use case?
A) It modifies pods before they're created — it's the same as a MutatingWebhook
B) It runs in kubelet and inspects containers after they start
C) It's an HTTPS webhook called by the API server AFTER mutation. The webhook receives an AdmissionReview with the object, evaluates it against custom logic, and returns ALLOWED or DENIED. Common use: block images with the :latest tag, enforce required labels, or reject pods without resource limits
D) ValidatingWebhooks are deprecated — use Kyverno or OPA instead
✅ Correct Answer: C

ValidatingWebhooks are the "policy enforcement" mechanism. The API server calls your webhook, waits for a response (with timeout), and obeys the decision. FailurePolicy determines what happens if the webhook is unreachable.

Microservice Vulns Q113 of 200
What does a MutatingAdmissionWebhook do that a validating webhook cannot?
A) It MODIFIES the object before persistence — returning JSON patches that add, replace, or remove fields. Common uses: inject Istio sidecar containers, add default securityContext to pods without one, inject volume mounts for Secrets, or add tolerations. Mutating runs BEFORE validating, so the fixed object is then validated
B) Mutating and validating webhooks are functionally identical — they both accept/reject
C) Mutating webhooks run asynchronously — they don't block the API request
D) Mutating webhooks can only add annotations — they cannot modify spec fields
✅ Correct Answer: A

Mutating webhooks are the "automation" mechanism. Instead of rejecting a pod that's missing securityContext, you FIX it automatically. This is how Istio injects sidecars and how Kyverno adds default security settings.

Microservice Vulns Q114 of 200
What is failurePolicy in a webhook configuration and why is it critical?
A) failurePolicy only affects logging — it doesn't change webhook behavior
B) failurePolicy: Fail means if the webhook is DOWN, ALL matching requests are REJECTED (cluster can become unusable!). failurePolicy: Ignore means requests proceed WITHOUT the webhook's check (security gap!). For SECURITY webhooks, use Fail to be safe. For CONVENIENCE webhooks, use Ignore with caution. Always ensure webhooks are highly available
C) failurePolicy determines how many times the webhook retries before failing
D) failurePolicy: Ignore means the webhook is disabled — it's a maintenance mode
✅ Correct Answer: B

This is the most dangerous configuration decision. Fail = cluster freezes if webhook is down. Ignore = security bypass if webhook is down. Run at least 2 replicas of every security webhook, and use PodDisruptionBudgets.

Microservice Vulns Q115 of 200
What is OPA/Gatekeeper and how does it use Rego for Kubernetes policy enforcement?
A) OPA is a firewall — it controls network traffic between namespaces
B) OPA is a monitoring tool — it alerts on policy violations but cannot enforce
C) OPA uses YAML policies — it's identical to Kubernetes native PSA
D) OPA (Open Policy Agent) is a general-purpose policy engine. Gatekeeper is the Kubernetes-specific implementation. You define ConstraintTemplates (written in Rego — a declarative policy language) and instantiate them as Constraints (with parameters). Example: A ConstraintTemplate defines "no images from unapproved registries," and a Constraint specifies "my-approved-registry" as the parameter. Rego evaluates JSON/YAML input against rules
✅ Correct Answer: D

Rego is the policy language. It's powerful but has a learning curve. The ConstraintTemplate defines the LOGIC, and Constraints provide the PARAMETERS — separation of concerns for policy management.

Microservice Vulns Q116 of 200
What is Kyverno and how does it differ from OPA/Gatekeeper?
A) Kyverno is just a GUI for OPA — they're the same engine with different interfaces
B) Kyverno only generates reports — it cannot enforce policies like OPA can
C) Kyverno is a Kubernetes-NATIVE policy engine where policies are written as YAML (not Rego). It supports validate (check and reject), mutate (auto-fix), generate (auto-create resources), and cleanup operations. MUCH simpler than OPA/Gatekeeper — no new language to learn. Trade-off: less flexible than Rego for complex logic
D) Kyverno and OPA are the same product — Kyverno is just the commercial name
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno is the "easy button" for K8s policy. Policies look like Kubernetes resources (YAML), not code. For 80% of use cases (require labels, block latest, enforce securityContext), Kyverno is sufficient and much faster to implement.

Microservice Vulns Q117 of 200
Kyverno vs OPA/Gatekeeper — which should you choose for anihpj/jobpost?
A) Kyverno: YAML-native, no new language, built-in mutation (auto-fix), lower learning curve. Best for teams without dedicated policy engineers. OPA/Gatekeeper: Rego-based, extremely flexible for complex logic, larger ecosystem, steeper learning curve. Best for enterprises with complex compliance requirements. For anihpj/jobpost (startup/small team): Kyverno is the pragmatic choice
B) Always use both — they complement each other and can run simultaneously
C) Neither — PSA is sufficient for all use cases, external policy engines add complexity
D) OPA is always better because Rego is more powerful than YAML-based policies
✅ Correct Answer: A

The pragmatic answer: Kyverno for simplicity, OPA for complexity. For CKS, know the differences and trade-offs. The exam won't test Rego syntax but expects you to understand when to use which tool.

Microservice Vulns Q118 of 200
What are Kubesec and KubeLinter — how do they perform static analysis of Kubernetes manifests?
A) Both are runtime security tools that monitor running pods
B) Kubesec is a CNI plugin; KubeLinter is a storage driver
C) They are identical tools with different brand names — both do the same thing
D) Kubesec: Static analysis that scores manifests on a risk scale (0-10). Checks for: privileged containers, readOnlyRoot, capabilities, runAsNonRoot, resource limits. KubeLinter: Best-practice linter that checks YAML against a configurable set of rules. Both run BEFORE deployment (shift-left security) and can be integrated into CI/CD to BLOCK PRs with insecure configs
✅ Correct Answer: D

Shift-left security: catch misconfigurations before they reach the cluster. A CI/CD pipeline that runs Kubesec + KubeLinter + Trivy on every PR prevents insecure manifests from ever being deployed.

Microservice Vulns Q119 of 200
Write an OPA/Gatekeeper policy that requires all pods to have a specific label and restricts image registries.
A) OPA policies are written in YAML — just like Kubernetes manifests
B) A ConstraintTemplate defines the Rego logic for checking labels/images, and a Constraint specifies parameters (required label "app.kubernetes.io/name", allowed registry "my-registry.io"). The Rego rule evaluates input.review.object and returns violations if labels are missing or images come from disallowed registries. This is enforced by the validating admission webhook
C) OPA policies are applied via kubectl label on namespaces — similar to PSA
D) OPA cannot restrict image registries — that requires a separate ImagePolicyWebhook
✅ Correct Answer: B

OPA/Gatekeeper's power is in the separation: ConstraintTemplate defines "HOW to check," Constraint defines "WHAT to check for." This allows platform teams to define templates and app teams to create constraints with their specific parameters.

Microservice Vulns Q120 of 200
How does Kyverno's mutate rule automatically add default securityContext to pods?
A) Kyverno sends alerts to developers asking them to fix their manifests manually
B) Kyverno only works on Deployments — it cannot mutate individual Pods
C) A Kyverno ClusterPolicy with a mutate rule targets pods (kind: Pod) and uses patchesJson6902 or patchStrategicMerge to add fields. Example: add runAsNonRoot: true, drop: [ALL] capabilities, and readOnlyRootFilesystem: true to every pod that doesn't explicitly set them. This happens transparently during admission — developers don't need to know about security settings
D) Kyverno mutate is deprecated — use OPA's mutating webhook instead
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno's killer feature: automatic security remediation. Developers push a pod without securityContext — Kyverno FIXES it, and the API server sees the hardened pod. Zero friction. This is how you scale security across large organizations.

🔧 Part 2: 100 Security Issues

Real-world Kubernetes security failure scenarios — each with symptom, 15 categorized causes (5 most likely + 5 less likely + 5 new cluster), diagnostic commands, solutions, and personal security advice. Patterned after the 5+5+5 cause-card format.

100Issues
5Categories
15Causes per Issue

💾 Category 1: Storage Troubleshooting (10 Issues)ST1-ST10

🟢 Storage Issues — PVC, PV, StorageClass, and Mount Failures

Storage is often the FIRST thing to break because it involves multiple components: PVC → StorageClass → Provisioner → PV → Mount. When any link in this chain fails, your pod can't start or your data is at risk.

📌 ST1–ST3: PVC Binding Failures

ST1
💾 CATEGORY 1: STORAGE — Issue ST1
PVC Stuck in Pending State — No PV Bound

🔍 Symptom: kubectl get pvc shows STATUS: Pending indefinitely. kubectl describe pvc shows "no persistent volumes available for this claim." The anihpj-db PostgreSQL pod can't start because its PVC won't bind.

  kubectl get pvc
  NAME         STATUS    VOLUME   CAPACITY   STORAGECLASS   AGE
  anihpj-pvc   Pending                      fast-ssd       5m

  kubectl describe pvc anihpj-pvc
  Events:
    Warning  VolumeBinding  no persistent volumes available for this claim
    Warning  VolumeBinding  storageclass.storage.k8s.io "fast-ssd" not found
🔴5 Most Likely Causes
  1. StorageClass does not exist: The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created. kubectl get sc — if "fast-ssd" is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
  2. No matching PV available: In static provisioning, no PV matches the PVC's storage size, access mode, or selector. kubectl get pv — check capacity and accessModes.
  3. StorageClass provisioner not installed: The CSI driver (e.g., ebs.csi.aws.com) is not deployed. kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep csi — provisioner pods must be Running.
  4. Access mode mismatch: PVC requests ReadWriteMany but only ReadWriteOnce PVs exist. Check kubectl get pv -o yaml | grep -A2 accessModes.
  5. StorageClass has volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer and no pod uses the PVC yet. The PV is only created when a pod actually references the PVC.
🟡5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CSI driver pod CrashLoopBackOff — provisioner can't create volumes
  2. Cloud provider API rate limiting — too many PVCs created simultaneously
  3. PVC selector labels don't match any PV labels
  4. Node where CSI driver runs has taint without toleration
  5. storageClassName: "" (empty string, not null!) — requests a PV with NO StorageClass, but all PVs have one
🟣5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No default StorageClass set: kubectl get sc — look for "(default)". Without it, PVCs without storageClassName won't bind. Set: kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}'
  2. CSI driver not yet installed — common in fresh kubeadm clusters
  3. Cloud credentials not configured for CSI driver to provision volumes
  4. RBAC for CSI driver service account missing
  5. kubelet missing --cloud-provider flag for legacy in-tree provisioners
🔍 What to Look At: kubectl get sc,pv,pvc --all-namespaces — the full storage stack in one view. kubectl describe pvc <name> — the Events section tells you exactly why binding failed.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl get sc — verify the StorageClass exists
2. kubectl describe sc <name> — check provisioner and parameters
3. For static: kubectl get pv — create a PV matching the PVC's size/accessMode
4. For dynamic: install CSI driver: kubectl apply -f <csi-driver-url>
5. Set default SC: kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass...is-default-class":"true"}}}'

💡 Personal Advice: The #1 PVC issue on the CKA exam is a missing or misnamed StorageClass. The exam environment may have a StorageClass named "standard" but your PVC says "fast-ssd". Always run kubectl get sc FIRST before writing any PVC YAML. Also: storageClassName: "" (empty string) is NOT the same as omitting storageClassName — empty string requests a PV with NO StorageClass.
